- **[Re-Search: Dissecting LLM Ideation Through a Two-Player Questioning Game](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14487)**  `arXiv:2611.14487`  
  _Muhan Gao, Hangxiao Zhu, Xinqi Su, Yu Zhang, Daniel Khashabi, Kuan-Hao Huang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Research ideation is iterative: researchers refine their ideas step by step based on feedback. Yet existing evaluations obscure this search process, making it difficult to understand how LLMs explore the space of research ideas and what limits their progress. We introduce Re-Search, which adapts the format of Twenty Questions to research ideation: given only the research problem of a published paper, a model must rediscover the paper's core idea by asking yes-or-no questions. Built from 200 ICML 2026 papers, Re-Search tracks how a model's idea evolves as it asks questions and receives binary oracle feedback, isolating ideation from execution. Across six recent LLMs, nearly all progress happens within the first 30 of 100 questions, which largely decide how the game ends; after that, most models plateau. Through controlled interventions on the candidate questions and the hypothesis space, we find that the bottleneck is generation, not judgment: models recognize a good idea or a good question when one is offered, but often cannot produce it on their own. We further analyze the ideation traces and find that models fail by either over-exploiting one direction or over-exploring too many. These findings point to concrete design principles for autoresearch systems: since models can judge candidates they cannot produce, enriching the search with externally sourced ideas and questions may be more effective than improving any single model's generation ability.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating LLM-Generated World Models for Neuro-Symbolic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14419)**  `arXiv:2611.14419`  
  _Souhaila Djaffal, Amna Jamal, Gagana Madhugiri Devaraja, Anidipta Pal, Jobst Heitzig_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Formal world models can support planning, verification, and safety reasoning, but constructing them from open-ended natural-language situations is difficult, particularly in multi-agent settings where several formalizations may be plausible. We study whether five LLMs can generate such world models as partially observable stochastic games and whether mechanical validity is sufficient to establish that the resulting model faithfully represents the intended decision problem. We introduce a benchmark of 44 scenarios spanning game, situational, and cultural settings and evaluate generated world models along three dimensions: mechanical validity, reference-relative structural agreement, and semantic criteria. Across three empirical tracks, we analyze 410 first-pass generations. In the reference-backed game track, 134/135 generated world models pass a strict mechanical audit, yet mean permutation-invariant structural F1 is only 0.366, with just 28/134 mechanically valid models exactly matching the authored reference. In the cultural track, explicitly naming the country changes the generated structural representation for most LLMs while producing only small changes in automated semantic scores. These results show that executability is a necessary but insufficient criterion for LLM-generated world models: models that are mechanically valid can still differ substantially in the structure used by downstream neuro-symbolic reasoning and planning.
  </details>

- **[Precision on Demand: Escalating Weight Bits at Uncertain Decoding Steps in a Small Local Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14418)**  `arXiv:2611.14418`  
  _Mohammed Nayeem Khan, Logesh L, Aliyas Ahmad Misri_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Local decoding usually fixes one weight precision for the whole generation. We study a different allocation: store 8-bit weights, decode with their top 4 bits, and redo a step at 8 bits only when the low-precision output distribution looks uncertain (entropy above a threshold or a small top-two margin). Quantization is simulated, the model is Qwen3-0.6B, and the benchmark is GSM8K. Cost is counted in weight bytes read per token. On 36724 replayed steps the entropy of the low pass ranks argmax flips with AUC 0.93. At a 15% escalation budget it catches 78% of flips and the margin gate 94%, against the 15% expected for a random gate of equal rate (a computed expectation, not a run). In 150 live problems all-high scores 64.0% and all-low 55.3% (McNemar p=0.053). An entropy gate with a 16% escalation rate scores 62.7% at 0.87 of the all-high weight bytes. Its paired difference is -1.3 (-8.0 to +5.3) points to all-high and +7.3 (+0.0 to +14.7) points to all-low (p=0.080), so the live data do not separate the gate from either end. Verified speculation reaches zero KL at similar bytes in the same cost model. A live random control, a thinking-mode phase rule, a second model and live KL were not run.
  </details>

- **[An Energy-Based Perspective on Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14383)**  `arXiv:2611.14383`  
  _Bo Dai, Yilun Du_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Energy-based models (EBMs) define a distribution through a scalar energy, $p_\theta(x)\propto\exp(-E_\theta(x))$, and are usually presented as one model family among many. This tutorial has a broader goal: beyond introducing EBMs, it uses them as a single lens that connects the different aspects of machine learning. Any EBM is specified by three choices, the energy, which determines what the model represents; the inference procedure, which samples from or optimizes the energy; and the learning method, which fits the energy to data. We first develop these components, including sampling, variational inference, and learning by maximum likelihood, noise contrastive estimation, pseudolikelihood, score matching, and adversarial training, and show that the learning methods share one contrastive structure. We then show that discriminative models, generative models such as GANs, normalizing flows, diffusion and autoregressive models, representation learning methods such as contrastive learning, word2vec, BERT, denoising auto-encoders, and diffusion representations, reinforcement learning with EBM policies and world models, inverse reinforcement learning, and planning, as well as inference-time computation with verifiers, are all EBMs that differ in these three choices, mostly in how they handle the partition function and sampling. This view explains existing methods, organizes them into a common framework, and suggests new algorithms by replacing one component while keeping the others, with composition of energies as a principled way to combine learned models at inference time.
  </details>

- **[Cognitive Mapping of Learning by Thinking Mechanisms in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14382)**  `arXiv:2611.14382`  
  _Dong Han, Jin Zhang, Kunyue Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) increasingly exhibit capabilities that resemble human Learning by Thinking (LbT), the acquisition of new insight through internal processes such as explanation and reasoning, without new external input. A systematic account of the cognitive mechanisms behind these capabilities, and of their limits, remains underdeveloped. This paper proposes a Cognitive LbT Framework that maps four mechanisms (Reflective Reasoning, Synthetic Memory Formation, Internal Simulation, and Conceptual Self-Organization) onto computational processes in LLMs. Drawing on representative methods, including chain-of-thought prompting, self-consistency, self-reflection, Tree-of-Thoughts search, Self-Instruct, and self-taught reasoning, the analysis argues that selected methods can functionally approximate aspects of these mechanisms and can improve performance on some tasks without additional labeled examples, although this does not always constitute durable learning in model parameters. For example, Self-Instruct generated approximately 52,000 instructions from 175 seed tasks and reported a 33% absolute improvement on Super-NaturalInstructions (Y. Wang et al., 2023). The same mechanisms also produce characteristic failures, including hallucination diffusion, model collapse, concept drift, and high computational cost. These failures are not isolated technical defects but follow from four structural asymmetries between LLMs and human cognition: the identity of generator and monitor, closed learning loops without external grounding, symbolic rather than sensorimotor simulation, and the cost of inference-time computation. The framework yields testable propositions about when LbT in LLMs should succeed or fail and offers a structured basis for designing more reliable autonomous learning systems.
  </details>

- **[CLADE: Category-Level Answering from Deficient Evidence in Retrieval-Augmented Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14355)**  `arXiv:2611.14355`  
  _S M Rayeed, Charles V. Stewart, Yao Ma_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-augmented systems often retrieve passages that are accurate and relevant but state the answer at a coarser level of granularity than the question requires. For example, a question may ask which specific disease a drug treats (familial hyperlipidemia), while the retrieved passage mentions the broader disease family (hyperlipidemia). Prior work on context sufficiency treats such coarse passages simply as insufficient. We argue that they form a distinct case, which we call resolvable insufficiency: text alone cannot settle the fine-grained answer, but the broader category it mentions maps to a short list of candidate answers in a domain ontology. When language models read these coarse, family-only passages, they exhibit a phenomenon we call Clade (Category-Level Answering from Deficient Evidence): they answer instead of abstaining or searching further. On biomedical questions from PrimeKG, family-only passages reduce abstention by 13.8 points compared to uninformative passages, and the resulting guesses are wrong about half the time. A counterfactual test confirms the cause: replacing an exact disease name with its broader family name lowers abstention by 17.4 points compared to deleting the sentence. Simple fixes like confidence filtering, scaling to a larger model, and explicitly instructing the model to abstain cannot resolve Clade, and this replicates on ecological species interactions in GloBI. Finally, we test whether an ontology can turn coarse passages into exact answers. Given the correct family, an ontology list includes the true answer in 98.5% of cases, but string matching finds that family on the page for only 19.8%. Letting the model choose the family raises this to 30.0% and end-to-end recovery from 10.0% to 26.8%. Overall, our results show when language models should stop guessing and consult an ontology, and highlight which steps of that process remain unsolved.
  </details>

- **[When Should Agents Remember? Falsification-Gated Self-Evolution for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14326)**  `arXiv:2611.14326`  
  _Runxuan Tang, Haoyu Gao, Yuyan Ding, Junyi Yao, Zihao Zheng, Zecheng Sheng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents increasingly operate in long-horizon, verifier-rich environments, but current self-improvement mechanisms often write episode-derived lessons directly into memory, making local success an unreliable signal for reusable and safe knowledge. This paper addresses the core question of when an experience-derived behavioral update should be admitted into persistent agent state. We propose Falsification-Gated Self-Evolution (FGSE), a verifier-grounded framework that represents each candidate lesson as a structured hypothesis with an explicit precondition, behavior change, expected effect, and verifier. FGSE applies the hypothesis only in a temporary state, tests it on target-transfer, falsification, and archived-regression probes, and then commits, refines, or rejects it according to measured gain and risk. Across web, app, tool-use, and long-term memory benchmarks, FGSE achieves strong task performance, including 39.8 WebArena success rate, 78.8 AppWorld average completion, and 0.724/0.487 Pass$^1$ on $\tau$-Retail/$\tau$-Airline, while reducing falsification failures to 5.0%, regression damage to 1.3%, and harmful committed updates to 3.6%. These results suggest that testing memory updates as hypotheses supports more reliable long-term adaptation.
  </details>

- **[Multimodal Abstention That Preserves Acquired Correct Answers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14311)**  `arXiv:2611.14311`  
  _Jinyuan Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adding abstention to a task-adapted multimodal model can discard correct answers, including capabilities acquired during adaptation. We formulate abstention repair as maximizing correct abstention on unanswerable questions under a budget on newly lost correct answers. We propose ReFrain (Regression-Free Abstention), which combines a linear probe on first-token logits, threshold calibration on held-out image clusters, and a deterministic veto that replaces rejected outputs with the displayed "none of the above" option while preserving all other responses. "Regression-free" denotes zero loss on the calibration set at the zero-budget target. All abstention methods in our main comparison start from the same answering-SFT checkpoint; we separately track lost correct answers, repaired errors, and retained SFT gains. On Qwen2.5-VL-3B, ReFrain at zero budget retains all 57 SFT-gained answers with no losses and improves correct abstention by 7.30 percentage points. At a 0.5% calibration budget, it reaches 74.05% correct abstention with 8 losses and retains 55 gains; CLIP-UP EmbLoRA reaches 64.65% with 62 losses, 41 repairs, and 40 retained gains. The same probe at its default threshold loses 298 correct answers. With models and thresholds frozen, confirmation on 228 independent question clusters yields 66.05% correct abstention and no losses at the 0.5% point, exceeding EmbLoRA by 32.72 percentage points. Ordinary refusal SFT achieves higher correct abstention at greater regression cost. Code and reproduction materials:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[LM-SimBench: A Unified Framework, Dataset, and Benchmark for Large Language Model Training Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14285)**  `arXiv:2611.14285`  
  _Yu Dong, Hong Li, Qiang Sun, Xu Zhou, Jinbiao Huo, Qixiu Cheng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating candidate parallelization strategies through distributed training runs makes strategy selection prohibitively expensive at scale. Performance simulation offers a lower cost alternative, yet controlled comparisons among existing simulators remain difficult because they differ in the training stages they model and the workloads on which they are evaluated. We introduce LM-SimBench, a unified framework, profiling dataset, and benchmark for evaluating LLM training simulators. The framework decomposes simulation into functional stages from model compilation to schedule level aggregation, connecting execution representations and cost estimators to measurable evaluation targets. Following this framework, we profile 23 model settings from 10 dense and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLM families on an Ascend Neural Processing Unit (NPU) cluster. The resulting dataset contains 348 feasible configurations that jointly vary five parallelism dimensions and activation recomputation, with six views spanning configuration, rank, operator, event, and kernel granularity. Evaluations of representative training simulators demonstrate how LM-SimBench supports comparisons of prediction accuracy, assessment of configuration selection, and diagnosis of prediction errors. Matched GPU-NPU experiments validate the cross-platform applicability of the collection and evaluation protocol and demonstrate NPU-guided GPU configuration selection.
  </details>

- **[A Cross-Scenario Survey and Analysis of Self-Evolving Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14256)**  `arXiv:2611.14256`  
  _Jingjing Jiang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work shows that multimodal agents increasingly self-improve without recurring human annotation. However, each self-improvement mechanism is designed and evaluated within a single scenario, leaving its preconditions unverified under cross-scenario transfer. This survey investigates the structural source of asymmetric mechanism transfer across four scenarios: multimodal reasoning, multimodal generation, GUI and Web navigation, and embodied manipulation. We first formalize the self-evolving multimodal agent by four criteria and a differentia of self-reference, and adopt the three-stage self-evolving loop of experience generation, feedback acquisition, and update consolidation as our analytical framework to position the scenarios along ceilings on rollout count and feedback fidelity. Comparing self-evolving with fixed loops within each scenario separates the limitations that the scenario imposes from those inherent in the self-evolving design. We then recast each mechanism as a trade-off that raises a scarce factor by presupposing a task-structural resource, such as output equivalence or checkable outcomes, and judge it portable, conditional, blocked, or redundant across scenarios. We attribute the asymmetry to divergent combinations of scarce factors and resources across opposite transfer directions, and identify the matched-budget measurements that could overturn these still untested verdicts.
  </details>

- **[Joint Quantization and Dynamic Partitioning for Context-Aware Edge Vision Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14196)**  `arXiv:2611.14196`  
  _Zhu Jiaqi, Jain Prakriti, Katare Dewant, Kumar Saurabh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deploying vision transformer models across connected and automated vehicles (CAVs) requires joint management of computation and Vehicle-to-Vehicle (V2V) communication. Static split-inference methods use fixed partition points and cannot adapt to time-varying link bandwidth, processing load, and concurrent multi-stream traffic. This paper proposes an edge orchestration framework that jointly selects model partition points, per-layer quantization bitwidths, and execution placement across heterogeneous vehicular compute nodes. The joint partitioning and mixed-precision allocation problem is formulated as a Mixed-Integer Linear Program (MILP) that minimizes compute and communication energy subject to end-to-end latency and task-quality constraints. The framework is evaluated on Jetson Orin Nano (8~GB), Jetson Orin NX (16~GB), and Jetson AGX Orin (32~GB) nodes using OPV2V-driven multi-stream workloads with $K \in \{2,4,6,8\}$. Across the evaluated configurations, the proposed formulation reduces V2V communication energy by $33.3$--$36.0\%$ relative to a Uniform INT8 split. At $K=8$, end-to-end task completion latency is reduced by $34.0\%$ on the Orin Nano and $33.1\%$ on the Orin NX relative to the same baseline, while limiting the reduction in 3D detection accuracy to $3.1$--$4.1$ percentage points in $\mathrm{mAP}_{50}$ relative to FP16 execution.
  </details>

- **[An Augmented Lagrangian Approach to Lifted Bellman Linear Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14183)**  `arXiv:2611.14183`  
  _Jongchan Park, Hyukjun Yang, Narim Jeong, Donghwan Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Temporal-difference critics regress onto a bootstrapped target and usually need a target network. Lifted Bellman Linear Programming (LBLP) instead expresses the in-sample optimal values of offline RL as the solution to a linear program over the dataset. Its neural solver ALBUM relaxes the constraints into hinge penalties with fixed coefficients, and its guarantees are conditions on coefficients chosen in advance. We solve the same program with the method of multipliers: the coefficients become multipliers learned by dual ascent, with one penalty parameter $\rho$. In the convex case the fixed points are the KKT pairs of LBLP, and at every stationary point the applied constraint weights balance the objective exactly. The critic can serve a Gaussian actor, a flow actor, and best-of-N sampling, and for the Gaussian actor we propose an advantage-normalized DDPG+BC objective. With a single $Q$ and $V$ and no target network, our AL-LBLP with best-of-N reaches 60.1\% average success on the 50 state-based OGBench tasks. On long-horizon tasks, it reaches 81.5% on humanoidmaze-giant and 79.8% on antmaze-giant. It does not collapse up to batch size 8192.
  </details>

- **[Cross-Border Fulfillment Center Selection via Doubly Robust Estimation and Linear Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14174)**  `arXiv:2611.14174`  
  _Kriti Mahajan, Patrick Bloebaum_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce the cross-border (XB) fulfillment center (FC) Selection Model: a multi-stage optimization framework combining forecasting, doubly robust estimation, and linear programming to select optimal source FCs. For each source-destination shipping arc, the objective is to choose a subset of source-country FCs that minimize delivery time and cost while maximizing profit and shipped units under a fixed domestic network. We evaluate the model on three production-scale international shipping corridors for a large e-commerce company using 12 months of shipment data, employing both aggregate offline replay and shipment-level counterfactual nearest-neighbor matching. Across all shipping corridors, the XB FC Selection Model achieves 8.5-51.1% reductions in delivery time and 4.4-48.3% reductions in logistics cost relative to human expert-defined FC selection policies, with up to 6.5 percentage-point improvements in 5/5 target compliance (5 days, $5 USD). Pareto analysis shows the model consistently dominates single-objective ranking strategies and unconstrained LP on the efficiency-reallocation frontier, with all results statistically significant.
  </details>

- **[BARS: Budget-Aware Sequential Sampling with Refusal for Majority Identification under Unknown Distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14171)**  `arXiv:2611.14171`  
  _Shenxi Liu, Yiwei Li, Mingyang Zhao, Yuhang Tian, Kan Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Repeated sampling with majority selection is an effective means of enforcing self-consistency in large language models. Recently, a variant has also been introduced that terminates early to save costs when the output distribution is favorable. But it still cannot yield a correct confidence estimate from the answer distribution. For this issue, we formalize this as majority identification under an unknown distribution with a budget and a target error rate, and propose BARS, a sequential rule certifying the top-two gap and returning an early stop, a budgeted majority vote, or a refusal when feasibility cannot be established. A refusal is a statement about the rule and its budget, not a claim that a question is unanswerable, and it tells practitioners when to widen the budget or switch models. A certified mode carries proven bounds on the joint answering error and on refusing an instance the budget could handle, while a practical mode, used in our experiments, replaces them with empirical calibration. On synthetic instances drawn from known distributions over thousands of seeds, the difficulty-driven behavior is recovered and the price of the certified mode isthis http URLGSM8K the practical mode draws 22.3\% fewer samples than fixed-8 sampling over the same pool, refused questions included, at the cost of declining 18.8\% of the questions. On MATH, it greatly improves the accuracy of its output answers into 82.5\% at the cost of honestly refusing to answer half of the difficult questions that the model itself is unable to solve.
  </details>

- **[Hx2Dx: From Anamnesis to Diagnosis - A Multimodal Arena for Conversational Clinical Reasoning in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14122)**  `arXiv:2611.14122`  
  _Federico Ravenda, Mirko Cesarini, Daniele Montagnani, Volodymyr Karpenko, Antonietta Mira, Andrea Raballo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) achieve expert-level diagnostic accuracy in static evaluations, where the model receives a curated vignette or case report that already condenses the diagnostically relevant findings. Such evaluations measure inference over already selected evidence; the evaluations do not account for the inquiry used to obtain it. In clinical practice, however, diagnosis is an iterative process of inquiry. Clinicians take the patient's history, or anamnesis (Hx), through targeted questioning, examine the patient, and request and interpret tests, progressively narrowing the differential until they reach a final diagnosis (Dx). We introduce Hx2Dx ARENA, an interactive benchmark of 1'000 clinical cases from the New England Journal of Medicine (NEJM). Each case is a simulated encounter that proceeds from initial presentation through history taking and physical examination to diagnostic workup. We evaluate open- and closed-source LLMs under multiple configurations. Performance varies considerably across models in the interactive setting, and every model loses diagnostic accuracy relative to the static setting, where the full case is presented upfront. The ability to ask the right questions accounts for much of both the gap between models and the loss of accuracy: diagnosis improves as questions become more targeted and build a more complete clinical picture. LLMs thus reason well once the relevant data are available but remain limited in hypothesis-driven information gathering, in knowing what to ask in order to know. Closing this gap is a prerequisite for their safe integration into real-world diagnostic workflows.
  </details>

- **[Personalized Memory Rubrics: Learning What to Remember per User via Rubric-Guided Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14017)**  `arXiv:2611.14017`  
  _Yupeng Han, Kai Zhang, Shuochen Liu, Ze Liu, Xianquan Wang, Zhihong Pan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon personalized agents maintain an evolving memory by continuously deciding what to add, modify, or discard. However, learning an optimal update policy remains challenging. Conventional training relies solely on terminal question-answering accuracy, yielding a coarse feedback signal that treats all intermediate updates identically. Furthermore, update utility is inherently user-specific, as identical memory actions may yield opposite effects across different user personas. Existing methods fail to address this heterogeneity, relying on globally shared evaluation standards with at most a single scalar intermediate reward. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{Personalized Memory Rubrics} (\textbf{PMR}), a framework that translates terminal feedback into fine-grained, per-step, and per-user credit assignment. PMR decomposes evaluation into a universal layer capturing cross-persona failure patterns and a per-user adaptive layer modeling individual traits as a specialized delta over the universal baseline. Through \textbf{Rubric-Guided Credit Assignment}, PMR redistributes credit at the token level, selectively amplifying validated decisions on successful trajectories while falling back to standard policy optimization on failed ones to avoid unreliable attribution. The rubric and memory policy \textbf{co-evolve} in a closed loop with anti-circularity guarantees. Crucially, PMR incurs zero inference overhead, operating without any rubric or external judge during deployment. Extensive experiments across four benchmarks demonstrate consistent gains over strong memory-augmented baselines.
  </details>

- **[TreeAgent: Structure-Aware Memory Agent for Processing Long Hierarchical Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13946)**  `arXiv:2611.13946`  
  _Wei Gu, Weiwei Qi, Jiajun Tang, Tianhang Zheng, Bo Chen, Zhibo Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Practical Web applications increasingly require agents to interpret long conversations or documents. For inputs exceeding the context windows of large language models (LLMs), memory agents process segments sequentially and retain past information in bounded memory. However, they typically divide inputs into flat token segments without preserving structure or hierarchy, causing three limitations for structured data such as API responses or HTML documents. First, fixed-length segmentation can split fields of the same record, breaking their relationships. Second, segments lack hierarchical information, obscuring their structural context and positions. Third, successive memory updates may discard details of earlier sibling records needed for reasoning on later segments. To address these limitations, we propose TreeAgent, a structure-aware agent that uses the input hierarchy to guide both segment-wise context processing and access to past information. Specifically, TreeAgent constructs segments from complete subtrees and groups of adjacent sibling subtrees to preserve local structural relationships. We provide theoretical guarantees on segment counts to guide token budget selection toward matching the segment count of fixed-length segmentation. We further attach structural paths to the segments for the agent to identify their ancestors and hierarchical positions. We also introduce residual memory links from the segments of low-level children to the segments of high-level parents, enabling direct information flows beyond successive memory updates. We evaluate TreeAgent with four representative memory agents on eight retrieval and aggregation tasks. The inputs are derived from real-world application data in JSON, HTML, YAML, and JavaScript and range from 8K to 1M tokens. TreeAgent improves the average score across the four datasets for all four agents, with 3.61% performance gain on average.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Proxy Metrics: MLLMs as Human Surrogates for Explainable AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13926)**  `arXiv:2611.13926`  
  _Eoin M. Kenny, Salim I. Amoukou, Tom Bewley_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Explainable AI (XAI) has expanded into a broad and impactful research area, but the fundamental question of how best to evaluate explanation techniques has proven frustratingly elusive. Methodologies typically revolve around either proxy metrics which tell us little about the utility of an explanation, or human evaluation which---while generally considered the gold standard---is expensive, difficult to scale, and challenging to reproduce. In this paper, we investigate if Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) can act as surrogate human participants to automate the evaluation of XAI. To do this, we first propose a ``purpose-grounded'' evaluation framework with metrics applicable to both MLLMs and human users alike. Then, we offer some conceptual insights as to why and when we can trust these models to automate this process. Lastly, we conduct extensive empirical testing across five purpose-grounded areas such as model improvement and fourteen popular MLLMs. Results indicate that all models trend positively, but OpenAI and Qwen models generally show higher alignment compared to other variants, which would be the recommended choices in practice. We expect our insights not to replace user testing, but rather to help XAI researchers complement their current evaluation methodologies and move the field away from heavy reliance on proxy metrics for measuring explanation utility.
  </details>

- **[Ceci n'est pas une pipe -- Toward a Symbolic Solver for the Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus Using Evolutionary Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13831)**  `arXiv:2611.13831`  
  _Luca Favole, Giorgio Mongardi, Giovanni Squillero, Alberto Tonda_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus (ARC) is a benchmark designed to assess the capability of Artificial Intelligence (AI) systems to generalize starting from a few samples, a task humans excel at. The ARC-AGI contests held since 2020 have been the focus of intense competition, with participants proposing approaches ranging from traditional symbolic AI to Transformer architectures. Despite a long history of successes, evolutionary algorithms (EAs) were notably absent from the approaches appearing in the main contest. As score-and-search program synthesis approaches, EAs suffer from issues related to choosing the proper representation in the Domain-Specific Language applied to the problem (for example, pixel-level or object-level), and distinguishing between two candidate solutions producing the same outputs. In this work, we present the first fully symbolic, evolutionary-based program synthesis approach submitted to the ARC-AGI-2 main contest. The main contributions are: evolving the grid parser and the output projector together with the program that transforms the grid, so that a task-specific language emerges during program synthesis; grounding the symbols the parser can extract in core knowledge theory from cognitive psychology; and an object-aware fitness that grades partially correct outputs beyond pixel-level discrepancies.
  </details>

- **[Integrating Action Prefix Prediction and Alignment Across Modalities for Multimodal Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13782)**  `arXiv:2611.13782`  
  _Sijie Xu, Francisco Cruz_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning representations from multimodal observations is difficult in reinforcement learning with limited interaction data. Multimodal Joint Predictive Representation combines multimodal latent prediction with contrastive learning across modalities, but its recursive rollout depends on earlier predictions and its pairwise alignment focuses on correspondence between modalities. We propose Improved Predictive Representation, which combines prediction from action prefixes with alignment at the observed anchor based on Contrastive Multimodal learning. At each horizon, it predicts future representations directly from the observed anchor and the corresponding sequence of actions. We evaluate two crop configurations: IPR-S with a minimum retained crop area of 10%, and IPR-H with 50%. Across five DeepMind Control Suite tasks, IPR-H achieves an average return across tasks that is 10.0% higher than the baseline with visual and depth images and 3.4% higher with visual images and position vectors. The relative performance of the two configurations varies across tasks. In Webots, both IPR configurations achieve higher mean final returns than the baseline without LiDAR, but not when LiDAR is available.
  </details>

- **[Broad-Persistent Advising in Interactive Reinforcement Learning via Latent State Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13712)**  `arXiv:2611.13712`  
  _Zhuoran Liang, Francisco Cruz_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep Interactive Reinforcement Learning (Deep IntRL) accelerates reinforcement learning training by incorporating external advice. However, conventional Deep IntRL uses each piece of advice only in the current step and discards it after a single interaction. Persistent Advising and Broad-Persistent Advising (BPA) extend this process by storing advice in persistent memory and reusing it in similar states through state generalisation based on K-means clustering. This research proposes Encoder BPA, which employs a latent encoder to retrieve and reuse advice. In this framework, a trained encoder is used to map a state sequence into a latent representation at every time step. Advice can be retrieved through latent comparison, together with a top-(k) aggregation and a consistency condition to improve reuse stability. Experimental results show that Encoder BPA improves learning efficiency and advice utilisation at an early stage of training compared with different Deep IntRL approaches, especially in complex and partially observable environments.
  </details>

- **[AutoManifold: Agentic Design of Data Visualisation Algorithms via Manifold Embedding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13710)**  `arXiv:2611.13710`  
  _Burak Susam, Ruiyuan Kang, Tingting Mu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Manifold embedding for data visualisation is dominated by a small set of hand-designed state-of-the-art (SOTA) algorithms. Their design targets are fixed at development time, resulting in inflexible algorithmic behaviour that cannot be readily steered towards user-specified structural priorities. Hand-designing new embedding algorithms to address user priority requires highly specialised expertise and is time consuming. To address autonomous algorithm design tailored to user preference, we introduce an agentic algorithm generation pipeline AutoManifold. It composes new manifold embedding algorithms from a constrained vocabulary of affinity, cost, and optimisation primitives, supported by multi-agent large-language-model (LLM) orchestration. AutoManifold conditions every stage of its design on user-specified structural preservation preferences, and iteratively refines the algorithm configuration through an LLM-guided, metric-grounded iterative loop. We compare the generated algorithms against the three strongest and most frequently used SOTA methods (t-SNE, UMAP, and PaCMAP) on real-world datasets spanning different difficulty regimes under identical evaluation infrastructure. The results demonstrate that LLM-driven, priority-conditioned algorithm synthesis can move beyond hyperparameter tuning, producing genuinely new algorithms that outperform traditional hand-designed methods in their ability to steer towards user-specified properties without compromising the intrinsic structure in the original data. The code of AutoManifold is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Topological Dynamics Under Non-Stationary Supervision: Persistent Homology of Neural Network Weight Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13701)**  `arXiv:2611.13701`  
  _Mitra Varun_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We investigate how the persistent topology of neuron- weight point clouds evolves under stable and non-stationary supervision. An identical multilayer perceptron is trained on Fashion-MNIST with correct labels, a fixed random permutation of the training-label vector, or a fresh permutation every epoch. Five paired seeds share initialisation and minibatch order, yielding 15 models trained for 100 epochs. Raw and row-normalised incoming weight clouds are analysed with H0 and H1 Vietoris Rips persistence at 22 checkpoints. Correct-label models reach 89.39% mean true-label test accuracy; fixed-random models fit 67.94% of their assigned training targets without generalising to the original task. Correct-label models have descriptively greater time-averaged normalised fc3 H1 persistence than either random-label condition, but the configured exact comparisons do not reach conventional significance after Holm correction. Early condition classification achieves 60.0% mean balanced accuracy using topology, 66.7% using conventional diagnostics, and 60.0% using both. Thus, no incremental predictive improvement is demonstrated in this setting. The archive contains all 3,300 configured topology cases. Interpretation is limited by five seeds, a single architecture and dataset, 282 capped H1 distance comparisons, and deferred bottleneck analysis.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking LLM Next-Step Recommendations with Knowledge Space Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13667)**  `arXiv:2611.13667`  
  _Peter Steiner, Jan Hochweber_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Selecting an LLM for next-step recommendation requires benchmarks that reflect the intended educational task and domain. We present a benchmarking method that uses Knowledge Space Theory (KST) as a formal evaluation reference. Given a task catalogue and an existing prerequisite relation, the method derives admissible knowledge states and the complete set of immediate next steps for each state. Recommendations generated by a model under a specified prompt are compared with these reference sets. A sample evaluation study uses 2,400 responses from six models across four mathematics domains, four prompt conditions, and two catalogue orders. Exact-set accuracy was 61.3% with descriptions only, 62.8% when models were asked to reconstruct prerequisites, 96.3% with supplied prerequisite relations, and 98.2% with the complete knowledge space. Performance varied across models and domains, and recommendation sets agreed across the two presentations in 85.25% of pairs. The benchmark supports comparisons of model-prompt combinations against a context-specific reference. Its scores describe structural recommendation accuracy under the evaluated conditions; they do not establish learning benefit or the necessity of using an LLM for selection.
  </details>

- **[Megrez: A Knowledge-Guided Agent for Formal Mathematics over MK Set Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13613)**  `arXiv:2611.13613`  
  _Tiancheng Xu, Wensheng Yu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose coding agents equipped with proof-assistant tools have shown strong performance on standalone competition problems, yet cumulative textbook formalization inside a low-resource mathematical library still faces three obstacles: aligning textbook concepts with library declarations, keeping long-horizon search synchronized with live proof states, and integrating locally verified proofs reliably into a growing codebase. We present Megrez, a knowledge-guided formalization agent for Rocq libraries built on Morse--Kelley (MK) set theory. Megrez coordinates a general coding agent through three mechanisms: (1) a source-rebuildable knowledge graph merging formal declarations, compiler references, and curated metadata to support retrieval via Chinese aliases, concept clusters, proof dependencies, and multi-lane full-text ranking; (2) a feedback-driven 1--5 tactic short-burst proving loop paired with a goal-local branch ledger and exponential-interval structured reflection; and (3) a three-check acceptance pipeline (statement fidelity, axiom auditing, and full builds) coupled with a multi-corpus Provider stack and a candidate-bound recoverable batch state machine. Deployment and evaluation on a point-set topology corpus demonstrate effective cross-chapter formalization and scalable premise retrieval.
  </details>

- **[Earth Embeddings in Environmental Remote Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13612)**  `arXiv:2611.13612`  
  _Yuchi Ma, Jiaqi Yang, Mengyu Liang, Ivan Higuera-Mendieta, Caleb Robinson, Anthony Ortiz, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Earth observations from satellite platforms have profoundly transformed research and applications across numerous domains. However, uneven observation coverage and frequent data gaps continue to limit their ability to capture Earth's surface dynamics. Moreover, processing multi-modal Earth observations requires substantial computational resources and sophisticated data harmonization frameworks, which may not be readily accessible to many remote sensing practitioners. Earth embeddings have emerged as promising solutions to these challenges. As general-purpose representations converted from multi-source Earth observations, Earth embeddings can support a wide range of downstream tasks with no requirement of task-specific feature design and preprocessing. In this study, we first introduce the theoretical foundations of Earth embeddings. Then, we review several representative Earth embedding products. We next examine the applications of Earth embeddings across six major domains of environmental remote sensing. Finally, we systematically assess the performance of Earth embeddings in four downstream tasks, including hyperspectral image classification, individual tree crown segmentation, crop yield prediction, and aboveground biomass mapping. Our review and case studies suggest that, despite several advantages over conventional Earth observation data, current Earth embeddings do not always outperform remote sensing imagery or hand-crafted features. Earth embeddings should not be viewed as a direct replacement for conventional EO data, but rather as representations that provide stable information across space and time. Remote sensing practitioners may consider evaluating Earth embeddings' suitability for specific tasks and using them strategically, rather than assuming superior performance by default.
  </details>

- **[Transformers Provably Learn Graph Search: Training Dynamics and the Exponential Power of Depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13594)**  `arXiv:2611.13594`  
  _Xutao Ma, Somayeh Sojoudi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable progress on complex reasoning tasks, yet the mechanisms by which they acquire reasoning capabilities and the efficiency of their reasoning remain poorly understood. In this paper, we investigate the path-finding problem over directed graphs, a symbolic abstraction of multi-step reasoning. We first characterize the architectural requirements for this task, proving that one-layer transformers require $\Omega(N^2)$ size on dense graphs to implement the key DFS child-selection primitive. While a two-layer transformer implements full DFS with $O(N\log(N))$ size. Thus, a second layer is necessary and sufficient for near-linear DFS reasoning. We then extend this expressivity analysis to deeper models: for every $k\geq3$, a $k$-layer transformer implements a bounded-reachability DFS policy with look-ahead horizon $1+2^{k-3}$, establishing an exponential increase in reachability horizon with depth. Finally, going beyond expressivity, given appropriately curated training data, we show that two-layer transformers can provably learn to execute DFS via gradient flow and generalize to unseen graphs. Together, our results provide a mechanistic account of how transformers implement graph search, how depth expands bounded look-ahead, and how such reasoning algorithms can emerge through training.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Vendor-Guided Optimizations on Edge NPUs: From NPU Only to End-to-End Performance. Extended technical report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13563)**  `arXiv:2611.13563`  
  _Youngjin Jo, Jihwan Kim, Minseok Choi, Kwanghoon Choi, Jusung Kang, Jaein Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Edge NPU performance depends not only on hardware capabilities, but also on model transformation and the overall deployment pipeline. We propose a benchmarking methodology that separates these effects by comparing three configurations: Baseline, Vendor Guided NPU Only (VG-NPU), and Vendor Guided End-to-End (VG-E2E), where VG-NPU preserves common model I/O and host-side processing while VG-E2E follows the vendor-native deployment pipeline. We apply this methodology to four edge NPUs and three vision workloads. VG-NPU improves both accuracy and Runtime-call latency in 10 of 12 NPU--workload combinations, whereas VG-E2E reduces System-level latency in all combinations but improves accuracy in only four. Moreover, NPU-level performance rankings do not always match End-to-End rankings, and VG-E2E provides an additional 10.4--70.7\% reduction in System-level latency over VG-NPU. These results demonstrate the importance of evaluating vendor-guided optimization separately at the controlled model-transformation and full deployment levels when comparing heterogeneous commercial Edge NPU platforms in practice.
  </details>

- **[Agent Benchmarks Stop Ranking Before They Run Out of Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13541)**  `arXiv:2611.13541`  
  _Zihan Dong, Qishi Zhan, Minxuan Hu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Data-science and ML-engineering agents are evaluated on benchmarks such as MLE-bench, ALE-Bench, SWE-fficiency and AlgoTune, whose tasks yield a quantity (a Kaggle metric, contest score or speedup). When one stops separating the best models, the usual remedy is a harder version. Re-scoring released runs of eight benchmarks, including SWE-bench Verified and Terminal-Bench for breadth, we find much of this saturation lies in the measurement, not the tasks. Most agent benchmarks reduce a run to one pass/fail bit, which fails in two ways. It is too coarse: neighbouring top-ten agents on SWE-bench Verified differ by less than its tests' errors, and correcting them reorders neighbours no more than deleting a random sixth of each agent's passes. It is capped at the reference: ALE-Bench's top ten, separated by contest score, tie when graded against the median human, although nearly half the runs beat that human. On fixed tasks, a higher bar only moves the problem; graded credit fixes it. We derive a clipped log-ratio score with dated anchors and cost as a separate axis. In a Terminal-Bench re-run, pass/fail-tied models differ over tenfold on the quantity the tests discard.
  </details>

- **[Nepali-English Code-Mixed Speech Recognition: Data Preparation and Adaptation of a Speech Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13512)**  `arXiv:2611.13512`  
  _Jeevan Neupane, Janardan Bhetwal, Kshitiz Paudel, Anup Aryal, Bipin Bashyal_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Nepali speakers regularly mix English words into conversation, but multilingual speech recognisers often struggle with language switches and the writing script used for English words. We study how to adapt SraVaani, a 449M-parameter hybrid TDT-CTC model, using a 172.8-hour corpus covering six Nepali speech domains. Its pretrained tokenizer does not support digits and several common symbols. Rather than replace it or discard these recordings, we generate Nepali and English readings for numbers and use zero-shot ASR output to select a plausible form. Full fine-tuning reduces test word error rate (WER) from 48.6% to 17.1%; a 12-epoch run reaches 16.3%, compared with 16.4% for fine-tuned Whisper large-v3-turbo. Freezing the encoder increases WER to 25.5%, while excluding numeric clips raises WER on numeric test speech from 14.3% to 21.3%. We also evaluate on FLEURS using both its original references and mixed-script alternatives. The comparison shows that transcription conventions explain part, but not all, of the out-of-domain performance loss.
  </details>

- **[The WoW: A Large-Scale World Knowledge Corpus for Full-Lifecycle LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13487)**  `arXiv:2611.13487`  
  _Tianze Sun, Yongyao Duan, Sam Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing Chinese educational corpora often suffer from fragmented coverage and a lack of systematic organization. In this paper, we propose WoW (\textbf{Wo}rld Kno\textbf{w}ledge), a large-scale dataset designed for the full-cycle training of foundation models. The construction of this dataset is driven by an Educational Knowledge Graph, which serves as a taxonomic backbone for the systematic collection and completion of missing educational modules across all levels. Compared to previous versions, this iteration provides enhanced systematicity, superior quality, and a larger scale, while incorporating multimodal signals and supporting every stage of model development. Experiments demonstrate that WoW significantly improves model performance across diverse benchmarks. To facilitate community research, we further provide an interactive platform for graph-based data selection and retrieval.
  </details>

- **[SafeRoute-MAS: Coverage-Aware and Risk-Calibrated Message Routing for Multi-Agent Scientific Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13454)**  `arXiv:2611.13454`  
  _Zhiming Xue, Jianan Liu, Weiran Yan, Aicheng Xie, Jing Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce SafeRoute-MAS, a coverage-aware, risk-calibrated procedure that keeps all specialist messages or removes one using only pre-integration information. On a sealed cache of 5,328 committees, its source-compatible operating point gains +1.80 percentage points (95% CI [+1.13, +2.52]) while removing 12.76% of message tokens. We also run 4,580 controlled integrations over a stratified 458-committee sample on a public gpt-oss-120b endpoint. The sample estimate is -0.09 points; design weighting to the original workload gives +0.94 points (95% CI [-1.31, +3.50]) and 18.72% acted-input reduction. A weighted token-matched comparison likewise does not establish a selector advantage (+1.68 points, CI [-1.27, +5.10]). Only 13 of 88 cached non-neutral labels (14.8%) retain direction by repeated-call majority. Expected-corruption UCL is 4.60% after weighting, but only 2 of 11 prespecified marginals pass the support/risk rule. We therefore propose a numerical qualification with Keep-All fallback, applied here retrospectively rather than validated prospectively.
  </details>

- **[SteerDistill: Quantizing LLM Agents via Closed-Loop Teacher Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13410)**  `arXiv:2611.13410`  
  _Genming Cui, Xiangyu Li, Zixu Hao, Xin Ding, Yunxin Liu, Ting Cao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-bit quantization reduces the inference cost of LLM agents but can compromise task success. Examining failed rollouts, we find that quantized agents overlook task requirements or environmental conditions, producing environment transitions that diverge from successful full-precision references. Correcting these behavioral divergences remains difficult: offline data may miss the student's failure contexts, while its own rollouts may provide few correct responses in those contexts. We introduce SteerDistill, a quantization-aware distillation method in which a full-precision teacher guides the student to revise failed responses and iteratively refines its guidance using feedback from student execution. This closed-loop teacher steering produces locally verified behavioral corrections. We internalize these corrections by training the student without guidance to match the steered student's predictions at corrected turns and the full-precision teacher's at other turns, enabling unassisted inference. Across five agentic benchmarks and three models, SteerDistill exceeds the strongest quantized baseline by 1.67--2.18 points in average score under W4A16 and stays within one point of BF16. On $\tau^2$-Bench, it outperforms QeRL by 1.84 percentage points in success rate using 67% fewer training GPU hours and produces shorter successful trajectories than the quantized baselines.
  </details>

- **[Living constitutions: Participatory alignment for sovereign foundation models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13402)**  `arXiv:2611.13402`  
  _Stine Lyngsø Beltoft, Jonas Vistrup, Peter Schneider-Kamp, Lukas Galke Poech_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In Constitutional AI, a plain-text "constitution" encodes values and gets incorporated into a model via post-training. However, the procedure for creating such a constitution poses significant challenges: who contributes, who decides which norms are included, and to what extent must the process be participatory or democratic? These challenges can be paralyzing. Yet, we argue that taking action is essential: without a deliberate constitution, sovereign models would risk misalignment or default to norms set by frontier model providers. We propose treating the development of a constitution as an evolving, participatory process akin to citizen science, iteratively drafted, tested, and refined. We therefore formulate five desirable principles: iterative, modular, justifiable, positional, and legitimate. We further argue that such a document must remain responsive not only over time but across the people it governs, and propose that provisions be marked as settled, contested, or underdetermined, so that durable disagreement is recorded in the constitution rather than resolved away. Empirically, we show via agent-based modelling that proposed process for maintaining living constitutions is robust against 50% bad actors among citizens - assuming a share of 75% participants with good intent in the deliberative body. This indicates that the proposed process tolerates a substantial share of adversarial participants.
  </details>

- **[Online-Adaptive Fuzzy Cognitive Maps for Neuro-symbolic Financial Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13388)**  `arXiv:2611.13388`  
  _Brandon Rodriguez, Abel Deulofeu, Olaoluwa Adigun_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present a neuro-symbolic framework that pairs online-adaptive Fuzzy Cognitive Maps (FCMs) with long short-term memory (LSTM) networks for financial forecasting. Financial markets exhibit regime shifts and nonlinear dynamics. Existing models often trade off one of these key properties: these models capture temporal patterns but hide causal drivers, or they recover causal graphs that cannot adapt post-training. We close this gap with two frameworks. Framework I constructs an FCM from live dual-source price series and macroeconomic indicators, and adapts its edges online through an error-driven Hebbian rule. We introduce the Asymmetric Additive Residual Fusion method to seamlessly combine the FCM and LSTM predictions under Framework I. Framework II constructs a literature-grounded, LLM-augmented FCM where every edge carries a verifiable citation that gives an auditable map of factor interactions. On NASDAQ Composite data, Framework I matched the performance of the LSTM baseline on state-directional accuracy and outperformed LSTM by 7.6 percentage points on the return-sign accuracy. Framework I outperformed standalone FCM models across both evaluation metrics. The adaptive maps converged to new regimes within 10-15 trading days, and Framework II center-node FCMs scored 68.9% to 85.7% on the state-directional accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Stage Phase-Aware Self-Supervised Reconstruction for Multi-Echo Gradient-Echo MRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13352)**  `arXiv:2611.13352`  
  _Juan Liu, Nian Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Purpose: To develop a phase-aware self-supervised framework for accelerated high-resolution multi-echo gradient-echo (mGRE) MRI that preserves anatomical detail and complex echo information for quantitative mapping. Methods: We developed multi-stage phase-aware self-supervised reconstruction (MSPR-GRE), integrating progressive k-space expansion with phase estimation and refinement. Sequential unrolled networks reconstruct complex multi-echo images and update echo-dependent phase priors. A relaxation-informed temporal subspace is used for acquisitions with at least four echoes, while shorter echo trains are reconstructed in the image domain. Training uses held-out acquired k-space samples without fully sampled targets. Evaluation included 9.4 T ex vivo mouse-brain data 30um eight-echo and 18um dual-echo) and retrospectively undersampled 7 T AHEAD human-brain data, with supervised ablations. Results:MSPR-GRE reduced artifacts in regions of rapid phase variation and preserved fine mouse-brain anatomy. Across five AHEAD test datasets at R = 4, 8, and 12, it generally improved magnitude-image, T2* map, and QSM metrics, particularly at higher acceleration. At R = 12, fourth-echo magnitude NRMSE was 12.09$\pm$0.60% versus 21.55$\pm$1.12% for SSDU. Ablations indicated benefits of phase refinement, while supervised experiments supported multi-stage phase-aware reconstruction. Conclusion: MSPR-GRE improves accelerated mGRE reconstruction and quantitative-map agreement without fully sampled training targets. Further validation of generalizability and phase-estimation accuracy is needed.
  </details>

- **[Disentangling Multimodal Rotary Positional Embedding in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13334)**  `arXiv:2611.13334`  
  _Zhaoyi Liu, Haichuan Zhang, Xiaochuan Gong, Yancheng Zhu, Bowei Tian, Wanghao Ye, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models widely adopt Multimodal Rotary Position Embedding (MRoPE), an extension of Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE) that incorporates temporal, height, and width axes to encode the spatial structure of visual tokens. However, MRoPE also assigns height and width coordinates to text tokens that lack inherent spatial structure, introducing spatial positional effects into text-vision attention. Existing MRoPE variants explore different frequency allocations and coordinate layouts, with their designs suggesting different intuitions about these cross-modal effects: they may provide useful spatial references for associating language with visual regions, or introduce unnecessary positional preferences that hinder text-vision interactions. In this work, we disentangle intra-visual spatial structure from cross-modal spatial effects to investigate this question. We identify a constraint of shared coordinate layouts that prevents eliminating cross-modal spatial effects while preserving intra-visual spatial encoding, and develop controlled experiments to isolate their respective contributions. Our results across multiple model scales, MRoPE variants, and benchmarks show that cross-modal spatial effects provide little additional benefit as spatial references and can even impair model performance, while intra-visual spatial encoding remains useful for fine-grained grounding.
  </details>

- **[Agent-Guided Discovery of Exchange-Correlation Functionals with Physics-based Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13329)**  `arXiv:2611.13329`  
  _Achuth Chandrasekhar, Amir Barati Farimani_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate, compact exchange--correlation functionals are central to predictive density functional theory. Symbolic search and language-model agents can propose new functional forms, but it remains unclear whether their gains survive self-consistency and external evaluation, whether the new terms carry physical content, whether agents outperform evolutionary search, and how language-model backbones compare. We connect frontier LLM agents to a GPU evaluator through the Model Context Protocol, with training-only fitting, persistent memory, and self-consistent and external evaluation. Opus-5.5 agents passed the screening gate with about 40-fold fewer tokens than Codex (GPT-6-Astra) agents and found a candidate with lower validation and held-out errors; only this candidate advanced to self-consistent evaluation. The resulting 13-parameter functional, O159, multiplies same-spin correlation by a smooth density window. Refitted controls favor a window that scales the entire same-spin factor, reducing it by 15--30\% at its peak, and that acts on reaction energies mainly in near-iso-orbital regions; against a refitted no-window control, it lowers heavy-atom total-energy errors on GSCDB137 by 29\%. With about 400 fits each, SyFES evolution did not improve on its GAS22 seed, whereas the agents found forms 0.10--0.13 \kcal\ lower in validation error by adding an explicit density scale. Self-consistent O159 lowers the held-out MGCDB84 test error of $\omega$B97M-V by 11.7\%, and its mean normalized error ratio on 36 GSCDB137 datasets (1.139) is comparable to $\omega$B97M-V (1.144) and below GAS22 (1.190). Frontier-LLM agents thus produced O159, a compact functional whose improvement over $\omega$B97M-V survives self-consistent, physics-based validation.
  </details>

- **[F3-AGI v4.0: Bioenergetically-Gated Active Inference, Metaplastic Preconditioning, and Residual Program Synthesis for Sample-Efficient Abstract Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13325)**  `arXiv:2611.13325`  
  _Henrique Oliveira_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Few-shot abstract reasoning on ARC-AGI requires inducing algorithmic transformations from 2 to 5 demonstration pairs without task-specific pretraining. Because few-shot demonstrations under-constrain the hypothesis space, program synthesis suffers from the Rashomon effect (predictive multiplicity): heuristic search finds spurious programs with low training error that fail on test inputs, while unconstrained library growth degrades latency. We introduce F3-AGI v4.0, a CPU-native neuro-symbolic and active inference architecture comprising three mechanisms: (1) F3-Metabolic-Optim, combining mistake-gated plasticity, Fisher-EMA preconditioning, and virtual ATP gating; (2) Adaptive Complexity-Guided Program Synthesis (AACR) with a Cycle-3 ResidualPatchSynthesizer and smallest-pair early rejection; and (3) a 512D L2-normalized random projection driving Expected Free Energy (EFE) exploration with dead-signature pruning. Across three empirical studies: (i) on a synthetic 5-task class-incremental stream without replay, F3-Metabolic-Optim achieves 91.67% final accuracy with 9.17% forgetting, skipping 93.47% of updates, cutting L1 plasticity cost by 65.7%, and running 2.19x faster than EWC; (ii) on the 400-task ARC-AGI-1 public evaluation set (offline CPU, zero LLM calls), F3-AGI v4.0 solves 57/400 tasks (14.25% pass@2; 56/400 pass@1) with a mean latency of 1.62 s/task, decomposed into 19/50 (38.00%, 1.19 s/task, a 42.5x paired speedup over Horizon 2) on the 50-task calibration slice and 38/350 (10.86%) on the 350 strictly uninspected hold-out tasks (with 7 autonomous Cycle-3 patch solutions); and (iii) in a local 5-game (15-level) ARC-AGI-3-inspired interactive suite, our agent completes 15/15 levels with 89.44% level-weighted RHAE (90.72% unweighted).
  </details>

- **[Towards Scalable Network-Based Interventions for HIV Treatment and Prevention via R2C-Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13312)**  `arXiv:2611.13312`  
  _Akseli Kangaslahti, Milind Tambe, Alastair van Heerden, Cheryl Johnson_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human Immunodeficiency Virus (HIV) remains a pressing public health challenge worldwide. Targeting limited HIV prevention and treatment resources effectively with network-based algorithms is a long-standing problem with a rich history at AAMAS and other computational venues. However, optimizing targeted biomedical interventions for minimizing spread of HIV often requires observability of the contact network of HIV transmission risk behavior among individuals, which is prohibitively expensive to collect at scale. Peer referral networks, i.e., networks collected through a recursive peer referral process, are an alternative network class often used to study HIV in hidden populations that can be collected digitally at scale, but they deviate from the underlying contact network: referrals can follow non-risk social ties and participants accept only one referral, hiding contacts among already-recruited individuals. In this work, we first formalize the novel problem of planning network-based interventions to minimize HIV spread in the hidden contact network while observing only the peer referral network. Second, we implement R2C-Agent, a decision making agent for interventions that is designed around our new conditional graph diffusion model called Referral-to-Contact Diffusion (R2C-Diff). R2C-Diff positions the peer referral process as incremental corruption of the underlying contact network and learns to reverse it. R2C-Agent plans its interventions over contact networks sampled from R2C-Diff, so its decisions account for uncertainty about the hidden contact network. Finally, we conduct simulated experiments using real-world HIV network data that demonstrate that R2C-Agent empirically plans more effective interventions than baselines from interdisciplinary literature and highlight key tradeoffs and assumptions driving performance.
  </details>

- **[Dependable Long-Horizon LLM Agents: A Trajectory-Centered Empirical Study of Where Errors Enter and Which Controls Reach Them](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13254)**  `arXiv:2611.13254`  
  _Shaohuang Wang, Jinghao Wen, Xulei Zhang, Xun Jiao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When an LLM agent fails part-way through a long task, the usual analysis records a symptom: a failure-mode label, or the agent and step judged responsible. It does not say where the error entered, which state carried it to the symptom, whether anything in the trajectory contradicted it, or which control could have caught it. We define an episode record that ties a candidate fault to its origin, propagation, detection, response and consequence, located by operational phase and state domain, and that names the control families whose observation and reach cover it. We turned the record into a written annotation protocol and applied it to 95 failed runs of six multi-agent systems from the public Who&When and MAD corpora, reading each trajectory in full. Faults enter early (median at 0.18 of the run), mostly as wrong decisions or fabrications over task state, and in 37 of 88 Who&When runs before the step the corpus' human labels mark as decisive. In 72% of failed runs the trajectory already contained evidence against the error before the failure; in 88% of those no agent acted on it. Something flagged a deviation in 31% of runs, but no response removed the deviated state. Independent judges reading the unused evidence, task contracts fixed at admission, and re-checking beliefs against the environment reach most episodes; restoring the agent's own state would have sufficed in 35 of 95 runs. A text scan of the raw corpus also shows that 45% of the hand-crafted Who&When failures ended with a provider content-filter crash after which the harness emitted a final answer no agent produced, a failure class that neither symptom taxonomies nor responsible-agent labels represent. Protocol, records and scripts are included.
  </details>

- **[NCP: Verifier-Guided Optimization of Weight-Space Control Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13236)**  `arXiv:2611.13236`  
  _Ekrem Aksoy_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present the Neural Control Plane (NCP), a closed-loop system that observes language-model execution, synthesizes candidate weight-space intervention rules, evaluates them, and maintains a versioned table of admitted policies. NCP separates a pretrained model's inference data plane from the control policy that selects temporary parameter interventions. W-ISA supplies transactional execution and parameter restoration; NCP supplies telemetry, candidate synthesis, admission criteria, priorities, and rule lifetime management. The optimization state consists of active rules and an archive of evaluated candidates, while base-model parameters are restored after each request. In a local SmolLM-135M-Instruct experiment, NCP generated three candidates from development telemetry and evaluated them on separate task examples and a 4,095-target WikiText-2 slice. One projection reduced validation token repetition from 0.4133 to 0.2191, with a perplexity change of -0.0574%. All candidates produced zero newly correct exact answers, so the configured admission gate archived all three and retained the original active policy. Fresh-process repetition and saved-table resumption reproduced the measured outcomes. NCP provides an executable framework for studying recursive self-improvement at the control-policy level through explicit synthesis, verification, and state transitions.
  </details>

- **[Decisions by Discrimination: Differential Evidence for Medical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13233)**  `arXiv:2611.13233`  
  _Yunpeng Xiao, Yuzhang Xie, Junhao Huang, Carl Yang, Xiao Hu, Kai Shu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly studied for clinical decision support, and medical question answering provides a testbed for evaluating this capability. In particular, medical multiple-choice questions (MCQs) reflect a core challenge in clinical decision-making: plausible options often share overlapping evidence, while only a few decisive factors distinguish the correct option from incorrect ones. However, existing retrieval-based and reasoning-oriented methods mainly focus on supplying broadly relevant knowledge or eliciting reasoning paths, rather than identifying the compact differential evidence that separates confusing alternatives. In this work, we present a retrieval-grounded differential evidence framework for medical MCQs. Our method extracts option-specific pieces of differential evidence and organizes them into concise evidence prompts for answer selection. This design better aligns external knowledge with differential reasoning while reducing irrelevant context. Experiments show consistent accuracy gains across multiple datasets and target LLMs. Ablation studies further show that our framework outperforms both directly using retrieved evidence chunks and closed-source LLM-generated general hints, and that its improvements are not solely explained by answer-proximal guidance, but are consistent with differential evidence serving as a compact aid for reasoning over competing alternatives. Our findings also offer insights for medical reasoning and other decision-making settings with multiple possible choices.
  </details>

- **[W-ISA: Transactional Execution of Weight-Space Intervention Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13232)**  `arXiv:2611.13232`  
  _Ekrem Aksoy_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present W-ISA, a software instruction set and transactional runtime for executing weight-space intervention programs on pretrained language models. Shape-checked instructions express parameter updates, projections, ablations, and rank-one transformations. The runtime applies these programs within request-scoped execution contexts and restores saved parameters after completion or interruption. Activation-based match-action rules connect measured model states to intervention selection. We evaluate restoration and replay on SmolLM-135M-Instruct and Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, and compare materialized rank-one updates with equivalent PyTorch hooks on the latter. In 45 timed requests, both intervention implementations produce identical greedy sequences at 32, 128, and 512 tokens; their maximum prompt-logit difference is 0.000184. On the complete WikiText-2 raw test split, the combined SmolLM attention-head ablation and MLP projection changes perplexity from 22.2353 to 22.3188, a relative increase of 0.3754%. W-ISA separates intervention specification, execution, and measurement, making parameter-level experiments reproducible and composable.
  </details>

- **[AI Alignment Is Neither Solvable nor Sufficient](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13167)**  `arXiv:2611.13167`  
  _Gerrit Großmann_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans and AI systems increasingly act within the same social and economic environments. Alignment, the goal of ensuring that AI systems act in accordance with human values, is widely regarded as a central challenge for ensuring that AI benefits society while avoiding harm. We argue that this endeavor is misguided. First, alignment is not well defined, as it presumes a sufficiently stable and identifiable set of human intentions or values. Second, where a concrete notion of alignment can be specified, it is not solvable. Third, where alignment appears solvable, solving it would not necessarily mitigate the negative consequences of AI. A key reason is that alignment is typically framed as a property of individual AI systems, while the resulting hybrid societies of humans and machines are complex systems shaped by interactions and feedback loops. Emergent risks, and not only those posed by individual misaligned systems, are therefore likely to become a major source of harm. We conclude that AI safety is an ongoing challenge and not a problem that can be solved once and for all by verifying a particular model property.
  </details>

- **[Teaching the Harness: Student-Aware Skill Transfer for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13144)**  `arXiv:2611.13144`  
  _Chang Liu, Bo Wu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective teaching requires reasoning about the learner as well as the task, a capacity that Theory of Mind describes as inferring another agent's latent state from its behavior. We ask whether a stronger LLM teaches a fixed student better when it applies this principle. The student is an LLM agent acting through a harness that fixes its tools, context, and action budget, so a procedure that helps one model can exhaust the budget of another. We introduce Student-Aware Teaching (SAT). The teacher builds a learner model that pairs an empirical profile with hypotheses about the student's limitations, tests these hypotheses with diagnostic probes, and writes reusable skills for the diagnosed failures. A skill enters the library only after it raises success on matched development tasks. With Claude-Sonnet-5 teaching DeepSeek-V4-Flash, SAT reaches 86.46% task success across four primary runs, leading both a profile-free teacher with the same framework (81.04%) and the untaught student (75.00%) in each run. Removing the analytical learner model lowers both prediction and success, and under misleading learner profiles none of the 48 candidate skills passes admission. When DeepSeek-V4-Flash and Gemini-3.8-Flash exchange libraries, each performs better with its own. Saved libraries keep average gains of up to 11.1 points after the teacher leaves. The benefit depends on a student that can follow instructions and a deployment route that delivers them.
  </details>

- **[Building World Models from Intrinsic Motivation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13113)**  `arXiv:2611.13113`  
  _Reece Keller, Declan Kutscher, Xaq Pitkow, Aran Nayebi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In the absence of extrinsic rewards or task supervision, human behavior is guided by intrinsic motivation---neural signals that drive curiosity, creativity, play, and other essential facets of intelligence. Intrinsic motivation plays an important developmental role in building robust world models that enable generalization and adaptation. Yet existing studies leave open how a broad range of intrinsic motivations interacts with world-model representation learning to support downstream control. This limits our understanding of which intrinsically collected experiences build reusable models. To address these gaps, we integrate 24 intrinsic objectives, including established methods and explicit variants spanning curiosity, entropy, and competency, into a common model-based reinforcement learning framework. We evaluate the world models they build through zero-shot, few-shot, and multi-task control in locomotion, manipulation, and open-ended gaming environments, using three representation-learning strategies: observation reconstruction, latent prediction, and redundancy reduction. Curiosity-driven exploration with observation reconstruction yields the strongest aggregate family profile, although the best individual pairing depends on the experiment. In compositional vision-based tasks, intrinsic motivation derived from both episodic and lifelong novelty---providing a complementary memory to the world model's generic recurrent state---achieves the best zero-shot transfer and few-shot adaptation. These findings motivate designing intrinsic motivation and representation learning jointly. Our results demonstrate world models as a powerful tool for measuring the quality of intrinsically motivated experience, and positions intrinsic motivation as a key ingredient for building world models autonomously.
  </details>

- **[APS-VLA: Action-Guided and Future-Aware Token Pruning for Efficient Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13030)**  `arXiv:2611.13030`  
  _Xiaoyang Xing, Chun-Mei Feng, Anthony Cohn, Xiaoliang Gong, Asoke Nandi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) policies have demonstrated strong generalization in robotic manipulation, yet processing dense visual tokens often incurs high inference latency.
