Visual token pruning offers a practical way to reduce this cost, but identifying a compact set of tokens that preserves reliable action generation remains challenging. Existing methods largely rely on static or immediate heuristic cues to estimate token importance. We introduce APS-VLA, a framework that learns visual token selection from robot demonstrations while keeping the pretrained VLA backbone frozen. A lightweight Decision Influence Estimator scores tokens conditioned on current visual observations, language instructions, and robot proprioception. Its training combines two complementary signals: action supervision matches both the full-input policy predictions and demonstrated actions, while an auxiliary visual transition objective uses demonstrated actions to predict subsequent visual features from the selected representation. At deployment, the estimator performs a single hard selection and packing step before the policy's native action-generation procedure; the auxiliary predictor is removed. Experiments with OpenVLA-OFT on LIBERO and with $\pi_{0.5}$ and XR-1 on RoboCasa365 demonstrate inference acceleration with modest changes in task success. Across five real-world tasks on two dual-arm platforms, APS-VLA reduces mean policy inference latency from 137.25 to 93.25 ms, achieving a $1.47\times$ speedup with an average success rate of 61%, compared with 60% for the unpruned baseline. Together, these results support APS-VLA as a modular, plug-in framework for efficient robotic control across VLA architectures, preserving the underlying policy parameters and native action-generation procedure.
  </details>

- **[GRAP: Gender Rubric-Aware Probing for Analyzing Gender Bias in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13024)**  `arXiv:2611.13024`  
  _Seona Seo, Geonhee Ahn, Hyunsoo Cho, Sookyung Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As LLMs increasingly mediate everyday interactions, subtle gender biases in open-ended responses pose growing societal risks. Existing probing methods have two key limitations: they rely on fixed-format tasks, such as pronoun resolution or occupation association, that alignment-tuned models can easily handle; and they compare raw group-level rubric scores, ignoring per-dimension variance and obscuring which bias dimensions reliably distinguish gender groups. We introduce GRAP (Gender Rubric-Aware Probing), a framework that uses a lightweight logistic probe to identify the most discriminative bias dimensions from responses scored across 10 rubric dimensions, operating task-agnostically on open-ended instruction following.
  </details>

- **[ChipOmni: A General-Purpose AI Agent for Electronic Design Automation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13020)**  `arXiv:2611.13020`  
  _Pengkun Wang, Rui Mao, Zhe Zhao, Dongting Xie, Haibin Wen, Song Xu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electronic design automation (EDA) workflows remain highly fragmented, requiring engineers to coordinate heterogeneous representations, specialised software tools, and iterative verification across multiple design stages. Although large language models have brought more general reasoning and code-generation capabilities to EDA, many existing approaches remain focused on isolated tasks and struggle to plan, verify, and repair designs autonomously within executable engineering environments. Here we introduce ChipOmni, a knowledge-enhanced, context-evolving AI agent framework for EDA. ChipOmni integrates multimodal understanding, dynamic planning, multi-agent collaboration, and verification-driven tool interaction into a unified autonomous system. By continually incorporating feedback from external engineering tools, the framework maintains an evolving task context, integrates domain knowledge, and iteratively refines intermediate designs. Across benchmarks for analog circuit, RTL, testbench, and formal-assertion generation, ChipOmni improves multiple task-specific measures over the evaluated baselines and solves all 30 AnalogCoder-Pro tasks. In a long-horizon case study, ChipOmni advances a natural-language RISC-V specification through three design levels to a micro-SoC GDSII layout that passes physical sign-off, without human intervention after initialization. Together, these results show that ChipOmni can coordinate verification-driven design across heterogeneous tasks and sustain long-horizon engineering workflows, advancing a shift from fragmented, tool-centred processes towards agent-centred, closed-loop systems.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Future Drafting: Mixture Block Drafters for Tree-Verified Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13017)**  `arXiv:2611.13017`  
  _Haoyu Huang, Jialiang Kang, Linlin Yang, Xiao Yang, Baochang Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Block drafters such as DFlash draft a block of future tokens in one forward pass, and draft trees such as DDTree verify the prefixes ranked highest by the product of the drafter's per-position distributions. Under tree verification, the expected number of accepted tokens is the probability mass the tree covers. We show that a product misallocates it: when the continuation can follow several futures, it splices them into chimeras, prefixes stitched together from different futures that the target never produces, and gives them as much budget as the futures themselves. Barring ties, no product-ranked tree can hold two futures that disagree in $m$ positions without $2^{m-1}-1$ chimeras, however well the drafter is trained, and in practice chimeras make up 27-33% of a 64-node tree. We propose Multi-Future Drafting (MFD), which restores the coupling with a latent variable instead of the chain rule: $K$ learned mode embeddings at the drafter's input draft $K$ coherent blocks in the same pass, and a mixture DDTree lets the verifier pick the future the target takes, losslessly and with $K+1$ extra vectors. On four Qwen targets from 4B to 27B parameters, at batch size 1, MFD reaches up to 7.8$\times$ speedup, 5-8% faster than the best single-mode tree trained identically and, on Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B, 9-16% faster than the released DARTree trained on the same prompts.
  </details>

- **[From Questions to Decisions: Benchmarking How LLM Agents Acquire Decision-Relevant Information from Human Beings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13015)**  `arXiv:2611.13015`  
  _Yuhan Tang, Ke Fan, Xinkai Zou, Baichuan Mo, Kangxin Cui, Ao Qu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are increasingly asked to make plans for people, such as scheduling drivers or assigning nurses. However, people's requests are often incomplete, and the supporting information for decision-making may be outdated, requiring agents to ask for clarification. Existing benchmarks typically provide fully specified problems or rely on uncalibrated LLM-simulated users, leaving this ability insufficiently tested. We introduce Ask2Decide, a benchmark of 43 planning tasks that evaluates whether agents ask for missing information that matters for their decisions. An operator holds facts absent from the agent's request and records, and optimization solvers determine which facts change the optimal plan. We also include similarly worded facts that leave the optimal plan unchanged to test whether agents distinguish useful information from irrelevant details. We collect 79 interaction sessions from 19 participants and find that, after an incorrect plan, human replies omit the correcting fact far more often than replies from an uncalibrated simulated user (43% versus 7%). Using these interactions, we calibrate a simulated operator that more closely matches human reply patterns. Evaluating four frontier LLMs, we find that their initial plans are infeasible on 58-98% of tasks when information is missing, compared with 5-28% when all facts are provided upfront. Agents uncover facts that change the optimal plan only slightly more often than facts that leave it unchanged (39-52% versus 32-41%). These results show that obtaining decision-relevant information through interaction remains a major bottleneck for LLM planning. This is ongoing work and will be updated. The date of this version is shown on the first page.
  </details>

- **[TrustMod-SM: A Multi-Axis Benchmark for Evaluating Trustworthiness of LLMs in Social Media Content Moderation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12970)**  `arXiv:2611.12970`  
  _Aizan Zafar, Sreekantam Sai Venkata, Gathik Jindal, Ritu Raj Sharma, Zishan Ahmad, Tulika Saha, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) show strong potential for social media content moderation, but their trustworthiness beyond overall accuracy remains underexplored. We introduce TrustMod-SM, a benchmark of roughly 29,000 instances across eight datasets, to evaluate LLM moderators on five dimensions: trustfulness, fairness, safety, robustness, and context integrity. Evaluating 16 open-weight models (0.5B-284B parameters, spanning text-only and vision-language architectures) and one closed frontier model, we find persistent trustworthiness gaps. Higher detection accuracy often coincides with greater demographic disparity, and models frequently comply with concealment or exaggeration prompts, assign high confidence to wrong predictions, rarely escalate ambiguous cases, and struggle to separate hate speech from counter-speech or reclaimed language (especially below 8B parameters). Scaling does not uniformly resolve these issues, and no single model excels across all five dimensions.
  </details>

- **[BuySideFlow: A Point-in-Time Benchmark for LLM Agents in Investment Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12966)**  `arXiv:2611.12966`  
  _Dong Bo, Fanjin Zhang, Ke Cheng, Xinxin Zhao, Xiaowei Su, Yang Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can generate SQL and SQL--Python workflows for investment research, but ensuring point-in-time (PIT) validity remains challenging. Agents must enforce disclosure cutoffs and select the appropriate historical states and record versions throughout data retrieval and financial analysis. We introduce \textsc{BuySideFlow}, a benchmark of 408 practitioner-derived Chinese-language tasks spanning equities, funds, fixed income, and macroeconomics over a fixed snapshot from a licensed production financial database. We provide executable references and a taxonomy of temporal errors, pairing result checks with expert audits of executed workflows. We evaluate nine agent configurations on all tasks, with result accuracy ranging from 16.2\% to 36.8\%. An audit of 900 runs on 100 shared tasks confirms PIT violations in 193 of the 380 runs that pass result checks (50.8\%), showing that output agreement alone does not establish temporal validity.
  </details>

- **[IIT Patna at "ICPR 2026 TVRID Competition on Privacy-Preserving Person Re-Identification from Top-View RGB-Depth Camera"](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12960)**  `arXiv:2611.12960`  
  _Abhinav Kumar, Swagatam Pati, Advait Deshmukh, Aditya Mehrotra, Bibek Das, Kumari Priya, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Privacy-aware person re-identification (Re-ID) from top-view cameras replaces frontal appearance with anthropometric shape, while depth sensing further reduces color-based identity cues. The ICPR 2026 TVRID competition evaluates this setting using 86 identities captured by four synchronized Intel RealSense D455 cameras with RGB and Depth streams across flat, ascent, descent, and oblique viewpoints. Performance is measured using mAP and CMC@1 across within-camera in/out, ascent-descent, and flat-oblique scenarios. The competition had 147 registered participants and 33 final submissions. Three teams from IIT Patna participated: 'abhinavkumar03' and 'sjkdgfhl_' ranked 5th and 7th among 31 ranked participants, and 'vivek katuri' reached 77.67% CMC@1 / 85.20% mAP on the RGB track. This paper presents the methods of the two top-10 teams. Methodology 1 (abhinavkumar03) achieves 93.51% mAP and 90.42% CMC@1 on Depth, ranking 4th of 28, while Methodology 2 (sjkdgfhl_) reaches 99.63%/99.35% on RGB and 86.26%/80.34% on Depth. Both substantially outperform the 57.19% track-average Depth mAP, with Methodology 1 yielding a 7.3-point gain over Methodology 2. These results demonstrate the importance of depth representation and supervision for privacy-preserving Re-ID.
  </details>

- **[Embodied Science Agents: Making Embodied Experimental Design Learnable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12958)**  `arXiv:2611.12958`  
  _Yihang Che, Zhongwei Yu, Yang Li, Xingyu Lu, Hongxia Yu, Jun Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in large models have accelerated AI for Science toward agentic science, where AI agents autonomously conduct scientific discovery. Realising this vision requires closing the loop between computational reasoning and physical experimentation, positioning self-driving laboratories (SDLs) as critical enablers. However, existing SDLs exhibit a fundamental paradox: foundation model integration has increased linguistic sophistication while severing physical grounding. By treating models as disembodied planners that output symbolic workflows for separate robotic systems, current architectures inherently block the development of embodied experimental intuition. We argue that overcoming this paradox requires embedding \emph{learnability} along three interdependent axes: cognitive learnability (learning what to investigate), experimental learnability (learning where to search), and embodied learnability (learning how to act). Building on this view, we introduce the Embodied Science Agent (ESA), which unifies digital intelligence with physical embodiment through three synergistic components: a Digital Agent that provides cognitive and experimental learnability via memory-based reasoning and Bayesian optimisation, an Embodied Agent that provides embodied learnability via generalisable instrument control, and an Embodied Unified Interface for seamless coordination. We validated ESA in autonomous wastewater treatment experiments, navigating over 200 million possible conditions. Through 15 continuous rounds, ESA raised the final solution transmittance from 82\% to 99.7\%, corresponding to a 98.5\% reduction in residual dye concentration and near-complete decolourisation, while its learned visuomotor policies generalised across varying instrument placements, a promising step toward next-generation SDLs for autonomous scientific discovery.
  </details>

- **[Physics2Weight: Direct Neural Network Architecture and Weight Generation from Physical Laws Using Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12954)**  `arXiv:2611.12954`  
  _Shogo Sato, Tomoyasu Shimada, Kazuhiko Murasaki, Takashi Sano_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have enabled weight-space programming (WSP), which directly generates neural network weights without gradient-based optimization. However, extending WSP to physical problems remains challenging, particularly in achieving both solution accuracy and physical consistency. In this paper, we propose Physics2Weight (PtW), a framework that extends WSP to solving partial differential equations by directly generating neural network weights and editing architectures based on governing equations and initial and boundary conditions. PtW introduces physics-constrained prompting and physics-guided architecture editing, combined with iterative weight refinement from WSP, to improve solution accuracy and physical consistency without gradient-based optimization. Experiments on four PDEs, namely the heat, advection, Burgers', and Allen--Cahn equations, demonstrate lower solution errors than conventional physics-informed neural networks, while revealing limitations in satisfying the governing equations for nonlinear PDEs.
  </details>

- **[Agent Architecture Cost-Success Trade-offs: A Controlled Evaluation across Enterprise-Style SOPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12953)**  `arXiv:2611.12953`  
  _Subhrangshu Nandi, Hitesh Arora, Yunfei Bai, Udita Patel, Chirag Shah, Hannah Kwon, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enterprises increasingly use large language model agents to execute standard operating procedures (SOPs): long, branching, tool-intensive workflows. Yet there is little controlled evidence for choosing among competing agentic architectures. Holding the model, tasks, and tools fixed and varying only the architecture, we compare four designs (a function-calling single agent, a skill-augmented agent, centralized multi-agent orchestration, and a triage swarm; eight arms) across 13 enterprise SOPs, and introduce a skill-definition quality rubric to build matched high and low-quality skill corpora. Three takeaways emerge. (1)~\textbf{There is no universal best architecture; the right choice is SOP-dependent.} On a given SOP, task success ranges from under $10\%$ on the hardest to near $100\%$ on the easiest; no architecture is statistically separable in aggregate, and added structure only plausibly pays off on complex, branching SOPs (a directional, not yet significant, trend). (2)~\textbf{Added architecture cost must be justified by a task-success gain.} Every coordinated arm (multi-agent or skill-augmented) spends $1.4$--$4.7\times$ the tokens and $2.6$--$5.8\times$ the latency of the single agent for gains that are usually small and rarely significant, making the single agent the efficient default; high-quality skills do lift success (to $73.1\%$ from $67.4\%$) while degraded skills fall below it. (3)~\textbf{Implementation quality outweighs the single- versus multi-agent choice.} A plausible multi-agent implementation loses to the single agent, yet a targeted redesign of it becomes the only arm to significantly beat the baseline ($75.6\%$ vs.\ $67.4\%$).
  </details>

- **[SetPrint: Black-Box LLM Fingerprinting via Setwise Correspondence of Response Changes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12950)**  `arXiv:2611.12950`  
  _Jinmo Han, Ju Yeon Kang, Jimin Hong, Sunghwan Ahn, Yonghyeon Jun, Nam Soo Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Black-box LLM fingerprinting aims to determine whether a target model was derived from a source model when only the output responses to input queries are observable. One way to reveal this relationship is to perturb each query and observe the resulting responses of both models, yielding two corresponding sets of response variations. These sets provide two complementary sources of information: the relations among response changes within each set, and the correspondence of response changes between the two sets. Existing approaches, however, have not fully exploited both. We introduce \textbf{SetPrint}, a training-free method that jointly captures both within-set relations and between-set correspondence. SetPrint expresses each response change relative to the mean change of its own set, and then compares changes from the two models that were induced by the same perturbation. On LeaFBench, SetPrint consistently outperforms existing training-free black-box fingerprinting methods. Further analyses attribute these gains to both within-set relations and between-set correspondence.
  </details>

- **[Tabular Foundation Models vs Tuned Gradient Boosting for Telecom Churn: A Profit- and Calibration-Aware Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12942)**  `arXiv:2611.12942`  
  _Ismail Hossain Polas_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tabular foundation models (TFMs) predict from a labelled context in a single forward pass. A recent benchmark reported that they decisively outperform tree ensembles for customer churn, but it compared them with untuned gradient-boosted trees (GBDTs) and scored only ranking metrics, while a retention campaign is judged in money. We re-evaluate TabPFN-2.5, TabPFN-3.5, TabPFN-3.5-Fast and TabICL v2 against Optuna-tuned GBDTs on five public telecom churn datasets over 10 seeded splits. We separate three questions: whether a model ranks churners well, whether that ranking earns more expected maximum profit (EMP), and whether its probabilities can drive a threshold policy. With a split-aware test and Holm correction over 20 pre-declared comparisons, TabPFN-3.5 earns significantly more EMP than the best tuned GBDT on Cell2Cell (1.9%) and Iranian (7.6%), and these two gains also hold against each of the three tuned GBDTs separately. On Orange it is ahead in all 10 splits (14.3%), but the gain is not significant after correction (Holm p = 0.155). The splits resample the same datasets, so these results do not stand for independent operators. TabICL v2, the earlier benchmark's leader, beats the best tuned GBDT only on Iranian, not all three of them, and underpredicts Orange churn by 2.1 points on a 7.3% base rate. On the datasets and splits where we tested them, a substantially larger tuning budget and a duplicate-aware split leave the main conclusions unchanged. Retuning the baseline cuts the earlier headline gap from 6.1 to 4.0 PR-AUC points without closing it. The licence of the TabPFN-3.5 weights we used (License v1.0), not scoring cost, is what stands between its gains and production use.
  </details>

- **[AI Governance Should Prioritize Control and Knowledge Boundaries Over Limiting Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12941)**  `arXiv:2611.12941`  
  _Hendrik Luuk_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid proliferation of autonomously acting AI agents makes the governance of agentic systems urgent. We propose the competence circuit, summarized as IKC + KC + C, that decomposes an agent's real-world impact into three pathways: intelligence-mediated discovery, procedural know-how, and direct control. Using the canonical fast-takeoff debate as a stress test, we formalize the conditions for intelligence-mediated fast takeoff as a conjunction jointly sufficient for fast catastrophic supremacy, showing that most rely on rapid knowledge acquisition or control attainment rather than intelligence alone. We operationalize epistemic intelligence as hypothesis-selection efficiency and validate this framework via stochastic simulation on NK fitness landscapes across six scenarios spanning the conjunctive chain. An analytic bound supported by the simulations shows that intelligence-driven speedup is capped by the ratio of competing intelligence levels regardless of task difficulty. The simulations also expose a fast-takeoff dilemma: a large intelligence-driven speedup multiplier materializes only when the hypothesis space is noisy, but the absolute timescale remains constrained by the time required for capability-critical tests, so a large multiplier need not imply rapid progress. Simulations also indicate that institutional chokepoints disproportionately slow down superintelligent agents when mandatory authorization delays are large relative to the negligible time these agents spend on unproductive search. We argue that the dominant safety levers are restricting control and enforcing knowledge boundaries rather than limiting intelligence alone, and this applies to agentic systems at any capability level. Purely digital domains weaken these bottlenecks, strengthening the case for architectures that intentionally "put physics in the loop."
  </details>

- **[Reinforcement Learning based control of Cancer Chemotherapy treatments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12940)**  `arXiv:2611.12940`  
  _Jayakody Kankanamalage Chamani Shiranthika_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning has achieved tremendous success in recent years. Extending its scope from the traditional complex games such as Atari, Go, Pokeman, and Chess, reinforcement learning has caught researchers' attention on modelling real-life scenarios. Personalized medicine and the dynamic treatment regimen are two such preliminary research fields; researchers are focusing their mindsets currently. Estimating the optimal dynamic treatment regimens from the observational real clinical data is being popularized among the researchers, widening its scope towards more advanced versions of reinforcement learning algorithms.This research is about a research study that focused on reinforcement learning techniques that have been approached in the context of cancer chemotherapy treatments. Primarily this thesis addresses the result of the study by means of the state-of-the-art on reinforcement learning techniques used in generating the optimal policy for sequential treatments, including the existing online reinforcement learning approaches. Thereby a simulation for cancer chemotherapy via traditional Q-learning would be presented. Secondly, the study will extend towards using offline reinforcement learning in the optimal policy generation. Thus, the proposed solution follows a data-driven approach, which works on the real human colon cancer clinical data.The novelty of the proposed solution resides in the implementation of an offline reinforcement learning approach by means of a model-based architecture using the conservative Q-learning and the model-based offline policy optimization algorithm. As per the experimental results and the evaluations, it was revealed that the proposed Chemo RL model caters well the problem of cancer chemotherapy treatment scheduling and serves as a supportive model for the junior oncologists in deciding their treatment decisions.
  </details>

- **[Graph Visualizations in Prompts for Knowledge Graph Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12938)**  `arXiv:2611.12938`  
  _Luiz do Valle Miranda, Andreas L. Opdahl, Grzegorz J. Nalepa_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study when and how multimodal input improves knowledge graph question answering (KGQA) in large vision--language models. Using a controlled evaluation over 1,500 WebQSP and CWQ questions and six VLMs within a fixed KGQA pipeline, we compare text-only prompting against text-plus-graph visualization at the per-question level and analyze performance changes as a function of reasoning complexity, model size, and image layout. Adding a graph image yields an average gain of 1.85 F1 points, driven by strong precision improvements on low-complexity questions (+4.65 F1). Mixed-effects and regression analyses show that these multimodal gains do not depend strongly on model scale, but are highly sensitive to graph layout: image-only settings sharply degrade performance, hierarchical Graphviz layouts outperform less organized renderings, and decomposing large subgraphs into separated paths yields the largest improvements. Taken together, the results indicate that visual prompting primarily aids KGQA by enhancing prediction precision in low-complexity contexts, provided that visual design respects cognitive and perceptual constraints. To optimize both accuracy and inference efficiency, we argue for adaptive visual prompting policies that selectively append diagrams to small, visually sparse subgraphs with well-structured layouts, avoiding unnecessary visual clutter and token overhead on complex queries. We conclude by relating these findings to a visual notation design theory, outlining how principled diagram encoding can guide future multimodal VLM prompting.
  </details>

- **[LandslideFM: A Foundation Model Towards Unified Landslide Perception and Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12936)**  `arXiv:2611.12936`  
  _Shanwen Wang, Sirui Wang, Xin Sun, Jungong Han_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Intelligent landslide detection is essential for disaster prevention. However, existing models primarily focus on isolated visual tasks, require extensive labeled data, and lack deep analytical and scene-understanding capabilities. To address these limitations, we propose LandslideFM, the first unified foundation model that connects visual perception tasks with contextual interpretation of landslides. Specifically, LandslideFM adapts to multiple visual tasks via a Source-Agreement Graduated Trust semi-supervised learning framework that leverages limited labeled data alongside abundant unlabeled data. Furthermore, predicted landslide regions provide explicit spatial guidance to a Vision-Language Model adapted through parameter-efficient fine-tuning, enabling comprehensive landslide reasoning. To support training and evaluation in this domain, we also present LandslideVL, the first benchmark for intelligent landslide analysis, combining visual imagery and newly introduced textual prompts. Comprehensive experiments across public datasets and LandslideVL demonstrate that LandslideFM achieves superior quantitative and qualitative performance. Most importantly, LandslideFM surpasses general-purpose VLMs in specialized landslide reasoning and scene analysis. Our code is released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Train on prefill, monitor at decode: Using sparse autoencoders for topic monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12935)**  `arXiv:2611.12935`  
  _Rishikesh Mallagundla, Pranav Patel_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Topic monitoring is the task of labelling the topic of each token in a model's response during generation. Activation-based monitors that run at generation time have so far been trained to detect harmful content and are limited to two or three classes. We introduce TopicScope, a framework for training causal topic classifiers on SAE features that assign a topic to every token, so that the label can change partway through a response. We train three detector architectures on synthetic multi-topic conversations covering seven topics. Across Gemma-3 1B and 4B, monitors trained on features extracted from prefill hidden states were able to generalise to decoding. We also found that the classifiers are robust to perturbations, losing at most 5 macro-F1 points when off-topic asides are inserted, topics are removed or sections are reordered. Ablations conducted to understand the contribution of the SAE revealed that classifiers reaching baseline performance without it were several times larger. Our findings show that compact topic monitors can be trained inexpensively in prefill and deployed during generation. More broadly, our work shows where SAEs are useful beyond safety classification. Our code, dataset and checkpoints are available atthis https URLandthis https URL
  </details>

- **[EarthRecord: From Pixels to Land Change with Multimodal Agents over Four Decades of Satellite Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12934)**  `arXiv:2611.12934`  
  _Haoran Zhang, Huizhen Sun, Hanbin Wen, Jie Leng, Jiaxin Zhu, Zhaohua Sun_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While multimodal agents now carry out complex data analysis with tools, their ability to turn decades of satellite imagery into reliable land-change analysis, crucial for Earth observation science, remains unmeasured. Existing benchmarks fall short: they pose questions on curated images or give agents preprocessed inputs, leaving out the raw multi-sensor archives where such analysis begins. We introduce EarthRecord, a benchmark built on the raw record of 56 sites: four decades of Landsat, MODIS, VIIRS, and Sentinel-2 data, with cloud masking and cross-sensor harmonization left to the agent. It poses 238 land-change questions across ten analysis tasks, scored against withheld references from global products, agreement among methods, and visual interpretation. Two controls test what answers rest on: the closed book removes the record, and instrument pairs alter one satellite's data while nothing changes on the ground. Evaluations of twelve multimodal agents reveal that leading agents complete most analyses end to end but falter when an answer requires mapping from imagery. Further analysis shows that the record moves answers from prior knowledge to the pixels but not yet to the real change on the ground: when one satellite's data are altered, half of the right answers follow the alteration. EarthRecord thus provides a principled platform for measuring and advancing agents from pixels to land change.
  </details>

- **[Agentic discrete-event simulation for manufacturing system design: an open 3D simulator, a benchmark and an industrial case](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10602)**  `arXiv:2611.10602`  
  _Javier Pernas-Álvarez, Diego Crespo-Pereira_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The design of manufacturing systems relies on discrete-event simulation (DES) models that experts still build in tool-specific interfaces. AI agents can now write simulation code, but every model they write from scratch is new, unverified software. We present SimuLean, an open-source 3D DES simulator on the Unity engine designed to be programmed by AI agents: its modelling semantics are verified once, in the platform, and the agent builds and studies one model through domain-level commands, in the 3D scene or headless; physically simulated durations are calibrated for fast headless studies. We evaluated it on 18 manufacturing modelling tasks, from single machines to published production lines. A frontier agent built valid models in 93 of 108 attempts (86%), in about two minutes each; on the benchmark's interface version, two frontier agents built 105 of 108 models through domain commands alone, so that each comes with a record of how it was built. Every residual failure was silent - a model that builds and runs but answers wrongly - and only comparison with reference models revealed them all. In an industrial case, one agent working alone rebuilt, verified and studied a robotised welding workshop from a published study, in SimuLean and in SimPy. Comparing them with the published, expert-built model exposed an error in it; against the corrected model, the agent-built models agree within 1%. One agent can already build complete, realistic and traceable models for the design of manufacturing systems; independent reference models are what make them trustworthy.
  </details>

- **[Seeing Isn't Enough: Unlocking Visual Reasoning by Bridging the Reasoning-Access Gap with TIGER](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10552)**  `arXiv:2611.10552`  
  _Gregory Kang Ruey Lau, Minh Huynh Nguyen, Bryan Kian Hsiang Low_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) inherit strong textual reasoning capabilities from their language backbones and text pretraining, but often fail to apply the same level of competency to visual inputs. We show that this degradation cannot be attributed to only perception failures, by evaluating models on semantically-equivalent text and rendered-text image inputs: models can accurately read text in the images and recover near-text performance when provided their own transcriptions, but just cannot reason well on the images directly. We characterize this discrepancy as a modality reasoning-access gap, where models fail to route perceived visual content into existing reasoning machinery accessible with text. To bridge this, we propose TIGER (Text-to-Image Gap-targeted training for Enhanced Reasoning), which first mines synthetic modality counterfactuals where a model succeeds on a text problem but fails when provided the corresponding rendered image, and post-trains the model on the image input via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), along with SFT and DPO variants. Importantly, although trained exclusively on synthetic rendered-text images with no multimodal annotations, TIGER results in model performance improvements across diverse real-world visual benchmarks including geometric diagrams (MathVerse), multi-disciplinary sciences (EMMA, MMMU), complex charts (ChartQAPro), and embodied spatial reasoning (RoboSpatial-Home). TIGER also complements existing state-of-the-art RLVR visual reasoning methods, yielding further performance gains. Analysis involving reasoning-subspace activation and activation patching provide support indicating that TIGER helps to align visual-to-reasoning pathways toward text reasoning states. Our implementation is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[FAIDER: Fairness-Aware Item-Level Decoding for Large Language Model Recommenders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10285)**  `arXiv:2611.10285`  
  _Ziwei Wu, Jingrui He_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) recommenders can produce semantically different recommendations when a user's protected attribute changes, even if the interaction history remains fixed. Controlling this disparity is challenging when model weights and full-vocabulary logits are inaccessible. We propose FAIDER (Fairness-Aware Item-level DEcoding for Recommendation), which incorporates fairness into list construction by selecting complete item candidates rather than manipulating token-level logits or repeatedly repairing finished outputs. The central idea is to separate the operational criterion for a violation from how strongly generation is constrained. Conformal-inspired empirical calibration fixes a cross-group semantic-disparity threshold, while online decoding applies either hard filtering or a soft penalty adjusted through dual-inspired feedback to target a prescribed violation rate. Separately, we establish a pathwise guarantee: under a uniform enforceability assumption, the soft decoder's empirical violation rate exceeds its target by at most $O(1/T)$. Experiments across recommendation benchmarks and LLM backbones show that Soft FAIDER improves both measured semantic fairness and ranking quality over the evaluated prompt-repair baselines. It also generally achieves a more favorable fairness-utility balance than the hard variant, indicating that stricter per-recommendation constraints do not necessarily yield lower measured disparity.
  </details>

- **[Key Institutional Considerations for Designing Effective Independent Verification Organizations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10168)**  `arXiv:2611.10168`  
  _Uma Kalkar, Rumman Chowdhury, Reva Schwartz, Gabriella Waters_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Independent verification organizations (IVOs) have emerged as a popular, bipartisan solution for facilitating high-quality independent evaluations of AI technologies. But momentum is outpacing design: there is emerging consensus on the necessary operating conditions for robust IVOs, but little discourse on institutional and policy specifications to ensure those conditions are met. IVOs are currently envisioned primarily as a mechanism operating at the AI frontier and model level to evaluate catastrophic risks. Little work has yet been done on how to establish or enforce an IVO ecosystem that is equipped to handle myriad risks (i.e., beyond catastrophic ones) and operate at the system and application level across the deployment and use stages of the AI lifecycle. This paper argues that IVO design should ensure the capacity for evaluating AI models, systems, and applications in context for both frontier companies and enterprise companies & AI-adopting organizations. Additionally, this paper provides early considerations to advance IVO discourse from normative to operative, and from existential to everyday risks. Drawing from existing institutional constructs in financial services, education, public health, and science, we outline key considerations on whom IVOs serve, what access they require, how they interact with external governing structures, how they are funded, and how they fit into the wider assurance ecosystem. Much of the structure and knowledge necessary to holistically design IVOs and their ecosystem does not yet exist, even as companies rush to ink agreements with their chosen evaluators.
  </details>

- **[Veterans' Mental Health Crisis Severity Annotation: Do LLMs Agree More Than Human Experts?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10048)**  `arXiv:2611.10048`  
  _K M Sajjadul Islam, Pranav Yaramalla, Nadiyah Johnson, Mark Flower, Praveen Madiraju, Zeno Franco_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) offer a scalable approach for annotating mental health and crisis-related social media content, but their reliability in safety-critical tasks remains unclear. This study evaluates LLM-based crisis-severity annotation of veteran-related social media posts against annotations from eight Veteran Subject Matter Experts (SMEs). Using 149 tweets and five severity categories, we evaluate 15 open-source and 10 proprietary LLMs across eight prompt variants, examining annotation performance, inter-model agreement, prompt sensitivity, and high-severity crisis detection. Human reviewers showed higher ordinal than nominal agreement ({\alpha} = 0.494), indicating that disagreements often occurred between neighboring severity levels. In contrast, selected LLMs demonstrated substantially higher inter-model consistency, with ordinal agreement ({\alpha}) reaching 0.8336 under the baseline prompt. Proprietary models generally showed better agreement with expert annotations and greater stability than open-source models. GPT-5.6-Terra achieved the best overall performance, while the open-source Qwen2.5-7B showed the strongest performance in identifying Acute Crisis with Suicidal Ideation cases. However, higher model consistency did not necessarily correspond to agreement with human judgments or reliable detection of rare high-severity cases. Label-flipped demonstration experiments also revealed model-specific sensitivity to misleading in-context examples, while explanatory demonstrations did not consistently improve performance. Overall, LLMs show promise as scalable complements to expert annotation, but require careful evaluation before use in high-stakes mental health applications.
  </details>

- **[Circe: Training-Free LLM Self-Evaluation via Component Interaction Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07948)**  `arXiv:2611.07948`  
  _Shuyi Miao, Zheng Li, Xu Xie, Linrui Xu, Fei Shen, Xiaoyu Du, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in real-world applications, evaluating the correctness of their responses is crucial for reliable use. However, such evaluation is challenging because reference answers are rarely available in practice. Existing reference-free methods mainly rely on output signals, which may assign high scores to incorrect responses and thus do not necessarily reflect correctness. This motivates exploring the model's internal computation for complementary correctness signals. Recent studies exploit internal states, but they mainly analyze hidden-state trajectories across layers, which record only the combined updates of attention and feed-forward networks (FFNs) and overlook how these components interact. We therefore propose Circe, a training-free self-evaluation method that assesses a response using both internal and external signals, without reference answers or repeated sampling. For internal signals, we first introduce component interaction consistency (CIC), which measures the directional alignment between attention updates in earlier layers and attention-induced FFN output changes in later layers. We further incorporate representation geometry (RG) to characterize normalized displacement and angular changes along layerwise hidden-state trajectories. For the external signal, we convert the mean negative log-likelihood of generated tokens into a bounded likelihood confidence (LC) score. Finally, we fuse CIC, RG, and LC into a unified correctness score. Experiments on five LLMs and six benchmarks across four domains show that our method distinguishes correct from incorrect responses more effectively than existing baselines, and generalizes to multilingual, multimodal, and open-world settings.
  </details>

- **[CompressionBench: How can your coding agents compress LLMs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06858)**  `arXiv:2611.06858`  
  _Xinrui Zhong, Zhifeng Li, Zhiming Wang, Xinze Feng, Yongqi Liu, Shanglin Yang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive self-improvement requires AI systems to improve the models and infrastructure used by subsequent systems. LLM compression offers a concrete efficiency objective: retain a pretrained model's behavior within a smaller model storage budget. We study whether coding agents can carry out this step autonomously. We formulate compression as a terminal task in which an agent receives a source checkpoint, storage limit, inference environment, and time budget, then constructs compressed weights and executable inference code. The agent controls its algorithm and experiments; perplexity feedback guides search, and independent verification checks the final model. Across six agents and six model--size settings, 29 of 36 submissions pass verification. Selecting the lowest-perplexity agent design per task yields an average 3.4% perplexity increase over BF16 and 77.33% downstream accuracy, compared with 79.56% for BF16. Recorded experiments reveal how agents achieve these outcomes and where improvement breaks down. Agents lower PPL in 21 of 24 revisions at nearly fixed storage; complete-model feedback reverses 17 of 79 prior local rankings; and generation and inference tests expose failures that likelihood alone misses. The results identify representation design, experimental judgment, and capability validation as central to agent-driven model compression, a measurable component of efficiency-oriented self-improvement.
  </details>

- **[Kierston & KierstonBench: Simulation of Allentown, PA and Evaluation of Language Models in Crime Investigation and Surveillance Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04785)**  `arXiv:2611.04785`  
  _Andrew Semenov, Svyatoslav Dorofeev_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are candidates for collecting and analyzing records in criminal investigations and mass surveillance. Their capability and accuracy in this work cannot currently be measured. Real deployments lack the ground truth needed to score a model's conclusions, and no benchmark evaluates these capabilities in a simulated environment. We present Kierston, a city-scale simulation engine, and KierstonBench, a benchmark that tests these capabilities against known simulated events. Kierston models approximately 125,000 residents of Allentown, Pennsylvania, generating their daily activities and the telecommunications, financial, administrative and license-plate records that surveillance and investigative systems collect about them. These records derive from a common behavioral timeline and are distributed across sources representing separate providers. Models investigate through a restricted records interface, linking observations across sources without access to the underlying simulation. The benchmark comprises 32 task families covering record linkage, routine reconstruction, incident investigation and assessment of deceptive accounts. In an evaluation of sixteen language models, most could not perform records investigation and submitted identifications the records did not support. A small number of frontier models solved most of the tasks and abstained when evidence was insufficient. In some cases these models still fell for common deceptions and assumptions, and none replaced an investigator.
  </details>

- **[FORGE: Fault-Oriented Rework Code Generation for Executable Interlocking Assembly](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02536)**  `arXiv:2611.02536`  
  _Fuqiang Wang, Jiahao Fu, Ren Hefeng, Yining Qian, Chuanting Zhang, Cheng Tan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models have demonstrated strong capabilities in structured reasoning and code generation, yet identifying an assembly fault does not guarantee the ability to generate executable rework programs. In interlocking assemblies, a locally plausible repair program may violate blocking dependencies, omit required recovery operations, or terminate in an incorrect configuration. We identify this challenge as fault-conditioned executable rework generation, where models must jointly reason about structural failures and generate programs that restore constrained assembly states. To benchmark this capability, we introduce FORGE-Bench, containing 4,000 tasks across 26 interlocking assemblies with five assemblies held out for evaluating generalization to unseen structures. We further propose FORGE (Fault-Oriented Rework Code Generation for Executable Interlocking Assembly), a structured state-to-program framework that decomposes rework generation into Rework Structuring and Action Grounding. Rework Structuring captures fault-induced recovery dependencies, while Action Grounding maps these dependencies into state-conditioned executable actions. Experiments show that locally reasonable repair code is insufficient for reliable assembly recovery, and that connecting structural recovery reasoning with executable action realization is essential for verified target restoration.
  </details>

- **[MAGAR: Multimodal Agents for GUI-Based 3D Mechanical Assembly and Minimal Rework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02472)**  `arXiv:2611.02472`  
  _Jingxuan Wei, Jiahao Fu, Fuqiang Wang, Xiaoming Wu, Yuwen Liu, Kaiwen Wei, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A GUI command can be correct at the interface level yet still produce an incorrect assembly. An agent must identify the relevant components, respect their structural relationships, and determine which parts should move during recovery. We introduce MAGAR-Bench, a benchmark of 4,000 multimodal samples from 26 assembly families that separates Assembly Decision, Atomic Action, and Minimal Rework. For rework, the objective counts distinct moved parts within the validated recovery candidate set for each state, rather than the number of actions. We also introduce MAGAR, a selective correction framework built on a frozen policy obtained by supervised fine-tuning. Its key design matches correction granularity to output dependencies. Structured candidates revise assembly fields and rework scope, while complete action candidates keep native GUI primitives coupled with their parameters. A learned selector retains the original prediction unless a candidate passes a task-specific intervention threshold. On recorded states from held-out assembly families, MAGAR improves Overall, the task-balanced aggregate, from 37.50 to 41.39, with gains on all three tasks. Across 209 interventions, 70 correct an error and 22 introduce one. These results support task-specific correction while highlighting the remaining tradeoff between fixing errors and preserving reliable predictions.
  </details>

- **[Low-Margin Branch Points in Deterministic LLM Deliberation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00546)**  `arXiv:2611.00546`  
  _Hajime Shimao, Warut Khern-am-nuai_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deterministic multi-agent LLM deliberation can exactly re- play a fixed transcript, yet exact replay alone does not test local resistance to controlled internal interventions. Because each response becomes context for later agents, a changed greedy token can redirect a transcript-mediated execution. We present a white-box audit of this local failure mode in a five-agent committee under greedy decoding. We prospectively select low- and high-margin token positions, require exact no-op re- play, inject norm-relative residual displacements, and measure first transcript divergence within ten generated tokens. Low- margin positions are more branch-sensitive than high-margin controls, while matched margin-reducing gradient directions branch substantially more often than margin-increasing con- trols. We then continue selected realized branches through the committee to distinguish textual persistence from preference and final-decision changes. The contribution is a controlled audit for local branch susceptibility in this specified protocol, not an estimate of deployment frequency, decision correct- ness, or a phenomenon unique to multi-agent systems.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Reason from Reference Samples Alone: Reinforced Adversarial Learning for Modern LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38851)**  `arXiv:2610.38851`  
  _Albert Bou, Javier Valdes-Aleman, Jon M. Laurent, Samuel G. Rodriques, Andrew D. White, Michaela Hinks, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In many settings where LLM post-training is desirable, high-quality answers are recognizable but difficult to score with explicit rewards. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is limited to tasks where an explicit correctness signal is available. Prior adversarial approaches to training reasoning LLMs either complement verifiable rewards with co-trained critics or require reference reasoning traces. We ask whether a co-trained LLM judge can turn reference answers alone into the sole reward for on-policy post-training of reasoning LLMs. We call this method Reinforced Adversarial Learning (RAL). We first validate RAL on three verifiable tasks (integer multiplication, code-based arithmetic-expression synthesis, and SMILES-to-formula translation), using the verifiers only for evaluation. With sufficiently capable judges and enough training, RAL matches direct RLVR. We then apply RAL to scientific abstract generation in two settings. In the first setting, RAL guides the generator to produce abstracts in the scientific domain of the reference abstracts, even though the prompt leaves that domain unspecified. In the second setting, we generate abstracts for U.S. National Institutes of Health grant proposals conditioned on the grant title. Human scientists and an ensemble of LLM judges find our generations harder to distinguish from real abstracts than those of frontier-LLM baselines. These results suggest a path toward post-training reasoning models in scientific and other high-value domains, where increasingly capable judges infer hard-to-specify reward functions from example answers.
  </details>

- **[Learning Where to Trust: Reliability-Guided Expert Routing and Velocity Gating for 3D Deformable Registration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36672)**  `arXiv:2610.36672`  
  _Chenyu Guo, Wenjie Dai, Yiqian Chang, Zhiyuan Zhang, Yunfan Wei, Shuhan Qi, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deformable image registration relies on accurate anatomical correspondences. However, explicitly coupling local matching reliability with spatial context selection and deformation update strength remains insufficiently explored in learning-based registration. This challenge is particularly pronounced in homogeneous, weakly textured, and severely deformed regions, where ambiguous matches may induce erroneous deformation updates. We propose ReliMorph, a reliability-routed symmetric framework for 3D deformable registration that couples correspondence assessment with spatial context selection and updatethis http URLcorrelation distributions characterize correspondence ambiguity using shared anatomical features with modality-specific adaptation. These reliability cues guide spatial expert fusion and gate residual velocities during coarse-to-fine refinement. Updated inverse-inconsistency feedback guides routing and gating at finer pyramid levels. To our knowledge, ReliMorph is the first 3D deformable registration framework to jointly condition spatial expert routing and residual velocity gating on correspondence reliability. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ReliMorph consistently outperforms competing methods in anatomical overlap, highlighting its effectiveness across unimodal and multimodal registration tasks.
  </details>

- **[Are Spiking Neural Networks Harder to Steal? The Role of Stochastic Encoding in Model Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36298)**  `arXiv:2610.36298`  
  _Ayana Moshruba, Maryam Parsa_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine learning models deployed on edge devices are vulnerable to model stealing attacks, in which an adversary replicates a model using only its predictions. Existing defenses add mechanisms that are costly on resource-constrained hardware. Spiking neural networks (SNNs) offer energy-efficient inference at the edge and have been shown to leak less information about their training data than artificial neural networks (ANNs). This work investigates whether SNNs also resist model stealing and identifies the source of any such resistance. We evaluate four model stealing attacks against SNN and ANN models on three image datasets and introduce a metric that accounts for the stochastic outputs of SNNs. SNNs exhibit greater resistance than ANNs: on average, surrogate models extracted from SNNs agree with their targets 38% less often outside the training distribution, and targeted adversarial examples crafted on these surrogates transfer 54% less often. This resistance, however, arises mainly from the randomness of their input encoding rather than from their spiking computation, and an adversary who repeats each query can largely overcome it. SNNs therefore protect against model stealing without additional defense mechanisms, but this protection does not withstand adaptive adversaries.
  </details>

- **[Scoped Semantic Invariance as a Foundation for LLM Reliability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33267)**  `arXiv:2610.33267`  
  _Rosario Uceda-Sosa, Maria Chang, Karthikeyan Natesan Ramamurthy, Moninder Singh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLMs often answer semantically equivalent queries inconsistently, even when the underlying facts are known. We formalize consistency as semantic invariance over equivalence classes of queries and show that invariance under arbitrary meaning-preserving transformations is undecidable in general. This motivates scoped semantic invariance, where model behavior is evaluated relative to bounded semantic domains.We operationalize this framework using knowledge graphs (KGs), which act as explicit semantic control structures defining local semantic constraints and grounding contexts. Across the MITRE D3FEND ontology and eight Wikidata- derived domains, we show that inconsistencies are structured rather than random, concentrating in localized hotspot subgraphs organized around recurring TREE, STAR, and CYCLE patterns that remain stable across models and domains.We further show that selective graph grounding improves correctness and cross-model consistency, while indiscriminate grounding introduces regressions. A layered grounding strategy achieves the strongest monotonicity profile, preserving correct knowledge while reducing inconsistency. Finally, we show that hotspot structures discovered from simple consistency probes transfer to more realistic compositional reasoning tasks.
  </details>

- **[TACS-RF: Tool-driven Agentic Circuit Synthesis of RF and mmWave Modules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31882)**  `arXiv:2610.31882`  
  _Tristan Liang, Aravind Nagulu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present the framework of TACS-RF - a Tool-driven Agentic Circuit Synthesis of RF and mmWave modules. In this framework, a large-language-model (LLM) coding agent (Claude Opus 4.8) directly drives industry-standard electronic-design-automation (EDA) tools such as Cadence Virtuoso/Spectre, Calibre, Cadence EMX, and Ansys HFSS to design RF and mmWave modules. We validate the framework through the agentic design of a 240-251 GHz sub-THz transmitter in a 90 nm SiGe BiCMOS process. The agent semi-autonomously designed the sub-blocks including a 60 GHz push-push Colpitts VCO with second-harmonic extraction, multistage differential cascode power drivers, a push-push frequency doubler, a differential bowtie antenna, and all the interstage matching networks. The transmitter chain with parasitic-extracted devices and EM-verified passives delivers +5.1 to +6.3 dBm across an 11 GHz varactor-only tuning range, phase noise of -80 to -88 dBc/Hz at 1 MHz offset while consuming a DC power of 361 mW. We describe the agent-driven design flow and the resulting division of labor between human and agent, taking a step toward a "text-to-ASIC" future for designing RF/mmWave hardware.
  </details>

- **[IECG: Weakly-Supervised Interpretability-Enhancing Foundation Model for Electrocardiogram](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28083)**  `arXiv:2610.28083`  
  _Huynh Dang Nguyen, Trong-Thang Pham, Anh-Thuan Do, Phuong-Nhi Dinh, Brijesh Patel, Ngan Le, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electrocardiography (ECG) is a cornerstone of cardiac diagnosis, yet most machine learning methods for ECG analysis lack interpretability, limiting their clinical adoption. We present IECG, a weakly-supervised ECG foundation model that jointly performs diagnostic classification and abnormality localization without requiring manual abnormal region annotations, overcoming a long-standing bottleneck in interpretable ECG learning. Central to IECG is a weakly-supervised attention strategy that explicitly incorporates clinical priors by deriving abnormality heatmap pseudo-labels from established ECG clinical knowledge. These pseudo-labels guide the model's CLS-token attention through a KL-divergence loss, encouraging it to focus on diagnostically relevant waveform regions. During pretraining, IECG is jointly optimized with clinically guided attention supervision, ECG-text contrastive alignment, and report captioning objectives, enabling it to learn rich multimodal representations while producing localized waveform evidence. By explicitly aggregating features from clinically guided attention regions, IECG grounds its diagnostic predictions in interpretable waveform evidence without sacrificing classification performance. Extensive experiments demonstrate that IECG achieves state-of-the-art abnormal region localization while maintaining strong classification performance, advancing trustworthy foundation models for ECG analysis.
  </details>

- **[MoveBench: A Rubric-Based Benchmark for Evaluating Large Language Models in Sports and Health Coaching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26185)**  `arXiv:2610.26185`  
  _Yitong Li, Jiang Bian, Jingling Wang, Pan Ding, Chengying Gu, Xing Huang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite rapid advances in large language models (LLMs), their deployment in the sports and health domain remains high-risk due to the absence of domain-specific evaluation benchmarks. Existing general-purpose benchmarks (e.g., MMLU and HELM) primarily assess broad knowledge and reasoning capabilities, but fail to capture critical domain requirements such as exercise safety, physiological appropriateness, personalization, and actionable coaching quality. This gap is particularly concerning in fitness and health applications, where incorrect or unsafe recommendations can lead to physical harm. To address this limitation, we introduce MoveBench, the first comprehensive benchmarking framework specifically designed for evaluating LLMs in sports and health coaching. The proposed benchmark integrates heterogeneous task formats, including multiple-choice questions (MCQs) and open-ended question answering (OpenQA), grounded in real-world user scenarios and paired with structured rubrics emphasizing safety compliance, scientific validity, and training logic. Through large-scale evaluation of 15 diverse LLMs on 809 questions with 5,012 rubric-level criteria, our results reveal substantial performance variability, particularly in safety awareness, structured program design, and personalization capability, highlighting a critical gap between knowing and doing. Our work establishes a foundational evaluation standard for sports and health LLM applications and provides a reproducible framework for advancing safe, reliable, and context-aware AI-driven coaching systems. Open resources are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MSCR: Jointly Balancing Utilization Across Modalities and Discovering Synergistic Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24395)**  `arXiv:2610.24395`  
  _Xinyu Chen, Liangjian Wen, Jiang Duan, Dongkai Wang, Yong Dai, Jiayu Bai, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In multimodal learning, modality imbalance often lets one modality dominate optimization and keeps the model from fully exploiting cross-modal interactions. Balance-oriented methods such as the Multimodal Competition Regularizer (MCR) perform well by alleviating this imbalance during training. These dynamic balancing methods, however, mainly adjust modality contributions and do not consider how imbalance affects the formation of synergistic information during fusion. Synergy is a major source of multimodal advantage, because it is predictive gain that cannot be recovered from unimodal evidence alone. We propose the Multimodal Synergistic Competition Regularizer (MSCR), a fusion method that jointly promotes balanced modality utilization and the discovery of synergistic information. MSCR has two complementary objectives. An anti-dominance objective suppresses excessive unimodal influence and leaves room for synergistic information, and a synergy objective encourages the fused predictor to outperform a non-synergistic unimodal reference. An adaptive gate further concentrates the regularization on samples that need stronger collaborative modeling. Experiments on five benchmarks show that MSCR consistently improves performance over MCR and strengthens synergistic capability. It raises conditional recovery, the accuracy of the fused prediction on samples that the unimodal predictors misclassify, as well as the share of the model's output that InterSHAP attributes to cross-modal interactions. Its gain in conditional recovery persists when the fusion module is Transformer-based. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Bridging the Evidence-to-Execution Gap:A Reflective Agent for Multi-Objective Peptide Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06190)**  `arXiv:2610.06190`  
  _Haosen Zhang, Yang Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models can reason over scientific literature, but textual plans do not reliably produce biological sequences with the intended properties. Sequence generators capture sequence patterns, yet they do not interpret evidence, choose interventions, or reflect on outcomes. This separation creates an evidence-to-execution gap between scientific reasoning and sequence-level control. We introduce EASER, a reflective agent that connects the two through a learned property interface. The interface consists of property-specific low-rank directions trained offline and fixed during search; an LLM controller (the controller) composes them to steer a frozen diffusion generator. From retrieved evidence, sequence context, and prior outcomes, it proposes intervention hypotheses that specify anchors, editable positions, and coefficients. Probe-and-Steer tests these hypotheses and allocates further samples from their joint predicted responses; reflection updates the agent's memory. On computational antimicrobial peptide design, EASER balances activity, non-hemolysis, and non-toxicity. Explicit hypothesis formulation yields larger multi-objective gains than direct action generation, and ablations support evidence retrieval, episodic history, reflection, and Probe-and-Steer. EASER achieves the highest mean hypervolume and lowest mean IGD+ among the evaluated search policies and generators on screened candidates. These results show that a learned property interface can make literature-grounded reasoning executable at the sequence level.
  </details>

- **[Decide, Ask, or Defer: Clinical LLMs under Incomplete Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04542)**  `arXiv:2610.04542`  
  _Mingzhan Yang, Weili Wu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical LLMs must decide not only what diagnosis to produce, but also whether the available evidence is sufficient for autonomous decision making. Binary DECIDE/ABSTAIN formulations merge distinct non decision states and do not explicitly evaluate information acquisition. We introduce a DECIDE/ASK/DEFER formulation together with a blinded protocol that prevents models from using evidence completeness metadata. We evaluate Qwen, Gemini, and GPT on 200 matched clinical evidence states constructed from DDXPlus. The models show substantial differences in action selection under identical evidence, with disagreement in 137 of 200 states. For Qwen, a matched targeted versus random analysis shows that selected information changes the likelihood of a subsequent autonomous decision more clearly than diagnostic correctness. Its matched DECIDE/ABSTAIN baseline further reveals a safety autonomy tradeoff: the three action policy rescues some erroneous autonomous decisions but also removes some correct autonomous deci sions. These results show that separating information acquisition from clinician deferral exposes behavior that binary abstention hides, without yielding a uniformly improved decision policy.
  </details>

- **[A Comprehensive Survey of Agent Distillation across Models, Artifacts, and Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36630)**  `arXiv:2609.36630`  
  _Ziluowen Luo, Senzhang Wang, Chaozhuo Li, Jun Yin, Hao Yan, Ming Cheng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge distillation provides a teacher-student framework for transferring capabilities at reduced cost and has been widely studied for large language models (LLMs). As LLM-based agents move from response generation to sustained, closed-loop task solving, distillation expands beyond model parameters to knowledge retained in reusable resources and execution workflows. This shift from model-level to agent-level knowledge transfer supports efficient deployment, broader capability access, and continual self-evolution. However, existing surveys largely examine model distillation and agent system design separately, limiting comparison of transfer strategies across agent components. To bridge this gap, we formalize Agent Distillation as persistent knowledge transfer between teacher and student agents and present its first unified survey. Distinguishing experience used for transfer from knowledge retained for reuse, we develop a knowledge-substrate taxonomy based on where knowledge persists: models, artifacts, or harnesses. Building on this taxonomy, we analyze cross-substrate transfer paths and hybrid strategies. To assess the resulting capabilities, we review benchmarks and evaluation protocols for retention, causal attribution, and deployed utility without further teacher support. This survey provides a unified basis for studying agent knowledge transfer and developing efficient, adaptable, and self-evolving agents.
  </details>

- **[Learning Action Models with Conditional and Quantified Effects via Uncertainty-Guided Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30955)**  `arXiv:2608.30955`  
  _Jeffrey Jewett, William Solow, Sandhya Saisubramanian_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate action models are critical for effective planning. Existing action-model learning methods largely assume simple action representations or become computationally intractable when learning conditional and quantified effects. We present Online Hypothesis-Driven Conditional Action Model Learning (OHCAM), an online approach for learning action models with conditional and quantified effects from limited interactions with the environment. OHCAM maintains a belief over hypothesized action models and actively selects informative actions to reduce uncertainty by maximizing disagreement among competing hypotheses, while being robust to noisy observations. To enable scalability, OHCAM begins with a small set of simple action model hypotheses and expands to more complex conditions only when the current hypotheses become inconsistent with the data. Experiments on six benchmark planning domains demonstrate that OHCAM is sample efficient in learning action models that solve substantially more tasks than baselines, even with observation noise. We validate OHCAM on two tasks using a Kinova Gen3 robot, demonstrating the real-world applicability of our approach.
  </details>

- **[TRACE: Business Rule-Grounded Reasoning Curriculum for Knowledge-Preserving Parametric Tool Retrieval in Enterprise LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22639)**  `arXiv:2607.22639`  
  _Sai Shruthi Sistla, Ashutosh Hathidara, Christopher Toukmaji, Mayank Shrivastava, Karthikeyan Asokkumar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parametric retrieval enables LLMs to retrieve tools implicitly by assigning each API a unique virtual token and training the model to generate it via constrained beam search. Toolsense shows that this regime has two critical drawbacks: it destroys parametric tool knowledge during training, and its beam-search decoding is too slow for real-time deployment. We introduce TRACE (Tool Retrieval via Augmented Chain-of-thought and Enterprise rules), a two-stage curriculum that resolves this dissociation. Stage 1 reuses the multi-format memorization SFT from ToolSense to seed tool knowledge with LoRA. Stage 2 is our core contribution: the model is trained to emit a thinking trace before producing a JSON list of tool tokens, using two data sources -- RRB pairs from ToolSense and queries synthesized to target business rules curated by domain experts -- both augmented with reasoning traces. This training objective preserves Stage 1 MCQ and QA probing accuracy while enabling single-beam greedy decoding at production latency. Evaluated on a combined enterprise catalog of 8,300+ tools across two enterprise product lines, TRACE training for Stage 2 not only preserves but improves tool understanding: MCQ accuracy gains +3.2 pp and QA probing gains +9 pp over Stage 1. On retrieval, TRACE achieves ~86% recall on Domain A and ~60% on Domain B -- compared to embedding baseline performance of ~27% & ~52% -- both with single-beam greedy decoding, making it directly deployable at production latency.
  </details>

- **[SupplyNetPy: An Open-Source Python Library for High-Fidelity Modeling and Simulation of Arbitrary Supply Chain and Inventory Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09745)**  `arXiv:2607.09745`  
  _Tushar Lone, Neha Karanjkar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper introduces SupplyNetPy, an open-source, well-documented Python library for modeling and discrete-event simulation of supply chain networks with arbitrary multi-echelon structures. It supports multiple replenishment policies, perishable inventory, node disruptions, and stochastic demand and lead times. All components are extensible via inheritance. Users describe a supply chain as a graph with node and link attributes, while the library handles simulation, providing logs and extensive node and network level performance reports. This paper presents the motivation, design, key features, and architecture of SupplyNetPy, along with detailed validation results (against analytical benchmarks, a commercial tool, and a published case study). A key motivation behind SupplyNetPy's development is programmatic generation and simulation of complex models, enabling design-space exploration, what-if analysis, training data generation, and supply chain digital twins.
  </details>

- **[Forethought: Verifiable Reasoning from Neurosymbolic Primitive Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04096)**  `arXiv:2607.04096`  
  _Vishvesh Bhat, Emmanuel Anaya Gonzalez, Jay Vaghasiya, Asad Aali_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern agentic workflows decompose user requests into sequences of tool calls with correctly resolved parameters, the results of which are processed through reasoning traces in the language model's context window. The prevailing route to improving such reasoning is test-time scaling, which trains models to search over long chains of thought; but the resulting capability is entangled in model weights, cannot be checked step by step, and is costly at inference. We present Forethought, a neurosymbolic reasoning system built from a library of symbolic and neural primitives composed through a typed domain-specific language. The results are reasoning programs: concrete representations of the model's work, in which every step carries an output contract that is checked at design time and enforced at execution time. Symbolic steps hold by construction, while learned steps are contract-checked and empirically validated, giving an explicit boundary between what is guaranteed and what is measured. Instantiated as a tool-calling execution kernel and evaluated on four standard tool-calling benchmark suites, Forethought improves end-to-end accuracy by a median of 32% relative to prompting the same base model directly, and reaches the highest aggregate accuracy among the evaluated configurations, though only by a margin within single-run noise over a frontier model. Systems pairing small open models with Forethought's fixed small-model layer match that frontier model, and a pipeline with a non-reasoning responder outperforms its reasoning sibling while requiring only a single fine-tuning stage. Removing the contract-and-control layer erases most of these gains, showing that checked structure, not decomposition alone, drives the improvement. Our results show the potential of rich symbolic orchestrators for building language-model agents that are more accurate, reliable, and auditable.
  </details>

- **[Towards end-to-end LLM-based censoring-aware survival analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25399)**  `arXiv:2605.25399`  
  _Yishu Wei, Hexin Dong, Yi Lin, Jiahe Qian, Yi Liu, Yifan Peng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Objective: Survival analysis is central to medical prediction, yet large language models (LLMs) are rarely used as end-to-end survival models because censoring prevents straightforward supervised fine-tuning. Here we present LLMSurvival, a framework that enables censoring-aware survival analysis with unmodified LLMs operating directly on tabular clinical data.Materials and Methods: LLMSurvival reformulates time-to-event prediction as pairwise ranking among comparable subjects, and derives test-time risk by aggregating comparisons against anchor individuals from the training cohort.Results: Across two clinical tasks (ICU mortality prediction in MIMIC-IV and fragility fracture prediction in a NewYork-Presbyterian/Weill Cornell Medicine cohort), LLMSurvival improves overall concordance over Cox proportional hazards modeling by 3.1% for ICU mortality and 0.5% for fracture risk, 2.1% on average for ICU mortality and 2.8% for fracture risk over three established deep learning survival models.Discussion: The results show that survival modeling with censoring can be made compatible with LLM fine-tuning through comparison-based reformulation. The framework demonstrates high portability and superior performance over expert curated scores like SAPS-II and FRAX scores across diverse clinical context. Furthermore, the framework supports local deployment, as compact, publicly available base models provide sufficient performance.Conclusion: The LLMSurvival framework serves as a proof of concept for an integrated, censoring-conscious approach to survival analysis via LLMs.
  </details>

- **[Differentiable Learning of Lifted Action Schemas for Classical Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13282)**  `arXiv:2605.13282`  
  _Jonas Reiter, Jakob Elias Gebler, Hector Geffner_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Classical planners can effectively solve very large deterministic MDPs represented in STRIPS or PDDL where states are sets of atoms over objects and relations, and lifted action schemas add or delete these atoms. This compact representation yields strong search heuristics and provides an ideal setting for structural generalization, since lifted relations and action schemas give rise to infinitely many domain instances.
