  Classical Bundle Adjustment (BA) relies on precise metric initialization and known camera intrinsics, and traditional Structure-from-Motion (SfM) pipelines struggle to exploit modern dense matchers because rigid track-building heuristics break down under their inherent noise. We present Track-Free Dense Structure from Motion (TFD-SfM), which jointly optimizes camera extrinsics, focal lengths, and geometry from a strict cold start. Our central idea is to relax the rigidities of classical SfM into soft, probabilistic decisions under Negative Log-Likelihood (NLL) objectives. Instead of forcing matched pixels onto a single shared 3D point, we model each match as a 3D Gaussian whose variance is optimized jointly with the geometry. Unreliable matches take on large variance and contribute little to the loss. This smooths the optimization landscape, so the solver converges even from identity cameras and uniform depths. A kinematic relative-pose parameterization stabilizes the cold start, and a dual-hypothesis mechanism resolves the mirror-symmetry ambiguity on object-centric scenes. Across DTU, Mip-NeRF~360, ETH3D, and IMC2021, TFD-SfM is competitive with both classical and learning-based SfM baselines and leads on ETH3D, recovering accurate camera poses, intrinsics, and dense geometry from unordered, uncalibrated image collections. Project~page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Continual-ARC: Measuring Skill Acquisition over a Working Lifetime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14505)**  `arXiv:2611.14505`  
  _Daniel Lenton_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent harnesses have emerged as a promising mechanism for continual learning, alongside test-time parametric learning. However, the current benchmarks have several limitations: they generally focus on a single applied domain, serve each instance only once, and assume a single family of learner, so memory-based and weight-based systems are rarely compared on the same scale. We propose Continual-ARC, a lifelong stream built from the three ARC-AGI editions, whose tasks need no domain knowledge. The same tasks return after dormant gaps with fresh instances, while new tasks keep arriving. On each instance, the learner may pay for demonstrations, submit answers, or act inside the harness, with retries permitted on incorrect submissions, which are paid for. Each instance is scored based on the share of this feedback budget consumed. Since a learner may keep anything between instances, memory-based and weight-based systems are scored on the same scale. After the stream, the learner is frozen and tested on fresh instances of the tasks it met, which measures memorization, and on unseen tasks, which measures generalization. We run an agent harness with frozen weights, with and without persistence, on Qwen3.5-9B and GLM-5.3 Flash, comparing them with two prompting floors, a program-induction method and Qwen3.5-9B's weights trained to imitate GLM-5.3 Flash. Persistence in the harness generally does not improve memorization unless provided with explicit expert guidance. Most harness-based methods still learn less than a floor that simply caches each task's demonstrations, across all models we tested. No harness-based method gains on unseen tasks, whereas weight-based learning shows substantial gains. This opens the door to future weight-based continual learning that could teach models to drive harnesses in new domains.
  </details>

- **[TimeSeek: Multi-Task and Multi-Domain Pretraining for Generalized Time Series Analytics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14491)**  `arXiv:2611.14491`  
  _Peng Chen, Yihang Wang, Zhongwen Rao, Yang Shu, Yuying Qiu, Yuxuan Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series analytics has been widely applied across various domains. While existing time series foundation models (TSFMs) have demonstrated promising results, they still face two major challenges: 1) Most TSFMs mainly rely on stacking Transformer layers to increase parameter size, lacking the ability to adaptively model complex time series patterns from multi-domain data. 2) Current TSFMs are primarily pre-trained for specific tasks, e.g., forecasting or anomaly detection, and a few models support multiple tasks, but they fail to fully leverage task specificity and commonality, resulting in suboptimal performance. To address these challenges, we propose TimeSeek, a novel generalized time series analytics model that adaptively models time series patterns from multi-domain data and unifies multiple tasks in pre-training. Conceptually, TimeSeek is guided by selective information sharing under domain and task heterogeneity. From the perspective of multi-domain data, we propose a Multi-Domain Feature Extractor that adaptively captures temporal dependencies and channel correlations based on domain-specific characteristics, including progressive multi-scale modeling and adaptive slot channel modeling. From the perspective of the diversity of tasks, we propose multi-task collaborative training, which leverages task specificity and task commonality through task customization and cross-task training, effectively unifying multiple tasks in pre-training and better adapting to diverse downstream scenarios. After pre-training on large-scale time series data, TimeSeek achieves state-of-the-art performance in forecasting, anomaly detection, and classification tasks across both few-shot learning and zero-shot situations, together with favorable inference efficiency.
  </details>

- **[Accurate predictions of properties of energetic materials with tabular foundation models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14462)**  `arXiv:2611.14462`  
  _Vagn Krister Nes Glomsrud, Mathias Bynke, Lars Henry Berge Olsen, Dennis Christensen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We use TabPFN, a recently developed tabular foundation model, to predict three properties of energetic materials: impact sensitivity, decomposition temperature and crystal density. Without any hyperparameter tuning, TabPFN outperforms the best published models for all three properties. It also substantially outperforms a wide range of established machine-learning models, including tuned gradient boosting (XGBoost), random forests and Gaussian processes. This holds both when the models are given hand-crafted molecular descriptors and when they are given learned representations from Chemprop's message-passing neural network. In particular, for all three properties, TabPFN applied to Chemprop's own embedding is more accurate than Chemprop's predictions. TabPFN is also among the fastest models we consider, more than an order of magnitude faster than XGBoost. As TabPFN predicts the entire distribution of the target rather than just a single value, it offers built-in uncertainty quantification, which we compare with the empirical distributions of compounds with repeated measurements. Finally, we interpret the predictions using Shapley values, which rank the features by their importance.
  </details>

- **[Quantifying the Sampling Frequency Invariance of Time-Series Foundation Models for Sensor Data Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14425)**  `arXiv:2611.14425`  
  _Sourish Gunesh Dhekane, Abinaya Kumar, Torsten Woertwein, Yan-Bo Lin, Thomas Ploetz, Shabnam Ghaffarzadegan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) are increasingly used in sensor applications, where sampling frequency (SF) varies across sensors, devices, and operating modes (e.g., power saving). Ideally, TSFMs must remain effective during such SF changes, provided the SF retains task-critical bandwidth. In this paper, we stress-test 4 prominent TSFMs: MOMENT, Chronos-2, Moirai-MoE, and TimesFM, to quantify their SF-invariance and investigate whether their behavior tracks the resulting loss of information bandwidth rather than the SF value itself. Our stress-test probes 3 dimensions: (i) the shift in embeddings, (ii) the degradation in forecasting performance, and (iii) the degradation in downstream performance, quantified by three novel metrics: FSI, FDI, and CDI, respectively. To our knowledge, this is the first study to place SF-invariance at the core of TSFM evaluations. Our results characterize: (i) how SF-invariance depends on the margin between signal's native-SF and the bandwidth its task requires, (ii) how much of that invariance the choice of TSFM explains, and (iii) how much of TSFM's performance degradation under a lower SF reflects information loss rather than a limitation of their abilities. These findings reframe SF-invariance as a measurable, signal-aware property of the (signal X TSFM) pair, and guide practitioners on when TSFMs display SF-invariance.
  </details>

- **[DnNP: Denoising Input Uncertainty in Neural Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14424)**  `arXiv:2611.14424`  
  _Fatemeh Tohidian, Chengzhi Shi, Soomi Lee, Matthew Elia, Amy Mueller, Stratis Ioannidis, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural Processes (NPs) are flexible meta-learning models capable of uncertainty quantification across diverse applications. However, the standard NP framework assumes access to noise-free inputs. This is unrealistic in many practical settings, where sensor noise or measurement errors are unavoidable. This discrepancy between model assumptions and real-world data can significantly degrade prediction quality and uncertainty estimates. We introduce Denoising Neural Processes (DnNPs), a principled extension of the NP framework that explicitly models input uncertainty. We train DnNPs via variational inference. DnNPs jointly learn to denoise corrupted inputs and perform meta-learning, making them robust to noisy training data. We demonstrate DnNPs' effectiveness on both synthetic and real-world datasets, showing consistent improvements in prediction and uncertainty quantification compared to standard NPs. On PM2.5 spatial interpolation from real-world air quality sensors, DnNP reduces RMSE by up to 30% and NLL by up to 73% relative to standard NPs. We also provide theoretical grounding: we show that the Bayes-optimal predictor under noisy inputs belongs to the DnNP model class, and that plug-in models with fixed observation variance incur an irreducible KL divergence gap that DnNP provably avoids.
  </details>

- **[Causal Concept Explanations for Deep Neural Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14407)**  `arXiv:2611.14407`  
  _Josh Rountree, Pulkit Verma, Oswin So, Chuchu Fan, Julie A. Shah_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Concept-based explanations in neural models are important for the development, certification, and adoption of autonomous systems. Yet, interpretability remains correlational. Without methods to guide and quantify causal relationships between explanations, a concept explanation can be provided without being necessary or sufficient for the observed output thereby appearing correct while misleading. We introduce Causal Concept Wrapper Network (CCW-Net), the first use of interventional mediation analysis from fields such as economics, medicine, and epidemiology as a training objective to align causal effects of concept-based explanations with a model's output. We further apply mediation analysis as an evaluation tool to quantify, per concept label combination, how much of any model's output is causally attributable to each concept explanation. As a training objective, CCW-Net shapes a model's concept representations such that they are both necessary and sufficient for the outputs they are credited with and independent of other concepts' contributions. Counterfactual concept representations anchor a set of causal alignment criteria: necessity, sufficiency, and independence. Necessity drives irrelevant concept effects toward zero, sufficiency ensures relevant concept representations fully reconstruct a model's output, and independence enforces the total effect decomposes linearly across concepts. We evaluate CCW-Net across aircraft control, driving, and image classification. CCW-Net provides a step toward concept-based explanations that are not merely interpretable, but quantifiably causal, supporting safer evaluation, certification, and deployment of transparent and trustworthy neural models.
  </details>

- **[A Margin Perspective on LoRA: Robustness to Catastrophic Forgetting and Adapter Merging (MaLoRA)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14397)**  `arXiv:2611.14397`  
  _Ziqing Xu, Hancheng Min, Lachlan Ewen MacDonald, Salma Tarmoun, Enrique Mallada, Weijie Su, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) is the de facto method for parameter-efficient fine-tuning of large neural networks, achieving strong task adaptation with minimal trainable parameters and low optimization cost. Beyond this efficiency, practitioners have observed two striking properties: \textit{robustness to catastrophic forgetting} and the ability to \textit{merge independently trained adapters} into a single model that performs competitively across multiple tasks. These properties are central to continual and multi-task learning, yet remain poorly understood. In this work, we provide a theoretical explanation through a unified \textit{margin-based perspective}. We analyze LoRA in multiclass linear classification under a near-orthogonal task regime with $\ell_2$-regularization, and characterize the optimal LoRA adapter across regularization regimes. Our analysis shows that, in an intermediate regime of regularization parameters, the optimal adapter aligns with the \emph{max-margin solution} on the fine-tuning data. Building on this characterization, we derive two key consequences. First, we obtain closed-form expressions for the margins on pre-training and fine-tuning data, revealing a precise margin trade-off: the regularization parameter controls the balance between retention and adaptation. Second, we analyze adapter merging, proving that merged models achieve positive margin on each task and deriving optimal mixing coefficients that balance margins across tasks and maximize the margin over their union. These results lead to a simple, training-free merging rule, which we term \textit{margin-based LoRA merging} (MaLoRA). Experiments on modern architectures and real datasets validate our theoretical predictions, showing that MaLoRA matches or outperforms several adapter-merging baselines across a range of vision and language classification tasks.
  </details>

- **[The Unused Input: The Initial State as a Conditioning Interface for Recurrent TSFMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14357)**  `arXiv:2611.14357`  
  _Bernhard Voggenberger, Christian Ganhör, Levente Zólyomi, Sebastian Böck, Sepp Hochreiter_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series foundation models (TSFMs) forecast unseen series without task-specific training. Increasingly they are built on recurrent backbones, whose constant per-step cost makes long-context inference cheap, and which, unlike transformers, carry a state. A recurrent backbone computes its prediction from two inputs: the observed context window and the initial recurrent state. In practice only the first is utilised and the initial state is unused by convention. We ask whether the initial state can be utilised in recurrent TSFMs. Using the xLSTM-based zero-shot forecaster TiRex as a case study, we freeze the backbone and train only a small head that writes a context vector into the initial state, so that any change in accuracy is attributable to the injected state rather than to adapted weights. A spectral summary of the input window, learned once and applied unchanged to every series, lowers mean GiftEval MASE by 0.175%, with every seed improving, which neither a parameter-matched LoRA adapter nor a learnable static state reproduces. An embedding of text attached to each series, evaluated on six MoTime datasets, reveals two roles for the state. As a per-dataset adaptation surface, a static state lowers MASE by 0.04-6.96% of the zero-shot baseline (8-70% of the gain of full fine-tuning). As a channel for exogenous information, the text-conditioned state lowers it by a further 0.15-0.41% of the zero-shot baseline on four of six datasets and, combined with a LoRA adapter, exceeds full fine-tuning, which has no access to the text, on one of them. In TiRex, the effect concentrates in the mLSTM blocks and is strongest at short context. The results are preliminary and the improvements small, but they point to the initial state as a direction for further research.
  </details>

- **[Auditing the Judge: Internal Signals for LLM Reasoning Assurance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14323)**  `arXiv:2611.14323`  
  _Mayank Gulati, Gerhard Wunder_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language-model systems use self-judgment to decide which answers to release, review, or recompute. Because intervention is costly, an assurance score must rank errors well at the available budget, not merely over the full ranking. We compare the judge's continuous verdict-token score with a supervised correctness probe trained on attention-head activations from the same prompt. Exact identities link fixed-budget error recall to residual risk and show that AUROC averages recall over all budgets. Across three open 7-12B models on GSM8K and MATH, the probe finds 1.3-22.5 additional percentage points of errors at 5% review in all six model-dataset settings (median 7.4 points). At 10% routing, probe-guided allocation improves accuracy over equal-cost random allocation in both GSM8K experiments, but beats judge-guided routing only for the weaker judge. Cross-dataset transfer lowers probe AUROC for every model in both directions relative to target-domain training. Because dataset, weight precision, and generation budget change together, this measures a joint protocol shift rather than a dataset-only effect. Internal signals can improve supervised, in-domain budget allocation, but are not domain-general confidence estimators.
  </details>

- **[Federated Learning for Cross-Regional Epidemic Forecasting with Asynchronous Outbreaks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14272)**  `arXiv:2611.14272`  
  _Ruibin Liu, Francisco Fernandes, Xuanfeng Li, Zige Liu, André Salgado, Chitin Hon, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Objective: Epidemic outbreaks are often asynchronous across regions, yet collaborative forecasting is hampered by privacy laws and heterogeneous public health interventions. We developed and evaluated, as a proof of concept, a federated learning (FL) framework for cross-regional COVID-19 forecasting under asynchronous outbreaks among Guangdong, Macau, and Portugal, while maintaining data sovereignty.Methods: Using daily case data from WHO and China CDC for the three regions, whose outbreaks were asynchronous and interventions heterogeneous, we trained Long Short-Term Memory networks via FL for short-term forecasting, benchmarked against localized and centralized training using Root Mean Square Error, Mean Absolute Error, and Symmetric Mean Absolute Percentage Error. All experiments used the Flower FL framework with consistent hyperparameters across sites. Publicly available, aggregated case-count data were used so that the centralized benchmark could be computed as a reference.Results: The dataset comprised 1,233 daily observations (411 days) of new confirmed COVID-19 cases from November 24, 2021, to January 8, 2023. The earlier Omicron wave in Portugal supplied transmission dynamics that improved forecasts in the later-onset regions of Macau and Guangdong. The FL model achieved a normalized Root Mean Square Error of 0.028, an 88.3% improvement over the local model (0.240) and comparable to the centralized model, with network overhead mainly determined by model size and aggregation rounds.Conclusion: FL achieved short-term forecasting accuracy close to centralized training while maintaining data residency, with acceptable overhead. Leveraging asynchronous outbreaks across regions may offer a practical path to privacy-preserving collaborative forecasting, though prospective validation is required.
  </details>

- **[SinkState: On-the-Fly SVD of Recurrent States for Efficient Hybrid LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14246)**  `arXiv:2611.14246`  
  _Haolin Jiang, Weihang Li, Zhongzhu Zhou, Jinwen Pan, Santosh Pandey, Hang Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hybrid models combine softmax attention with linear attention, which compresses history into fixed-size recurrent states to balance model quality and inference efficiency. However, linear attention has not pushed this compression to its limit: we find that the queries, keys, and values of linear layers concentrate in a stable, low-dimensional subspace, a phenomenon we call the linear attention sink, so each state is effectively low-rank, with a rank that varies across states. Yet existing serving systems keep these redundant states dense, reading and rewriting them in full at every decoding step, so their memory traffic grows with the batch size and dominates the decoding cost. Thus SinkState stores each recurrent state in an adaptive low-rank form throughout inference with buffered updates and a local SVD restricted to the top subspace. SinkState further consolidates the remaining components asynchronously on the CPU. On four hybrid models with Mamba-2 and Gated DeltaNet (GDN) layers, from 4B to 120B parameters, SinkState reduces the peak memory of recurrent states by up to 2.6x (1.9x on average) and speeds up decoding by up to 1.58x (1.25x on average) over Mamba-SSM and FLA, while maintaining accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Tree-Ising: Repairing Language Models with Bit-Level Ising Edits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14210)**  `arXiv:2611.14210`  
  _Hadi Salloum, Ammar Ali_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Weight-space programming, introduced in recent MIT work by Takehi et al., enables a frontier LLM agent to read a network's weights and write a repair. Their GPT-6 Astra agent corrected the reasoning loops of Qwen3.5-0.8B without gradients, using 158 agent turns, 89.7 million tokens, and 50 forward-pass requests. Our method, Tree-Ising, casts this repair as a sparse discrete optimization problem over the model's own BF16 weights. Forward statistics define an objective that weakens a loop direction, strengthens a concluding direction, and preserves the layer's output on productive tokens. Within a state-tree representation, where lossless BF16 storage acts as a special case, the admissible edits are sign flips and exponent shifts that preserve all shared group exponents and mantissas. We decompose this objective exactly into independent QUBO (Ising) problems, one per output row, which we solve on a GPU using simulated annealing or quantum-inspired simulated bifurcation. On the agent's two layers, Tree-Ising achieves 54.6% on MATH-500, outperforming the agent's edit (51.7%) under the same evaluation protocol, while the base model scores 7.9% as reported. The simulated bifurcation solver yields a statistically indistinguishable repair at 55.1%. Both solvers outperform the agent edit across all five math benchmarks, with statistically significant improvements on MATH-500 and GSM8K after correcting for multiple comparisons. Our approach modifies 13% to 17% fewer weights and reduces BF16 bit flips by a factor of 2.2 to 2.3. It requires only four forward-pass requests and zero LLM tokens, with the annealing recipe costing about $0.90 in GPU time. On Qwen3.5-2B, with layers and dose selected using only the base model, the same recipe increases greedy GSM8K accuracy from 21.6% to 69.4%. As a proof of concept, we execute an eight-variable repair subproblem using QAOA on an IBM Heron processor.
  </details>

- **[Unipolar Neuron Networks Trained by Recirculation for Reward-Based Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14153)**  `arXiv:2611.14153`  
  _Florin Leon_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unipolar neurons are the predominant type of nerve cell in the octopus and in most other invertebrates. A single neurite (a fiber extending from the cell body) branches into a tree that carries both input and output synapses. The branches send graded local outputs to nearby cells, and one site on the main neurite sends a global output to distant targets. This paper proposes a network of such neurons and a learning rule for it. Each neuron is modeled as a tree of passive segments, whose activations are computed exactly as the steady state of a linear system. The network learns from reward alone, with a rule called recirculation. After each forward pass, a second pass sends requested changes of the outputs back through the same connections. Each synapse then adjusts its weight using only quantities available at its own location. Because the system of each neuron is symmetric, this update closely follows the gradient of the error in the predicted reward. Two mechanisms limit forgetting when tasks are learned in sequence: a task code inhibits a different subset of branches in each task, while consolidation lowers the learning rate of synapses that changed strongly in the recent past. The model is evaluated on a sequence of MNIST and Iris tasks. The network maintains high accuracy with low forgetting, whereas gradient-based training of the same network forgets earlier tasks almost completely.
  </details>

- **[Deviation-Aware Pretraining for Cardiovascular Disease Detection from Electrocardiograms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14141)**  `arXiv:2611.14141`  
  _Siyuan Yan, Haibo Zheng, Feng Lu, Wei Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electrocardiogram (ECG) signals are critical for cardiovascular disease (CVD) detection. Most current deep learning models rely on supervised, task-specific training, which limits their ability to generalize across diseases, patient populations, and acquisition settings. This paper presents a self-supervised ECG feature extraction framework that learns from nearly one million unlabeled 12-lead ECG recordings. The framework includes two modules: Deviation Localization Embedding (DLE), which detects waveform-level deviations from normal patterns, and Association Reconstruction Embedding (ARE), which models cross-lead associations through deviation-guided reconstruction to capture abnormal patterns expressed across multiple leads. These modules enable the framework to capture subtle deviation-related patterns and their cross-lead context. The framework achieves up to 6.5% AUROC and 12.8% F1 improvements over prior methods on multiple public datasets, even without additional fine-tuning the pretrained feature extractor. Embedding visualizations show that the learned features support meaningful disease grouping and anomaly identification. The approach reduces the need for labeled data and can support clinicians in diverse healthcare settings. The framework has potential to improve early detection and monitoring of cardiovascular diseases, contributing to more accessible and equitable heart care.
  </details>

- **[HaiRuo: Forecasting Ocean Eddies as Fields and Objects with a Satellite-Altimetry Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14136)**  `arXiv:2611.14136`  
  _Xukun Qin, Haochen Sun, Yuyang Tian, Ge Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mesoscale eddies carry most of the ocean's kinetic energy and move heat, salt and nutrients across basins, yet where an eddy will be two weeks ahead is still read off ocean models with post-hoc tracking heuristics. We introduce HaiRuo, which forecasts eddies as fields and as objects at once: from 30 days of satellite sea-level maps, one forward pass returns 15 days of sea level and geostrophic velocity, daily eddy masks and the trajectory of every eddy alive today. HaiRuo pairs two components with a clear division of labour: (1) HaiRuo-1B, an open-weight 1.05-billion-parameter foundation model pretrained without labels on 24 years of global satellite-altimetry observations, which supplies the 15-day physical fields and masks (+0.14 field skill and +1.1 mIoU points under the same decoder) and further lowers 15-day centre error by 1.2 km frozen (1.35 km over training from scratch); and (2) XuanJi, a Lagrangian object decoder that samples the evolving forecast in each eddy's co-moving polar frame and refines the 15-day path across three look-ahead rounds, removing 2.5 km on HaiRuo-1B (2.9 km with joint fine-tuning) and 2.5-3.3 km via its object attention across six published backbones. Under one pre-registered global evaluation over the whole ocean, HaiRuo improves on fourteen methods on every endpoint (25.2 vs. 30.6 km 15-day centre error, -18%), holding its margin outside the labelled regions and through both phases of the El Nino-Southern Oscillation (ENSO), while reproducing westward drift, 50-60 deg S eastward reversal and poleward cyclone-anticyclone deflection. Over ten days through one fixed tracker, HaiRuo's forecast field cuts the eddy-centre error of WenHai and XiHe by 37-43% (-2.8 km vs. day 0 while they degrade +4.3-8.7 km), and its object trajectories cut pipeline error by 77-78% globally. Open-weight checkpoints, code and project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Right for the Wrong Reasons? Evaluating Plausibility and Behavioral Change in Explanation-Guided Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14134)**  `arXiv:2611.14134`  
  _David Dembinsky, Adriano Lucieri, Andreas Dengel_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Explanation-Guided Learning (EGL) promises models that are not only accurate but also rely on task-relevant evidence. However, whether improved agreement between model explanations and human-provided rationales also implies a changed decision strategy remains unclear. We study this tension under controlled synthetic shortcut introduction using a constructed shortcut dataset. Inspired by real-world applications, our benchmark covers shortcuts of different kinds, including localized, background-based, and global cues. This allows us to evaluate whether different EGL objectives produce behavioral changesand explanatory changes. Across these axes, we find that the effects of EGL are strongly objective-specific. Rationale-based data augmentation provides the clearest robustness gains when shortcut evidence can be separated from task-relevant foreground information, reaching average accuracy improvements of up to +4.8 percentage points (pp) together with increased foreground reliance. Attribution-constraining objectives primarily improve explanation plausibility, increasing Importance Mass by up to +14 pp on average, but provide weaker and less consistent behavioral correction, with average accuracy gains of at most +1.6 pp. Moreover, neither improved robustness nor improved plausibility reliably implies higher perturbation-based fidelity. Overall, our results show that EGL can be useful, but its success cannot be inferred from rationale agreement alone and should instead be evaluated along complementary behavioral and explanation-level axes. We publicly release all experimental results to enable further analysis (this https URL).
  </details>

- **[Hierarchical Graph Representation Learning with Pooling-Induced Substructures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14126)**  `arXiv:2611.14126`  
  _Luca Sbicego, Xiaowen Dong, Dorina Thanou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hierarchical graph-level tasks that require reasoning over substructures that extend beyond local neighborhoods are posing challenges for standard Graph Neural Networks (GNNs). While graph pooling aims to address this by coarsening graphs into hierarchical representations, existing methods often fail to explicitly preserve the substructures they induce, leading to inconsistent empirical performance. Moreover, evaluating such approaches remains challenging due to the lack of benchmarks with explicit hierarchical structure. We propose Pooled-Substructure-Aware GNNs, an approach to graph pooling that explicitly preserves and processes substructures uncovered by structure-based pooling schemes. We show that this design enhances the expressive power of pooling-based GNNs in the sense of the Weisfeiler-Lehman (WL) test. To enable principled evaluation, we introduce two new synthetic datasets with controllable hierarchical structure and propose a metric to characterize the prevalence of hierarchical patterns in real-world graph-level benchmarks. On tasks with relevant hierarchical structure, our approach outperforms matched pooling baselines, while remaining competitive on structurally simpler benchmarks. Code and datasets are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Opportunity-Cost Option-Critic: Adaptive Switching Costs for Robust Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14099)**  `arXiv:2611.14099`  
  _Khaled Ammoura, Aya Kayal, Naseem Daher_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deliberation costs are commonly used in hierarchical reinforcement learning (HRL) to prevent options from collapsing to primitive actions. Existing approaches rely on fixed penalties or duration-based adaptations, both of which are indirect proxies for an option's utility. This paper argues that option persistence should instead depend on the opportunity cost of abandoning the current behavior. We propose the Opportunity-Cost Option-Critic ($OC^2$) framework, which defines a state-dependent deliberation cost using the positive value gap between the active option and its best alternative. This encourages persistence when the current option is clearly preferable and allows rapid switching when a better option emerges. We show that directly learning such costs leads to a degenerate zero-cost solution and introduces an auxiliary objective that aligns the learned cost with the value-gap target. The approach is evaluated in Four Rooms and Pinball domains under stationary, goal-shift, and geometry-shift conditions. In the Pinball goal-shift task, the $OC^2$ framework achieves a 52\% first-ten-episode success rate compared with 18\% for a fixed-cost baseline. The results suggest that opportunity-cost-based persistence improves adaptation in non-stationary environments.
  </details>

- **[Generalizing Causal Decision-Making: Markov Equivalence, Transportability and Counterfactuals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14066)**  `arXiv:2611.14066`  
  _Min Woo Park, Sanghack Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Structural causal bandits use causal knowledge to guide exploration when reward mechanisms are unknown. This paper extends the approach to partially known causal structure, information from other environments, and realizable counterfactual actions. First, we develop graphical criteria for minimal intervention sets under Markov equivalence and characterize possibly-optimal minimal intervention sets for maximal ancestral graphs. A search algorithm finds sets that are possibly optimal in at least one graph represented by a partial ancestral graph. Second, we derive target reward bounds from source distributions through partial transportability, accounting for non-manipulable variables. Dominance relations provide further bounds for action pruning and restricted exploration, with a regret guarantee under valid prior bounds. Third, we formulate counterfactual structural causal bandits over actions supported by specified experimental capabilities. Graphical characterizations of minimality and possible optimality yield algorithms that remove equivalent or dominated choices while preserving an optimal action. Experiments with specified structural causal models examine how these reductions and transferred bounds affect learning. Together, these results clarify how causal knowledge and experimental capabilities determine which actions merit exploration and guide learning within the retained action space.
  </details>

- **[Structured Confidence Perturbation via Fisher Geometry for Adversarial Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14046)**  `arXiv:2611.14046`  
  _Chenlong Wei, Hanling Wang, Chong Zhang, Qinkai Yu, Fangyu Wu, Xiaobo Jin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adversarial training is often plagued by "pseudo-attacks", perturbations that significantly increase the loss value but fail to truly alter the model's confidence structure. To address this, we propose two complementary approaches to directly attack and defend against confidence structure deformation. First, building upon the traditional loss maximization perspective, we introduce a hybrid objective that integrates cross-entropy and KL divergence, encouraging adversarial examples to simultaneously disrupt prediction accuracy and distribution consistency. Second, we geometrically reconstruct adversarial training by generating perturbations that maximize the distribution distance on the confidence manifold. Theoretical analysis shows that the optimal perturbation direction aligns with the principal eigenvectors of the Fisher information matrix. We also propose an efficient algorithm to avoid high-dimensional matrix computations. Experiments on multiple datasets and model architectures demonstrate that our method outperforms existing methods in adversarial robustness and robust transferability, particularly excelling in structural perturbations within high-confidence regions.
  </details>

- **[Margin-Guided Token Credit for Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14015)**  `arXiv:2611.14015`  
  _Chi Liu, Xin Chen, Yuchuan Weng, Srinivasan Manoharan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, a language model is rewarded for a correct final answer, and GRPO passes that reward to every token of the solution in equal measure. We instead place the credit where the model hesitated, in a method we call MCPO (Margin-Credit Policy Optimization). We measure hesitation by the top-2 margin $M=p_1-p_2$ of the policy's next-token distribution, map it to a score with two fixed thresholds, and spread each solution's advantage over its tokens so that the mean stays unchanged. The margin is read from a forward pass the trainer already performs, so the method needs no extra model evaluation and no batch statistics. We train MiniCPM5-2B-Base with LoRA for 200 steps on 2,048 DAPO-Math problems and evaluate on six competition-math benchmarks. MCPO beats an unmodified GRPO baseline for all five data seeds, by 6.55 points of macro avg@32 on average (95\% CI $[4.1, 9.0]$), and a Qwen3-4B-Base replication improves for all three seeds ($+4.75$). The gain is largest on problems that GRPO solves only some of the time and grows with $k$ in pass@$k$, reaching 13.8 points at pass@32. MCPO models also write two to three times longer answers, and runs that write longer score higher; our experiments do not separate the two effects.
  </details>

- **[BioXArena-Pro: a research-grade benchmark for autonomous biomedical machine learning agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13994)**  `arXiv:2611.13994`  
  _Loka Li, Duzhen Zhang, Xingbo Du, Leonard Song, Kun Zhang, Le Song_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents are increasingly proposed as autonomous builders of biomedical models, but existing benchmarks are small, near saturation or open to leaked information. We present BioXArena-Pro, 64 research-level modelling tasks from eight biomedical domains, each rebuilt from a published study; most hold out whole biological units such as species or cohorts. Across 22 agent configurations, the best scores 55.1 of 100, and the scaffold matters almost as much as the model. We re-ran a published method for every task: where the published recipe applies unchanged, the best agent and the methods tie; on rebuilt tasks the methods trail by 8.2 points, suggesting that agents gain by adapting to new data. Pretrained models (including biological foundation models) matter where the strongest agent uses them: forbidding them decreases its score on those tasks by 9 points. Every task was audited in several AI-assisted rounds and a final human review.
  </details>

- **[Temporal Behavior Trees for Reinforcement Learning: Specifications as Rewards, Curricula, and Diagnostics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13993)**  `arXiv:2611.13993`  
  _Vladimir Krsmanovic, Bernd Finkbeiner_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning from formal specifications often separates task definition, reward shaping, curriculum design, and policy diagnostics into different mathematical objects. This fragmentation requires additional conversion steps and discards structure already present in the specification, such as subtask boundaries, ordering constraints, and quantitative measures. We introduce TBTCL (Temporal Behavior Tree Centric Learning), a framework that uses Temporal Behavior Trees (TBTs) as a unified interface for specification-guided reinforcement learning. A single TBT specifies the task, induces dense robustness-based rewards, defines a curriculum, and supports better understanding of learned policies. By adapting TBT syntax and semantics for specification-guided reinforcement learning, we provide a formalism that is more expressive than popular specification languages such as LTL and inherently hierarchical. We present algorithms to automatically extract a curriculum DAG and derive dense, robustness-based rewards directly from TBT semantics. Furthermore, we utilize TBT trace segmentation to build understandable competence profiles and automatically adapt the reward toward bottlenecks. We evaluate TBTCL on six specifications across discrete and continuous domains and show that Temporal Behavior Trees can serve as a specification, a reward, a curriculum, and a diagnostic.
  </details>

- **[RB-LDC: Redundancy-Balanced Latent Coding for Noise-Robust Diffusion Latents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13955)**  `arXiv:2611.13955`  
  _Hyunseok Jeong, Jaeho Jeon, Young-Sik Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent diffusion models decode approximately correct latents, making decoder sensitivity to perturbations relevant to image quality. After whitening, forward diffusion corrupts every direction at the same signal-to-noise ratio (SNR), despite unequal decoder sensitivities. We study an invertible linear layer between the encoder and diffusion process that shifts each direction's log-SNR. Its linear minimum mean-square-error (LMMSE) recovery risk has an exact spectral expression for any whitened latent distribution. Under an energy budget and a floor ensuring invertibility, minimizing decoder-sensitivity-weighted LMMSE risk integrated over the diffusion schedule yields a unique optimal allocation. The layer aligns with the decoder's expected pullback metric and equalizes marginal risk reduction across directions, computed by a deterministic spectral solver. At large budgets, the allocation follows a power law in sensitivity whose exponent is determined by the low-SNR tail of the loss weighting: one half for noise prediction and two thirds for data prediction under the cosine schedule, with a crossover on truncated schedules. We instantiate this principle as RB-LDC using Jacobian-based sensitivity estimates. On ImageNet at 256 x 256 resolution with SD-VAE and VA-VAE, trained RB-LDC improves reconstruction from noisy latents over budget-matched isotropic coding in all four metrics and five coding configurations, averaged over 15 SNRs and three pipeline seeds. It reduces average perturbed FID by 17.1 for VA-VAE block-local coding while preserving clean reconstruction. We also report intermediate SNRs where perturbed FID worsens. With a frozen diffusion model, RB-LDC yields lower FID point estimates in all 12 tested combinations of sampling progress and channel SNR under corruption during sampling.
  </details>

- **[Chem World: A Large-Scale Benchmark and Physics-Aware Framework for Trustworthy Molecular Property Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13945)**  `arXiv:2611.13945`  
  _Tianyou Bai, Huan Wang, Mingchen Gao, Tongrui Yang, Fangyue Lin, Pinze Ren, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Molecular property prediction is fundamental to computational chemistry and drug formulation research, yet existing benchmarks remain fragmented across datasets, properties, and evaluation protocols, limiting systematic evaluation of model generalization. We introduce Chem World, a large-scale benchmark integrating 17 public datasets with over 800,000 molecular and chemical-system instances across 10 property tracks, covering single-molecule and multi-component systems, including drug solubility. Chem World provides a unified testbed for evaluating molecular representations and compositional modeling across diverse chemical properties. Building on the benchmark, we propose PACM (Physics-Aware Compositional Modeling), which combines molecular representations with composition-aware modeling and domain-specific physical constraints. Extensive experiments show that molecular language models provide strong and transferable representations, while PACM consistently improves prediction across diverse chemical systems. Chem World and our PACM-powered drug-property prediction Agent support generalizable chemical modeling and offer a foundation for AI-assisted biomedical research and drug formulation development.
  </details>

- **[PARDIG: Global Precipitation Estimates Learned from Sparse Satellite Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13941)**  `arXiv:2611.13941`  
  _Guy Shalev, Stephan Rasp, Boris Babenko, Andrew El-Kadi, Mohammed Alewi Hassen, Samier Merchant, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Estimating precipitation at the global scale is vital for Earth system science and disaster response, yet observational coverage and measurement precision remain constrained. Existing solutions provide options that trade off desirable traits such as skill, latency, geographical coverage, temporal coverage, and spatial and temporal resolution. We present PARDIG, an artificial intelligence framework based on a spherical graph-mesh transformer architecture that produces near-real-time global precipitation estimates. PARDIG is trained on dense multi-modal inputs, including atmospheric state analyses and multi-spectral geostationary satellite imagery, to predict sparse, high-precision precipitation measurements from spaceborne radar. At inference, the trained model is applied densely across the globe at $0.1^\circ$ spatial and 15-minute temporal resolution, producing complete near-real-time precipitation fields from 2016 to the present, alongside a historical variant extending the record back to 1999. Evaluation against three independent radar networks (over the US, Europe, and Japan) and against over 36,000 surface stations worldwide shows that PARDIG consistently outperforms IMERG V07 (Final and Early), MSWEP V3, ERA5, and HRES across precipitation thresholds and regions, with the largest improvements at extreme intensities. Case studies of extreme precipitation events demonstrate that PARDIG successfully captures their spatial structure and timing. The improvements also extend to forecasting: WeatherNext 3, a global weather model, produces more skillful precipitation forecasts when trained to predict PARDIG. Both the historical and near-real-time PARDIG datasets are openly available under a CC BY 4.0 license.
  </details>

- **[Dual-Core Tensor Wheel Decomposition with Structure-Cached Acceleration for Tensor Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13938)**  `arXiv:2611.13938`  
  _Wenchao Xie, Qingsong Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tensor wheel decomposition captures complex correlations in high-dimensional data through interactions between outer-ring factors and a high-order central core. However, updating these components requires the explicit construction and manipulation of large-scale matrices, which limits computational efficiency and incurs particularly high computational and memory costs for the central-core update. To address these limitations, we propose dual-core tensor wheel decomposition (DCTW), which factorizes the central core into two lower-order cores linked by a shared latent bond. Each core is coupled to a different group of outer-ring factors. We further propose a structure-cached acceleration strategy that avoids explicit large-scale matrix multi- plications and caches intermediate tensors generated during tensor network contractions for reuse across block updates. This reuse reduces redundant computation and avoids the explicit construction of large matrices. Complexity analysis and experiments show that the strategy based on structural caching accelerates optimization compared with explicit and implicit up- date schemes while limiting the increase in peak memory as the ranks grow. Experiments on color video completion and robust denoising show that DCTW matches or outperforms representative tensor network methods in reconstruction quality, computational efficiency, and scalability.
  </details>

- **[Learning from First Discrepancies with Joint Reset and Pilot Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13913)**  `arXiv:2611.13913`  
  _Michal Čertík_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A run that stops at its first wrong decision shows the queries and their correct labels up to and including that decision, and nothing after it. We study how many such runs are needed for learning. Each run tests a hypothesis, the pilot, chosen beforehand from a fixed binary class of VC dimension at most $d$, on a fresh record of at most $D$ queries drawn from one unknown distribution that does not depend on the pilot. The terminal information of a record is shown only if the pilot makes no discrepancy, and a bounded loss, fixed in advance, is attached to the position where a hypothesis stops. If the discrepancy probabilities of members of the class have infimum at most $f$ and their risks have infimum at most $r$, one learner uses $\widetilde O((d+1)[D(f+\varepsilon)+r]/\varepsilon^2)$ runs and $\widetilde O(D(1+f/\varepsilon))$ distinct pilots on every history. It returns a member of the class and an interval of width at most $\varepsilon$ containing, with probability at least $1-\delta$, both the output's risk and the optimal risk. We also treat unknown ceilings and expected costs. Lower bounds for finite classes show that each order is necessary in the worst case, possibly on different instances, at positive dimension, small accuracy and fixed confidence. The learner enumerates label patterns of the class on displayed queries, possibly in exponential time; the results, under standard measurability conditions, concern statistical resources only. The proof combines a gain computable from stopped displays, exponential weights, a sparse depth-robust graph and a harmonic-mean bound.
  </details>

- **[Conformal Risk Control for QoT-Aware Lightpath Feasibility Under Technology-Driven Domain Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13893)**  `arXiv:2611.13893`  
  _Kiarash Rezaei, Omran Ayoub, Paolo Monti, Carlos Natalino_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Artificial intelligence and machine learning (AI/ML)-based regression models support operational decision-making in optical networks but typically expose only point predictions, which carry no statistically controlled information about their reliability. This paper proposes a post-hoc conformal risk control (CRC) framework that keeps a pretrained AI/ML regressor fixed, provides a finite-sample guarantee on uncertainty band miscoverage, and maps the band to operational decisions through policy rules. The guarantee applies to interval miscoverage, while decision-error rates are evaluated empirically. We instantiate the framework for quality of transmission (QoT)-aware lightpath (LP) feasibility assessment, in which a regressor predicts optical signal-to-noise ratio (OSNR) under domain shifts caused by fiber, amplifier, and switching upgrades. Feature shift contribution scores identify the LP features whose relation to QoT changes the most across a technology transition, and two policies translate the bands into feasibility decisions, allowing operators to tune the trade-off between conservatively rejecting or aggressively accepting LP realizations. Experiments on open space-division multiplexing (SDM) QoT datasets over the CONUS topology show that, for a target coverage of 90%, the uncertainty bands meet the target in all 170 trials spanning 17 domain shifts and ten calibration-test partitions, whereas a source-only quantile regression (QR) baseline meets it in none. Under severe shifts, the efficiency-oriented (EO) policy empirically improves zero-shot (ZS) feasibility-decision accuracy from about 85% to above 99%, approaching a transfer learning (TL) policy while avoiding retraining. The framework provides finite-sample control of uncertainty band miscoverage for regression-based optical network decision support, with potential applications beyond QoT-aware LP feasibility assessment.
  </details>

- **[Mechanistic Interpretability of Deep Neural Networks: Methods, Frameworks, and Perspectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13868)**  `arXiv:2611.13868`  
  _Lorenzo Cirillo, Irene Amerini, Andrea Cavallaro_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep neural networks underpin fundamental tasks in a wide range of signal domains, such as image, audio, and language processing. However, their internal mechanisms are notably difficult to interpret. Internal activations are structured signals transformed across layers that may encode a multitude of interdependent relationships, as individual neurons frequently represent multiple underlying concepts. Mechanistic interpretability aims to uncover the internal mechanisms of neural networks by identifying components whose activations are causally responsible for specific behaviors. In such a context, this article provides an overview of advances in mechanistic interpretability for the analysis of causal inference in deep neural networks.
  </details>

- **[Where to Approximate in Neurosymbolic Inference?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13862)**  `arXiv:2611.13862`  
  _Samy Badreddine, Emile van Krieken, Luciano Serafini, Antonio Vergari_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Probabilistic neurosymbolic methods rely on weighted model counting (WMC) to combine neural predictors with symbolic constraints. Exact WMC is #P-hard and typically scales poorly, so many methods resort to approximations. By unifying existing approaches under a three-step bottom-up approximate compilation pipeline, we study where approximation budget is best allocated. Inspired by tensor networks, we instantiate two steps with tensor train decompositions and derive new pipelines that exactly multiply approximated factors and recompress their products via SVD- or interpolation-based schemes. Across six tasks in Sudoku, arithmetic reasoning, and scene understanding, and across increasing problem sizes, our pipelines produce WMC estimates orders-of-magnitude more accurate than prior methods. This translates into stronger downstream learning on tasks where reasoning relies on tight interactions between symbolic factors, where cruder approximations ignore these dependencies. Our pipelines open novel design space for effective approximate compilation of challenging probabilistic reasoning tasks.
  </details>

- **[JAX-WAVE: A Differentiable Physics-Supervised Framework for Inverse Wave Design in Heterogeneous Media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13826)**  `arXiv:2611.13826`  
  _Honghui Du, Binyao Guo, Yushu Pan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Designing a prescribed wave field in a heterogeneous medium requires an initial pressure distribution that compensates for the medium's propagation response. Repeated numerical inversion solves this task afresh for each target and environment. We introduce JAX-WAVE for amortized inverse wave design: a shared neural network maps desired terminal fields and known wave-speed maps directly to bounded initial pressures. Training through a differentiable finite-difference time-domain solver uses the physical design objective without requiring optimized control labels. Linear propagation and symmetric pressure bounds imply an exact sign-reversal relation for the regularized optimum. A shared-weight projection enforces this relation, preserves feasibility, and satisfies an exact identity for the paired-sign objective. We characterize the discrete propagator and its weighted adjoint to connect learning, numerical inversion, and optimality assessment. On an independent confirmation set, three trained models achieve unseen-material errors of 25.24% for geometric targets and 4.77% for reachable targets. Matched-capacity comparisons show improved reconstruction with the sign parameterization. Within a 5 ms median online budget, the learned map reduces mean error by 45% relative to accelerated numerical inversion on the heterogeneous benchmark; longer numerical solves recover greater accuracy. The results establish a reusable inverse-design procedure and show how its accuracy and computational value depend on target structure and query workload.
  </details>

- **[AnchorMTS: Multi-View Signal Representation and Forecast-Anchored Fusion for Long-Term Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13794)**  `arXiv:2611.13794`  
  _Gao Wu, Tingting Xu, Changning Wu, Tengfei Long, Yong Liu, Jiabin Jin, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term time-series forecasters typically optimize a single numerical representation, even though the same history exposes complementary geometry, recurrence, and semantic structure. We propose AnchorMTS, which derives self-aligned visual and textual views from each observed window and uses them only to refine a complete numerical forecast. A trainable evidence path produces a candidate forecast, while a parameter-free output projection enforces an exact per-variable bound on its displacement from the numerical anchor. Across six benchmarks and four horizons, AnchorMTS achieves the best macro MSE/MAE (0.336/0.367) among seven supervised baselines and wins 18 of 24 MSE settings against the cellwise baseline envelope; removing the auxiliary path raises macro MSE to 0.340. The projection leaves 95.8% of candidate refinements unchanged while yielding zero bound violations, showing that complementary multi-view evidence can improve forecasting while its final influence remains explicitly controlled.
  </details>

- **[Omni-JEV: Text and Dual-Stream Speech Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13777)**  `arXiv:2611.13777`  
  _Shengzhou Kong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A user utterance does not uniquely determine the next action in full-duplex dialogue: its consequences depend on the assistant's response state and interaction history. Speech activity, utterance completion, and interruption intent provide related evidence, whose implications for a response require an explicit action policy. We formulate this task at collected-segment endpoints and introduce Omni-JEV, which maps text or role-indexed user and assistant-reference speech to probabilities over five interaction actions. An aligned speech-to-speech understanding backbone encodes the acoustic roles separately and shares its candidate readout with text inputs. Bilingual weak supervision supports cross-entropy fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning for probability-sensitive decisions (RLCD). We compare Gaussian-logit and categorical Choice RLCD from the same supervised initialization under matched training exposure, observing different trade-offs between classification and probability quality. Gaussian Omni-JEV achieves test accuracies of 84.08% for audio and 96.55% for text. This common action interface supports decisions about when a full-duplex voice assistant should respond, preserve ongoing speech, interrupt, or resume an interrupted response.
  </details>

- **[Plug-and-Play ADMM for Inverse Problems with Flow Matching Denoiser](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13742)**  `arXiv:2611.13742`  
  _Dibyanshu Kumar, Magda Gregorova_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Plug-and-Play (PnP) methods with generative priors have achieved strong performance in inverse problems, with recent flow matching models enabling fast and scalable reconstruction. However, a key challenge in PnP methods is maintaining consistency between data fidelity updates and the learned generative prior, as data-consistency steps can move iterates away from the flow trajectory. We propose an ADMM-based PnP framework for flow matching priors that enforces stronger consistency between the measurement model and the prior through auxiliary variables and dual updates. In addition, we introduce a trajectory correction mechanism that restores consistency with the flow via a combination of deterministic and stochastic projections. Our method provides a more principled integration of flow priors within PnP frameworks, achieving high-quality reconstructions with reduced computational cost.
  </details>

- **[Weighted-AoI-Driven UAV Trajectory and Reporting for Dynamic CKM Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13726)**  `arXiv:2611.13726`  
  _Zeyu Zhao, Yueling Che, Sheng Luo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing channel knowledge map (CKM) construction assumes quasi-static environments, failing when channels rapidly decorrelate. This paper investigates continuous online dynamic CKM tracking over an airborne 2D plane via a UAV fleet. We formulate a long-term error minimization problem under UAV mobility and reporting constraints. This problem, however, is intractable with unobservable errors for unmeasured cells. To tackle this, we propose a metric-driven scheduling approach featuring the Weighted-Age of Information (W-AoI). W-AoI evaluates cells by combining exact temporal staleness with spatial uncertainty predicted by a 2D U-Net. We prove that top-$K$ W-AoI selection maximizes the one-slot error reduction bound. Guided by this guarantee, we develop a lightweight greedy trajectory policy incorporating continuous along-path sensing. Simulations demonstrate our method reduces steady-state error by 3.8 dB$^2$ and 4.8 dB$^2$ over AoI-only and FIFO baselines, closely approaching an idealized oracle.
  </details>

- **[TRUER: Test-Time Reward Correction Using an Error-Aware Rectifier for Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13714)**  `arXiv:2611.13714`  
  _Mingxiang Shen, Jinhao Li, Jiaming Xu, Yibo Han, Guohao Dai, Yijia Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time reinforcement learning enables language models to adapt using rewards derived from their own answers. However, majority voting can reinforce incorrect solutions even when correct alternatives are available. Our training audit shows that false positives (incorrect responses receiving positive rewards) dominate reward errors. We focus on a practical regime where ground-truth rewards are difficult to obtain at scale, while a limited labeled source set is available. We introduce TRUER, a framework that uses limited supervision to train a reward rectifier that distinguishes correctness from consensus. To achieve this goal, we train the rectifier with an emphasis on incorrect majorities and correct minorities, targeting cases where consensus and correctness disagree. After that, we freeze the rectifier and use it to correct rewards during subsequent test-time adaptation. By improving reward accuracy and thereby model performance, TRUER consistently outperforms naive TTRL in both metrics across all evaluated configurations.
  </details>

- **[FedVP: Federated Learning across Heterogeneous Models by Pooling Locally Trained Verifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13708)**  `arXiv:2611.13708`  
  _Yifan Li, Xi Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conventional federated learning relies on the shared model backbone to aggregate client updates, limiting collaboration among institutions that deploy large language models (LLMs) from different families and at different scales. We propose Federated Verifier Pool (FEDVP), a new federated learning paradigm that enables heterogeneous clients to collaborate through shared verification rather than parameter aggregation. Each client trains a verifier using local data and responses generated by its chosen LLM backbone. The server pools these independently trained verifiers and combines their judgments to select answers without merging model parameters. This allows both LLM backbones and verifiers to differ in architecture and size, while keeping the LLM backbones frozen and deriving collaborative gains from improved answer selection. We evaluate FEDVP across five public medical question-answering datasets treated as federated clients, using Qwen and LLaMA backbones ranging from 3B to 14B parameters. FEDVP consistently improves performance across all five clients and outperforms the strongest conventional federated baselines by 1.46-5.79 percentage points in mean accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Bootstrapped On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13680)**  `arXiv:2611.13680`  
  _Yue Wu, Xueyu Zhou, Yangrong Hu, Jian Huang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) improves language models by distilling teacher predictions on student-generated trajectories, while on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) removes the need for an external teacher by using a reference-conditioned copy of the model as the teacher. Despite its great early improvements, we find that OPSD does not remain consistently effective under continued training and can eventually suffer substantial performance degradation. We study this training behavior and link the degradation to a growing mismatch between the evolving reference-free student and the frozen privileged teacher. In the early stage of OPSD, the privileged teacher remains closely aligned with the student and provides useful guidance for improving the student's generation trajectories. However, as training progresses, the growing distributional mismatch between the privileged teacher and reference-free student can cause the teacher to provide misleading supervision, ultimately degrading student performance. This phenomenon motivates us to first leverage privileged reference supervision to acquire useful knowledge and then transfer it to a fresh student. We introduce Bootstrapped On-Policy Distillation (BOPD), a two-stage framework that first uses OPSD to internalize task-relevant information from reference-conditioned supervision into an intermediate model, and then distills this reference-free intermediate model into a fresh copy of the initial model. Experiments across models show that BOPD improves mathematical reasoning and code generation beyond both the initial models and the constructed OPSD teachers. These results suggest that privileged reference supervision can be more effectively exploited by first transferring its task-relevant signal into a task-adapted model and then distilling from that model without reference conditioning.
  </details>

- **[SepsisFlow: an event-stream foundation model for continuous risk warning and trajectory modelling in ICU sepsis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13661)**  `arXiv:2611.13661`  
  _Yawen Zhang, Wanzhe Xu, Zhenhua Zhang, Jinyu Wu, Yizhou Zhuang, Yitao Yang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sepsis is a leading cause of ICU death with heterogeneous onset and course, yet most warning models predict only whether sepsis will occur, leaving clinicians uncertain of its onset context and early trajectory once an alarm sounds. To answer these questions on one hourly clock from ICU admission, we built SepsisFlow: before onset, it combines a Mamba model pretrained on 2.57 million EHRs with about 1,700 hourly features in gradient-boosted trees to predict 6/12/24-h risk; at onset, it fuses Mamba and tabular encoders via a gated network into continuous patient states defining onset contexts; after onset, it encodes 72-h organ-support and physiological trajectories via an attention bidirectional recurrent network into early courses. For pre-onset warning in MIMIC-IV, SepsisFlow detected more cases than NEWS2, qSOFA, and SIRS at equal alarms, gaining about 8 cases per 100 from hourly updating, while outperforming 16 retrained algorithms and maintaining risk ranking in eICU. At onset, its contexts separated 2% cardiovascular-ICU from 15%-17% other 28-day mortality, and within each context after onset, persistent support carried fivefold higher mortality than de-escalation. SepsisFlow establishes a foundation-model paradigm for continuous risk and trajectory stratification in critical care.
  </details>

- **[Unified Optimization of Traffic Signal and Lane Channelization for an Isolated Intersection Using Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13599)**  `arXiv:2611.13599`  
  _Jiwon Kang, Jiyoung Kim, Hyunseok Jun, Keemin Sohn_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Optimizing traffic signal control has been a long-lasting critical issue in traffic engineering, and a number of robust methodologies have emerged to solve the problem so far. On the other hand, the road design for lane channelization is a static problem that is difficult to solve jointly with the real-time traffic signal control. If traffic volume does not vary, it is possible that both problems can be solved in an integrated manner as a single static optimization problem. Recently, researchers have devised a mixed integer linear problem (MILP) to jointly determine the traffic signal and road width designs. However, this joint optimization problem must be solved repeatedly whenever the traffic flow pattern varies. Solving the MILP may also take impractical computing time as the problem size gets larger. We present a robust methodology for solving the joint optimization problem using reinforcement learning (RL) on a real-time basis. Abrupt changes in lane markings with an incompatible signal phase can jeopardize drivers. To sidestep such a dangerous action change, we train a model with human feedback to filter infeasible action transitions in a rule-based manner. We devised a deep Q-network that can accommodate joint actions for traffic signal and lane marking plans. To ensure the implementation of the proposed operation, a variable sign for lane markings is necessary at each stop line of an intersection. The experimental results confirm that the proposed methodology enhances the intersection performance by increasing throughput.
  </details>

- **[Discovering Structurally Plausible and Interpretable Cognitive Models with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13561)**  `arXiv:2611.13561`  
  _Boyoung Kwon, Jiyoon Seo, Sang Ho Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Computational cognitive modeling has traditionally relied on manual model construction, which is a time-intensive process that requires substantial domain expertise. Although recent LLM-based approaches have begun to automate model generation, they often prioritize predictive accuracy at the expense of structural plausibility and parameter identifiability. We introduce DisCo, an LLM-guided framework that discovers interpretable symbolic cognitive models through evolutionary search with two structural components: a Validator that filters structurally invalid models, and a Cleaner that promotes parameter identifiability by removing redundant parameters. Across three behavioral tasks, DisCo consistently discovers models that achieve performance competitive with task-specific baselines. Ablation analyses show that the Validator is important for recovering psychologically meaningful structure. Without this component, models tend to violate behavioral constraints and show less consistent associations with external cognitive measures. These results suggest that LLM-driven model discovery, when coupled with appropriate structural constraints, can yield cognitively plausible models that go beyond predictive fit.
  </details>

- **[Massive Activations Confound Sparse Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13548)**  `arXiv:2611.13548`  
  _Nathan Delisle, Chenhao Tan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are commonly evaluated by variance explained and ablation damage. We show how massive activations undermine both evaluations, and propose a correction. Massive activations cause up to 99.9% of activation variance to lie along the first principal component (PC1). Because the PC1 component has no unique sparse decomposition, each SAE run rebuilds it from a different set of features. These features dominate importance rankings: an average of 47 of the 50 most important features are unmatched in a separate run, compared with about 27 of a random 50. A dictionary can also report 99.96% of variance explained while leaving 33% of the variance outside PC1 unexplained. We bypass PC1 by training the SAE on activations with PC1 removed, then adding PC1 to the reconstruction. In affected models, the top-50 feature sharing rate rises from 4-39.5% to 73-99.5%. At the same TopK sparsity, the resulting SAE degrades the model up to 44% less and reduces content reconstruction error by up to 33.9%.
  </details>

- **[Adaptive catalyst discovery emerges from composable scientific intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13536)**  `arXiv:2611.13536`  
  _Wentao Li, Qilong Cai, Qi Lei, Jinxing Chen, Ruixuan Chen, Jiangjie Qiu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Catalysis underpins chemical manufacturing, yet knowledge for catalyst design remains dispersed across experimental observations, computational data and catalysis subdisciplines. To overcome this challenge, we present Composable Catalytic Intelligence (CatAI), a physics-grounded modular AI framework that learns 1.8 million structure-property relations and organizes them into 30 physicochemical encoders. This reusable intermediate layer enables task-specific models to guide catalyst discovery for targeted reactions and objectives. Across benchmark tasks, CatAI achieves a 7.8% lower task-averaged prediction error than the next-best models. In experimental validation, CatAI identified a new confined-passable pocket catalyst applicable in industrial homogeneous polymer synthesis with 9 times the activity of the commercial benchmark. In parallel, it guided autonomous closed-loop optimization of a highly active Cu-based geminal-atom photocatalyst for C-O coupling reactions surpassing the traditional Ni-based systems. This approach connects property-level data to catalytic objectives, supporting physicochemical knowledge reuse across mechanistically distinct systems through computational screening and iterative model-experiment feedback.
  </details>

- **[Beyond the Log-Linear Frontier: Pre-Asymptotic Analysis of Finite-Budget Neural Scaling Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13534)**  `arXiv:2611.13534`  
  _Tianyu Zhao, Zhixiang Ren_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Classical neural scaling laws model excess risk as a separable power law in model capacity $N$ and dataset size $D$. At finite budgets, however, empirical learning curves and compute-optimal allocations depart from this log-linear frontier. This paper analyzes how spectral and domain heterogeneity govern these finite-scale departures. Along the capacity axis, Mellin--Barnes analysis of covariance resolvents yields a three-term expansion where competing spectral and regularization modes produce finite-scale curvature and logarithmic transitions. Because fitting multiple power laws on narrow pilot grids is ill-conditioned, we eliminate the loss floor via first-order Aitken differencing to obtain a well-conditioned closed-form initializer. Along the data axis, KKT duality reduces $K$-domain mixing to scalar water-filling, yielding finite-budget bottleneck bounds, an online sampling curriculum, and a simplex Hessian metric for cross-domain transfer. Along the iso-compute frontier $C=6ND$, we prove that the optimal allocation exponent drifts in proportion to capacity and data exponent variances, while Schur-complement reduction separates intrinsic drift from frozen-hyperparameter bias. Across 45 operator spectra, Pythia (70M--12B), Chinchilla (245 runs), and RegMix (17 domains), our estimators achieve $5.89\%$ holdout MAPE, cut high-compute allocation regret by up to $76.4\%$, and reduce cross-scale mixture RMSE by $59.4\%$ with zero selection regret.
  </details>

- **[The RSI Tax: When Recursive Self-Improvement Is Not Worth the Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13524)**  `arXiv:2611.13524`  
  _Yitong Zhang, Ang Li, Yisen Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive self-improvement (RSI) seeks to improve an agent using its own task experience. Its practical value depends on whether performance gains justify the full cost of self-improvement and task execution. We evaluate this trade-off on continual task streams, scoring each task before its feedback is used for subsequent updates. We define the \emph{RSI Tax} as the token cost of the self-improvement process and include it alongside task-execution costs in end-to-end evaluation. Across six methods and two language models, a bounded-note baseline matches or exceeds the observed accuracy of Meta-Harness, EvoSkill, and RRSI, with mean total-token savings of 65.6-71.0\%. We decompose the RSI Tax into verification, exploration, and writing costs, and track subsequent use of the associated state or artifact. For these three RSI methods, verification accounts for 74.8--93.6% of the aggregate RSI Tax. A substantial share of the RSI Tax is also associated with states or artifacts that are not used later in the evaluated streams. These results expose a practical limitation of costly RSI procedures: improvements in the deployed agent need not justify the expense of the improvement process. They motivate RSI objectives that jointly optimize the RSI Tax and expected task-execution cost while ensuring continued growth in agent capabilities across the task stream.
  </details>

- **[Sharp Sample Complexity of KL-Regularized Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13480)**  `arXiv:2611.13480`  
  _Andrew Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  KL-regularized reinforcement learning, the objective behind maximum-entropy RL and RL from human feedback, is variational inference in the graphical model of "control as inference": the regularized optimal policy is a posterior over trajectories, and the suboptimality of a policy is $\beta$ times a KL divergence between path measures. However, it is not known how many samples it takes to learn this posterior, even with a generative model. Earlier $1/\epsilon$ guarantees scale with $H^5$ in the effective horizon $H$, and no lower bound for MDPs depends on the temperature $\beta$. In this work, we show that for a discounted MDP with $K$ state-action pairs, the plug-in estimator finds a policy that is $\epsilon$-optimal from every initial state with $\widetilde{O}(KH^3/(\epsilon\max\{\epsilon,\beta\}) + KH)$ samples, and that no algorithm, even one that chooses where to sample, can do with fewer when $\epsilon \lesssim \min\{H, H^2/\beta\}$. For $\epsilon < \beta$ this is a $1/\epsilon$ rate with the same horizon dependence as unregularized RL. We also find the exact asymptotic constant of the plug-in estimator, a Fisher-weighted variance of the estimated soft Q-function. The estimator is locally asymptotically minimax among methods that sample every pair equally often, and we give the allocation of samples that is optimal among adaptive designs. Simulations on small MDPs match the rates and the constants.
  </details>
