- **[Deep Personalization: Continuous Context Observation for Local Proactive Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13457)**  `arXiv:2611.13457`  
  _Yijia Shao, Song Bian, Junxian Guo, Jindong Jiang, Zhen Dong, Huizi Mao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As AI agents become increasingly capable, users must still recognize when and how to ask for help, leaving many opportunities for useful support unrealized. Proactive agents hold the promise to close this gap, but effective proactivity requires understanding users' real-time activities and personalizing intervention timing so that assistance is offered when it is likely to be helpful rather than disruptive. We introduce deep personalization, a closed-loop process that connects continuous context observation with continual agent adaptation: an agent observes user activity, selectively retains long-term context, and adapts intervention decisions from implicit feedback. We initiate deep personalization in Coco, a proactive desktop agent, by having a separate Observer Agent that monitors desktop activity, maintains compact long-term user modeling context, and learns when assistance is desirable from implicit signals such as engagement, dismissal, explicit requests, and repeated work patterns. We further develop efficient on-device strategies to enable the Observer Agent to run on local AI compute platforms without streaming raw activity data off-device. For evaluation at scale without collecting sensitive real-user activity, we introduce a data-synthesis pipeline that transforms existing computer-use trajectories into OSWorld-Persona, a benchmark with persona-conditioned intervention labels. On OSWorld-Persona, deep personalization improves macro F1 across three model backbones from 0.12-0.19 under direct prompting to 0.35-0.48 after adaptation, a 2.4-3.0x relative gain. In a week-long user study, we observe that Coco improves its macro-averaged F1 score from 0.199 to 0.515. These results suggest that on-device deep personalization can make proactive agents more timely and increasingly aligned with each user's everyday work.
  </details>

- **[SLIC: Reinforcement Fine-Tuning Small LMs for Multi-Turn Analog Circuit Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13452)**  `arXiv:2611.13452`  
  _Yan Xu, Zhengqi Gao, Ching-Yun Ko, Zhang-Wei Hong, Wenjie Lu, Tao Yu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Analog integrated circuit (IC) design is a multi-turn, simulator-grounded engineering task that demands iterative reasoning over coupled performance tradeoffs, yet proprietary process design kits (PDKs) make hosted large language model workflows difficult to deploy in practical design settings. We present SLIC (Small LM Iterating on Circuits), a reinforcement fine-tuning framework that trains a 4B-scale, locally deployable language model for closed-loop analog circuit optimization. SLIC pairs structured circuit representations with discretized relative-update actions, domain- and success-gated multiobjective rewards, and Reward-Guided Rollout Steering (RGRS), which turns per-step simulator feedback into signals for both policy learning and online rollout construction in this sparse, highly constrained domain. We further release an open, goal-conditioned benchmark of 41 amplifier topologies with standardized protocols for in-distribution validation, held-out topology evaluation, and cross-technology-node transfer. Trained solely with open source 65 nm CMOS predictive technology models (PTM), SLIC-Random achieves 87.1% in-distribution four-specification Pass@1 within eight turns, surpassing the 763B DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Think by 48.5 percentage points. Beyond the training distribution, SLIC-Softmax reaches 72.6% Pass@1 on held-out topologies, and the trained policies consistently transfer across five unseen technology nodes without additional fine-tuning. This transfer extends to a commercial TSMC 65 nm CMOS PDK, where SLIC-Random achieves 49.9% held-out-topology Pass@1, demonstrating practical deployment through local inference without transmitting proprietary data to external LLM services. Performance further scales with test-time simulator interactions, reaching up to 83.2% held-out-topology Pass@1 at a 32-turn budget.
  </details>

- **[Fermat ProbCover: Coverage-Based Active Learning on Fermat Distance Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13448)**  `arXiv:2611.13448`  
  _Gokul Bhusal, Delanna Do, Kevin Miller, Ekaterina Merkurjev, Stephan Wojtowytsch_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Active learning aims to reduce annotation cost by selecting the most informative examples for labeling. In the low-budget regime, where uncertainty-based methods often fail, coverage-based active learning methods such as ProbCover have been shown to be effective. However, these methods typically rely on Euclidean distance, which may not adequately capture the underlying geometry of data. In this article, we propose PC-Fermat, a coverage-based active learning method that replaces Euclidean coverage neighborhoods with neighborhoods defined by a Fermat distance, i.e., a data-adaptive distance that reflects the underlying distribution of the data points. To facilitate computation, we construct a weighted Euclidean neighborhood graph and use a truncated shortest-path algorithm to compute the Fermat coverage graph. We also establish sufficient conditions under which this construction exactly preserves the Fermat coverage computed on the complete graph. Finally, we evaluate PC-Fermat on eight classification datasets across varying labeling budgets, with particular emphasis on the very low-budget regime. Our experimental results demonstrate that PC-Fermat outperforms several existing active learning methods on the majority of the datasets.
  </details>

- **[You Don't Need Feature Learning to Do In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13431)**  `arXiv:2611.13431`  
  _Joseph Turnbull, Jamie Simon_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How should we understand memorization vs. generalization in in-context learning (ICL) tasks, and when does one happen vs. the other? To investigate, we study how rotation-invariant kernel machines learn the ICL linear regression task proposed by Raventos et al. (2023). By decomposing the task in the kernel eigenbasis, we obtain a spectral picture of learning in this ICL setting. This picture reveals that as sample size grows, kernel machines learn successively-higher-degree components of the memorizing solution, which depend upon successively-higher-degree statistics of the training task set. The generalizing solution is approximately a low-degree truncation of the memorizing solution, and so the model first generalizes, then degrades into memorization as training proceeds.The takeaways are threefold. First, if this case is indicative, learning a "generalizing" solution on an ICL task is really just underfitting the memorizing solution! Second, the fact that kernel machines can generalize on this task means that feature learning is inessential to ICL. Third, transformers empirically trained to do ICL are shown to memorize tasks through the higher-order statistics of the training tasks that the kernel-based theory predicts, suggesting it applies even to models capable of feature learning.
  </details>

- **[AdjointFlow: Accelerating Scientific Inverse Problems with Physics-Based Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13389)**  `arXiv:2611.13389`  
  _Ethan Hanold, Nicholas M. Boffi, Qin Li, Leonardo Zepeda-Núñez_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Inverse problems are ubiquitous across scientific applications ranging from seismic imaging to astrophysics to nondestructive testing, yet classical solvers often struggle with nonconvexity and repeated evaluations of expensive physical models. Diffusion models can amortize this computation offline, but largely data-driven conditional models underutilize known physics and suffer from ill-conditioned transport maps when the target distribution is concentrated. Plug-and-play (PnP) methods explicitly leverage the physical model to guide a learned prior, improving data consistency but reintroducing costly iterative computations. We instead propose AdjointFlow, a framework built on stochastic interpolants that injects the known physics into the generative transport itself. We design a data-dependent, adjoint-based initialization to reduce transport distance to the target, modify the transport velocity to remain regular as the endpoint distributions concentrate, and parameterize the network to build the initialization directly into the learned velocity. Together, these enable accurate reconstruction at substantially lower cost than generative models starting from Gaussian noise. Experiments on inverse scattering, seismic imaging, and black hole imaging show that AdjointFlow consistently produces more accurate reconstructions than conditional diffusion models while being up to five orders of magnitude faster at inference than PnP methods. Optional inference-time refinement that queries the physical model can further improve data consistency, matching or exceeding the accuracy of PnP methods without retraining.
  </details>

- **[Looping Deeper Without Growing the KV Cache: Recurrent Depth for Causal Encoder-Decoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13378)**  `arXiv:2611.13378`  
  _Jiaqi Leng, Minshen Zhang, Zhaofeng Sun, Yu-Yang Qian, Peng Zhao, Hao Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped language models deepen computation without adding parameters, yet caching full-context key-value (KV) states across layers and rounds severely bounds serving batch size. We design a looped causal encoder-decoder (CED) that achieves recurrent depth with zero round-wise KV growth, addressing where and how to loop an asymmetric stack in which only sparse encoder layers generate global KV. We find that recurrence must be placed in the encoder to iteratively refine global KV representations rather than re-reading static decoder projections, which both drives language-modeling gains and preserves long-context scaling. Furthermore, training the encoder loop under stochastic recurrence depth induces a fixed-point equilibrium where every round losslessly shares the last round's KV cache. Compounding cross-layer and cross-round KV sharing, the resulting Looped CED matches an unlooped CED's minimal memory footprint and recovers 86-93% of a looped Transformer's gain with 1/6 of its global KV. To test serving at production scale, we loop the 552B-parameter DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash for 8 rounds on one 4xGB200 node: with per-layer and per-round KV the loop cannot fit a single 128K-token request, whereas Looped CED holds 36.8x less KV per token, fits 38 such requests (as many as the unlooped model), and serves 3.6x higher throughput on 8K-token prompts.
  </details>

- **[When Are Predictions Enough? An Evaluation Protocol for Frozen Expert Composition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13356)**  `arXiv:2611.13356`  
  _Alessandro Pereira, Keith J Ransom, Lewis Mitchell_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Practitioners often combine independently trained models while keeping their weights frozen. These experts are commonly combined through their final predictions, although their internal features may retain useful information that those predictions omit. We ask when combining features improves on combining predictions, and how to make that comparison fair. We introduce a reusable evaluation protocol, apply it to 21 frozen deepfake detectors from DF40 (Yan et al., 2024), and audit how evaluation choices change the conclusions. The protocol uses strong prediction baselines, compares simple and larger feature combiners, varies architecture and training data separately, and reports results by held-out generator with train/test overlap controls. Under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, logistic regression on the features of six architecture-diverse experts improves macro AUROC by 0.081 over the best tested prediction combiner in each fold, with gains on seven of eight generators. The larger network shows no clear improvement over the linear model (approximately 3.5M versus 10K parameters), suggesting that access to richer information matters more than additional combiner capacity on this pool. Features may help when they retain information that the predictions omit and that transfers to new generators. Predictions may be enough when the strongest prediction baseline already performs well, while neither approach reliably compensates for poor expert transfer. Weakening prediction baselines on the overlap-affected standard split inflates the apparent feature advantage by up to 0.172 AUROC. Overlap-aware evaluation also changes the estimated gain, though the contrast does not isolate overlap. Results by generator and expert pool show where the advantage holds, weakens, or reverses. Without these controls, the same benchmark can support different conclusions.
  </details>

- **[Stochastic Projections and Rank-Aware Learning Rates for Federated LoRA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13331)**  `arXiv:2611.13331`  
  _Saber Malekmohammadi, Kevin Kuo, Virginia Smith, Golnoosh Farnadi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning large-scale foundation models across resource-constrained, distributed clients presents significant computational and communication challenges. While merging Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) with Federated Learning (FL) enables rank-based flexibility, the interdependence of the adaptation matrices ($\mathbf{A}$ and $\mathbf{B}$) often complicates optimization. In this work, we theoretically demonstrate that LoRA fine-tuning with Gaussian initialization and a frozen $\mathbf{A}$ matrix acts as a stochastic approximation of full fine-tuning, where batch gradients are subject to a random, rank-dependent perturbation. Motivated by this insight, we propose freezing and periodically resampling $\mathbf{A}$ matrices at the client level. This strategy decouples the $\mathbf{A}$ and $\mathbf{B}$ gradients, diminishes LoRA low-rank constraints and reduces both computational and uplink communication overheads by 50\%, allowing for the reallocation of resources toward higher adaptation ranks. Furthermore, we show that in this frozen-$\mathbf{A}$ regime, the adaptation rank $r$ directly governs the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) of the perturbed gradients. Specifically, we find that the SNR increases linearly with $r$, implying that clients' learning rates must be dynamically scaled with their adaptation ranks for higher utility. Building on these foundations, we introduce SPRA-LoRA (Stochastic Projection Rank-Aware LoRA), a federated rank-aware fine-tuning strategy that utilizes frozen, resampled $\mathbf{A}$ matrices and rank-dependent learning rates for clients. We also prove its convergence with heterogeneous client resources. Our experiments across benchmark datasets demonstrate that SPRA-LoRA consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines with half the overhead on clients.
  </details>

- **[Robust Flow Matching under Target Corruption and Label Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13324)**  `arXiv:2611.13324`  
  _Mert Can Kurucu, Erik Englesson, Hossein Azizpour_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flow Matching (FM) is a strong framework for generative modeling due to its stable training and efficient sampling. However, FM assumes clean target data and labels, an assumption often violated in practice. We formulate FM training as a time-dependent regression problem over conditional vector fields and analyze how the two corruption types affect the objective: i) target corruption adds a time-dependent residual term that grows with time, ii) label noise causes updates to the wrong class-conditional vector field. Based on this analysis, we propose to improve FM robustness by mapping corruption-specific residual scores to sample weights. For target corruption, we propose the Temporal Residual Score (TRS), which emphasizes residual magnitudes at intermediate-to-late FM times, where clean and corrupted samples are more separable. For label noise, we propose the Comparative Conditional Score (CCS), which compares the residual of the observed class-conditional vector field against alternative class-conditional vector fields for the same trajectory and velocity target. Experiments across controlled corruption settings and real-world noisy labels show that the proposed methods improve robustness over standard FM and robust-training baselines, supporting corruption-specific residual structure as a practical basis for robust FM.
  </details>

- **[Train High, Ship Low: Efficient LoRA Synchronization for Distributed On-Policy Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13306)**  `arXiv:2611.13306`  
  _Shenghui Li, Li Ju, Thiemo Voigt_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transmitting LoRA adapters to rollout workers can bottleneck distributed on-policy post-training over bandwidth-constrained networks. Lowering the training rank may limit the capacity a workload needs, while adaptive optimizer updates are difficult to compress. Rollout workers can instead reconstruct updates from gradients by maintaining replicated optimizer states. We introduce HiLo, a compressor-agnostic framework that replaces parameter transmission with compressed gradient dissemination and local optimizer replay. By applying the same decoded gradients, the learner and workers maintain consistent policy states at the same version under reproducible execution. We further develop Spar, a budget-aware gradient compressor that combines spectral truncation, adaptive rank allocation and error feedback to decouple training rank from communication cost. Experiments across on-policy distillation and reward-guided post-training show that HiLo with Spar maintains learning quality close to full synchronization while transmitting as little as 1.6% of the adapter bytes, and accelerates end-to-end updates by up to $2.2\times$ under bandwidth constraints.
  </details>

- **[Neural Network-Assisted CLEAN for Efficient Multipath Parameter Estimation in MIMO Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13298)**  `arXiv:2611.13298`  
  _Chaofan Deng, Linyu Sun, Jaeho Lee, Zishen Wan, Arijit Raychowdhury_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-resolution multipath estimation in wideband multiple-input multiple-output (MIMO) channels requires repeated searches over angle of arrival, angle of departure, and propagation distance. This paper presents neural network-assisted CLEAN (NN-CLEAN), which replaces the multidimensional search at each extraction step with a multi-head classifier while retaining least-squares (LS) estimation of the complex path gain, parametric reconstruction, and residual cancellation. The same classifier is applied to successive residuals, allowing the extraction depth to vary without changing its output structure. With the number of iterations prescribed by the reference multipath-component (MPC) count, off-grid synthetic tests show that NN-CLEAN slightly exceeds grid-search CLEAN (GS-CLEAN) in path accuracy for one and two MPCs, although its accuracy declines more rapidly at larger path counts. For complete three-MPC extraction at a batch size of eight, NN-CLEAN achieves an approximately $27\times$ speedup and a $52$-fold reduction in peak GPU memory relative to GS-CLEAN. Applied without retraining to two-MPC line-of-sight (LOS) and non-line-of-sight (NLOS) Sionna ray-tracing channels within a coded MIMO link, NN-CLEAN substantially reduces channel-reconstruction mean-squared error (MSE) and bit error rate (BER) relative to interpolated LS channel estimation. GS-CLEAN retains an accuracy advantage on these channels, indicating the estimation cost associated with the computational savings.
  </details>

- **[An Analytical Model of Compute-limited Multistage Training Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13276)**  `arXiv:2611.13276`  
  _Nishil Patel, Jin Hwa Lee, Basile Confavreux, Andrew Saxe_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern foundation models are trained through multiple stages, yet how these stages interact and how compute should be allocated across them remain poorly understood. We introduce an analytically tractable high-dimensional model combining unsupervised pretraining with a linear autoencoder, supervised fine-tuning (SFT) with noisy stepwise labels, and reinforcement learning (RL) with sparse episodic rewards. Using random matrix theory and an operator-projection closure, we derive approximate low-dimensional learning dynamics that closely match simulations. We show that finite pretraining data imposes a representation-dependent performance limit, with Bayes classification error scaling as the inverse square root of the pretraining sample ratio, and observe the same exponent in asymptotic SFT and RL errors. Better pretraining also reduces sensitivity to noisy supervision. SFT initialization shortens the initial RL plateau induced by sparse rewards, an effect we also observe in OLMo-2 1B on GSM8K. Longer SFT warm starts additionally improve asymptotic RL performance. Optimizing compute across all three stages reveals that the optimal allocation depends on supervision quality and total budget, with a non-monotonic dependence of the RL compute share on budget. Finally, a complementary compositional model shows how RL selectively strengthens task-relevant pretrained skills while largely preserving unused skills. Together, these results identify mechanisms linking representation quality, feedback structure, and compute allocation in multistage training.
  </details>

- **[Off-policy Learning with Excursion Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13275)**  `arXiv:2611.13275`  
  _Jiamin He, Mark Rowland, Zhaohan Daniel Guo, Hado van Hasselt, Diana Borsa_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Off-policy reinforcement learning (RL) tries to learn the value of a target policy while data comes from a different behavior policy. This often requires trading off bias for variance or stability. One way to trade these off is to linearly interpolate between the behavior and target policies, but this represents only one point in a broader space of interpolation mechanisms. We introduce excursion policies--a structured alternative where the agent follows the target policy for a multi-step "excursion" before reverting to the behavior policy. We derive dynamic programming results for these policies, establishing the theoretical basis for their use in RL. We further develop provably sound off-policy temporal-difference (TD) algorithms for excursion value estimation. We find that excursion policies provide an efficiency-stability trade-off comparable to mixture policies in continuous control benchmarks, and our analysis illustrates conceptual differences in their behavior.
  </details>

- **[Wrong the Same Way: Cross-Prompt Sharing Both Suppresses and Amplifies Reward Hacking in RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13218)**  `arXiv:2611.13218`  
  _Michael Li, Rohan Pandey, Ray Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Two accounts of how reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) interacts with an imperfect verifier coexist: in one, reward hacking is inherited from the base model; in the other, RL amplifies it. We study both in a controlled environment small enough to compute the entire response distribution exactly. We vary the universality of a leak, or the fraction of prompts that accept the same wrong response, and the verifier's static false-positive rate. At its KL-regularized optimum, RLVR acts as a filter that reweights the accepted set without altering its composition, so the hack rate is inherited exactly. Taking that as a null model, a known decomposition writes the drift from it as a sum over prompts of covariances between reward and the empirical tangent kernel. The terms from other prompts vanish without parameter sharing. With sharing, every prompt that accepts a wrong answer pushes it up and every prompt that rejects it pushes it down through the baseline, in proportion to the model's probability of producing it there. A shared-parameter policy thus drives a leak that few prompts accept below the base rate and amplifies one that most prompts accept. At every convergent learning rate under Adam, SGD, and sampled GRPO, hacking ends higher at high universality than at low, but whether either end crosses the base rate depends on the step size. Independent per-prompt copies remove the dependence. The direction of this push depends on neither the verifier's error rate nor universality alone, but on how much of the model's kernel-weighted probability of a wrong answer falls on prompts that accept it, which frozen base model controls support. Verifier fixes should target the mistakes the model repeats across prompts: two patches that remove equally many errors differ by up to 38 points of true accuracy. We propose this alignment as a hypothesis for why earlier studies disagree.
  </details>

- **[Audited Gradients, Anchored Policies: Model-Based Reinforcement Learning for Efficient Flow Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13203)**  `arXiv:2611.13203`  
  _Ruige Kong, Stefania Fresca_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Feedback controllers for flows are expensive to design, since each candidate must be evaluated with a high-fidelity solver. Differentiable surrogates reduce that cost, but they also introduce a failure mode that prediction error cannot detect. Policy optimization uses the derivative of the objective with respect to the action, which we call the action gradient and which can be computed either through the surrogate or through the high-fidelity solver. Optimization through the surrogate uses only its action gradient, and a surrogate can follow the flow trajectory closely while misestimating this derivative. The surrogate action gradient is compared against a central finite-difference estimate from the high-fidelity solver on held-out states, under acceptance criteria fixed before the comparison, and we call that comparison an audit. A low-dimensional feedback law is then fitted to the surrogate action gradient when the surrogate passes the audit, and to the solver estimate when it does not. This law initializes a neural policy, and a quadratic penalty holds the policy near it during optimization. The proposed approach is validated on four test cases. The resulting controllers outperform or match a model-free baseline, while using 3 to 156 times fewer high-fidelity solver interactions across the four test cases. One surrogate predicts the objective at correlation 0.9996 but its action gradient at only 0.459, so prediction accuracy does not identify a usable surrogate. Another surrogate, whose action gradient agrees with the solver estimate at correlation 0.996, makes the objective 0.52% worse when the policy is optimized through it without the anchor penalty, so an accurate gradient alone does not provide a working controller. Finally, a surrogate that passes the audit at one fixed action fails it around the actions the deployed controller applies, so a pass is local.
  </details>

- **[XAI-Driven Confidence Estimation for OOD-Resilient Runtime Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13199)**  `arXiv:2611.13199`  
  _Mahmoud Sanad, Mohammed Bakr Sikal, Osama Abboud, Heba Khdr, Jörg Henkel_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine learning-based resource management (RM) models degrade under out-of-distribution (OOD) inputs at runtime, yet existing approaches in the literature rely on a single global error margin that is overly conservative and fails to distinguish reliablepredictions from catastrophic failures. We propose Lightweight Runtime EXplainability (LEX), the first post-hoc XAI framework for input-specific runtime confidence estimation in ML-based RM. LEX clusters the embeddings of the training input space using a Gaussian Mixture Model and associates per-cluster prediction error bounds to input samples, enabling fine-grained runtime confidence signals without retraining. Evaluated on a state-of-the-art LSTM-based IPS forecasting model on Intel Alder Lake, across 34 benchmark applications, LEX achieves ~94% containment on unseen workloads and produces ~17% tighter per-input confidence bounds than global baselines. With its negligible runtime overhead, LEX advances the field by providing explainable and reliable input-specific confidence signals of ML predictions, thereby making ML-based resource management resilient to OOD at runtime.
  </details>

- **[Structured Kernel Surrogate Models for Multimodal Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13198)**  `arXiv:2611.13198`  
  _Ziniu Zhang, Zhenshuo Zhang, Jianglin Lu, Ruoxuan Xiong, Yun Fu, Hongyang R. Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study the problem of cross-sample modality attribution, which involves measuring how one modality in one sample affects the learning outcome in other multimodal samples. This problem is crucial in a multimodal system because a data modality (such as a text description) may be redundant for one sample (a self-explanatory image), but the same data modality would otherwise be uniquely informative for another sample, or informative when combined with other modalities. Prior work focuses on influence estimation at the level of one sample, and it remains an open question how one should capture the interactions across both samples and modalities. To capture scenarios like the above, we propose structured kernel surrogate models, which are surrogate mappings from both samples and modalities to the prediction outcome when they are trained together. We design an algorithm, SKS, which applies kernel ridge regression with a structured, compositional kernel on the selection states of individual samples and modalities. Specifically, the kernel function multiplies kernel-based similarity scores between every pair of data modalities for one sample and a pair of two samples. Additionally, we design a gradient-based estimation algorithm to efficiently estimate the structured kernel surrogate model. We evaluate our approach on two multimodal benchmarks, spanning a range of data modalities including satellite images, road networks, text, audio, and tabular data. We find that SKS achieves $57.8$% higher correlation with the true prediction outcomes than existing methods. In downstream data selection, SKS improves the average accuracy by $1.93$% over baselines. Models with data selected by SKS have lower Hessian traces, indicating a flatter loss surface. Using SKS to select satellite images identifies road segments, where accident scenes commonly occur, as opposed to background images.
  </details>

- **[RadRef: A Reference-Free Metric for Radiology Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13168)**  `arXiv:2611.13168`  
  _Shelly Schwartz, Alan Ropp, Michael B. Sneider, Nur Yildirim, Cree M. Gaskin, Chirag Agarwal_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI-generated radiology reports are usually evaluated by comparison with a radiologist-authored reference, leaving no way to score them when no reference exists. We introduce RadRef, a reference-free metric that scores report claims against image-derived beliefs over a clinical finding vocabulary, weighted by relevance to the clinical indication. On ReXVal, RadRef achieves 73.1% agreement on non-tied same-radiograph report pairs with radiologist rankings, comparable to leading reference-based metrics overall. It is also competitive on false findings and has its clearest relative result on omissions (missed findings). We further evaluate 100 model-generated and 100 radiologist-authored reports from 100 MIMIC-CXR studies, all independently annotated by two board-certified radiologists. On omissions, RadRef attains Kendall's tau-b = 0.295, against 0.386 between the two readers themselves, and no compared metric differs significantly from RadRef on this target. The same image-grounded representation provides a false-finding readout, RadRef-FF, with tau-b = 0.32 on ReXVal and 0.29 in the reader study, without requiring a reference. Controlled perturbations confirm that RadRef depends on the radiograph, report, and clinical indication. RadRef enables image-grounded evaluation before a radiologist-authored reference exists, supporting quality monitoring of report-generation systems in clinical settings, and complements reference-based metrics when one is available.
  </details>

- **[High Probability Risk Control for Online Policy Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13163)**  `arXiv:2611.13163`  
  _Yihong Guo, Drew Prinster, Suchi Saria, Anqi Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study risk control for online policy learning, where an agent adaptively collects data and aims to learn a new policy whose risk stays below a tolerance threshold at every round with high probability. Such policy updates and data collection induce feedback covariate shift (FCS): observed data depend on history through evolving policies and the data-collection process. FCS brings two substantive problems for risk control: 1) efficient risk evaluation for each candidate policy and 2) valid policy selection with high-probability risk control. To address these, we propose a permutation-based weighted risk evaluator with weights efficiently approximated by the Sinkhorn algorithm and select policies whose estimated risk upper confidence bounds (UCB) fall below the target threshold, with the UCB obtained through a proposed Gaussian bootstrap method. Theoretically, we prove that our risk evaluator has a no-larger variance upper bound than that of the standard importance-weighted evaluator. Our policy control method also yields an asymptotically valid simultaneous risk bound and provides asymptotic high-probability risk control for the selected policy. Empirically, we evaluate our method on the online contextual bandit problem, safe question answering benchmark, and Medical Claim Filtering tasks. Our method maintains the target risk level, achieves lower risk than baseline methods, has lower variance in risk estimation, and achieves reliable policy selection with high-probability risk control. Further ablation studies support our theoretical claims.
  </details>

- **[Byzantine-Robust Muon: Resilient Momentum Orthogonalization for Distributed Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13137)**  `arXiv:2611.13137`  
  _Grigory Malinovsky, Yury Demidovich, Peter Richtárik_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Optimization methods, including the Muon optimizer based on the Linear Minimization Oracle (LMO) and momentum orthogonalization, have shown strong empirical performance in large-scale and distributed learning, yet their robustness to adversarial behavior and data heterogeneity is not well understood. We study these methods within a unified first-order trust-region framework and analyze Muon-based updates with robust aggregation for Byzantine-robust distributed and federated learning. Our analysis does not rely on the i.i.d. assumption and introduces a non-Euclidean notion of heterogeneity consistent with the trust-region geometry. Under this framework, we establish convergence guarantees for both general non-convex and star-convex objectives. Our results provide a theoretical characterization of Byzantine-robust Muon-based optimization, with an additive heterogeneity error floor consistent with known impossibility results, under explicit aggregation assumptions. Under an explicit additive oracle-error condition, the guarantees also cover inexact orthogonalization, with the non-convex Byzantine terms in the bound's numerator unchanged but a smaller progress denominator. In simulated federated training with ResNet-18 and ViT-Tiny on CIFAR-10, additional image benchmarks, and a 38M-parameter byte-level language model on TinyStories, the tested Muon configuration attains higher attacked accuracy (lower perplexity) than the tested SGD and normalized-SGD configurations, and than sign updates on CIFAR-10. Fixed-honest controls further show a substantial RFA advantage over averaging under a bounded RFA-query attack, higher attacked accuracy for Muon-NS than tensorwise Euclidean normalization from shared checkpoints, and lower held-out TinyStories perplexity than a tuned AdamW baseline.
  </details>

- **[Continuous-Time Set-Transformers for Asynchronous WiFi-IMU Indoor Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13057)**  `arXiv:2611.13057`  
  _Mohamed Bachar, Ilyass Abouelaziz, Yuehua Ding_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Indoor localization commonly combines WiFi received signal strength indicator (RSSI) fingerprints with inertial measurements. However, these sensor streams are sampled asynchronously at different rates; therefore, observations rarely align in time and may be missing or stale. Most existing methods resample the data onto a shared clock and process each modality using a separate branch. We instead present a continuous-time transformer that represents observations as an unordered set of timestamped tokens. Each token encodes its measurement, modality, and elapsed time since acquisition. A single attention block jointly fuses information across modalities and time, avoiding both resampling and modality-specific branches while remaining invariant to token order. During training, we randomly mask entire modalities and individual observations, enabling the model to operate under missing or outdated inputs. We validate the modality-specific encoders on their respective benchmarks and evaluate the fused model on both simulated and real datasets, including a crosssession dataset collected on unseen days. The proposed approach improves upon an open WiFi fingerprinting baseline under cross-session drift, remains competitive with learned fusion and dead-reckoning baselines, and degrades gracefully as observations become missing or stale. These results suggest that a single continuoustime transformer provides a practical framework for indoor localization from asynchronous sensors.
  </details>

- **[Explanation-Aware Fairness Beyond Output Parity: Joint Output-Attribution Regularization for Medical Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13052)**  `arXiv:2611.13052`  
  _Gökhan Özbulak, Oscar Jimenez-del-Toro, Lilian Berton, André Anjos_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Output-level fairness does not constrain whether demographic groups rely on similar predictive contributions in representation space. For a nonlinear encoder with a linear head, we show that outcome-conditioned output score disparity equals the signed sum of feature attribution differences across subgroups by allowing small prediction gaps to arise through cancellation effect. We test whether an attribution disparity penalty complements output-level equalized odds based fairness surrogates. Across 960 DINOv2-LoRA models on HAM10000 and Harvard-GF datasets for gender and race fairness, attribution augmentation increases utility-fairness trade-off performance for hypervolume in six different settings with paired 95% confidence intervals and relative gains of 1.49-10.57%. Under a predefined utility constraint, augmented model families also achieve 9.0-38.7% lower equalized odds difference, lower attribution disparity, and reduced cancellation ratio while preserving predictive utility. These results support attribution fairness as a complementary explanation-aware constraint on output fairness.
  </details>

- **[Permutation-Based Pre-trained Model Merging via Reciprocal Orthogonality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13016)**  `arXiv:2611.13016`  
  _Akira Ito, Daiki Chijiwa, Atsutoshi Kumagai_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ito et al. (2026) showed that sufficiently wide, independently trained neural networks can exhibit linear mode connectivity (LMC) with softmax temperature calibration, even without permutation. LMC means that a low-loss linear path connects the models' parameters. This behavior is explained by layerwise exponentially weighted connectivity (LEWC), whereby each merged layer outputs an exponentially weighted sum of the original models' corresponding representations, yielding ensemble-like predictions. However, prior work observed LEWC with substantial widening, such as 8192 units per hidden layer for MLPs and $16\times$ width for VGG-11, raising the question of whether such widths are necessary. We therefore search for permutations that encourage reciprocal orthogonality (RO), which suppresses interference between each model's weights and the other model's representations. This allows both models' computations to coexist in the merged network, promoting LEWC at smaller widths. We formulate the search as independent layerwise quadratic assignment problems and approximately solve their continuous relaxations using Frank--Wolfe. Experiments show that our method facilitates LMC with less width expansion. It also merges models trained on different data distributions, where weight matching, which maximizes weight similarity through permutations, struggles. The merged predictions approximate those of a logit ensemble of the original models.
  </details>

- **[FaST: Factored Synthesis of Transitions for Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13013)**  `arXiv:2611.13013`  
  _Daniel Campa, Spandan Das, Srinjoy Das_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Synthetic data augmentation promises to scale offline reinforcement learning beyond fixed logged datasets, but leading diffusion-based generators model the transition tuple (state, action, next state, reward, terminal) as a monolith: no single factor can be natively redrawn in isolation. We present FaST (Factored Synthesis of Transitions), which factors transitions via the chain rule into state, action, dynamics, reward, and terminal stages, fitting each with an estimator matched to its statistical properties. Across twelve D4RL locomotion and maze benchmarks, TD3+BC and IQL agents trained on FaST transitions match the SynthER diffusion baseline while generating transitions over 90x faster. The explicit action-given-state conditional can also be re-queried for a fixed state, enabling targeted action synthesis: drawing N candidate actions per generated state and keeping the highest-advantage one under a value function fit to the same data, thereby synthesizing improved behavior during generation rather than filtering for it post-hoc. Best-of-10 synthesis beats training on real data on 11 of 12 medium and medium-replay settings (+8.9 D4RL points on average, up to +28.4), and is competitive with advantage-filtered SynthER at a fraction of its generation cost. On expert mixtures both mechanisms degrade identically, tracing the failure to the advantage estimate itself rather than to either targeting mechanism.
  </details>

- **[The Shape of a Program: Path Signatures for Trace-to-Program Induction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13005)**  `arXiv:2611.13005`  
  _Mohamed Ghanem, Bernd Finkbeiner_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Synthesizing programs from execution traces is a fundamental challenge in algorithmic interpretability and the reverse engineering of complex systems. We bring a rough-path-theoretic perspective to program induction, treating execution traces as high-dimensional rough paths and encoding them with the path signature transform, a tool from stochastic analysis whose expectation provably characterizes the distribution of traces a program induces. On three program-induction domains, Karel, List, and RASP, our log-signature trace encoder outperforms LSTM, Transformer, and Fourier baselines in almost every setting, with its largest margins when training data is scarce. We further observe that the trace-to-program pipeline is naturally contractive: it often produces valid programs that are shorter than the ground truth. We turn this into a bootstrapping procedure that exposes redundancy in the ground-truth supervision without any external oracle, which we demonstrate on redundancy-injected versions of our datasets. Finally, the log-signature encoder induces a markedly more interpretable latent geometry than the baselines, in which the nearest neighbors of every state lie on its own execution trace.
  </details>

- **[Branch-and-Commit for Frozen RL Agents: When and Where to Spend Test-Time Compute](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13003)**  `arXiv:2611.13003`  
  _Sahar Admoni, Assaf Hallak, Yftah Ziser, Ofra Amir_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning agents are often deployed with policies that cannot be retrained or modified. In digital environments whose states can be copied and resumed, an operator has another option: sample several continuations from the current state, evaluate them, and continue from the endpoint of the selected branch. We call this procedure branch-and-commit. We study when it improves return, what produces the improvement, and where its computational budget should be spent. Across four environments, two frozen policies, five continuation verifiers, and a model-free selection rule, we find that the benefit of branching depends on dispersion: the difference between the best candidate return and the mean candidate return. Branching provides no benefit in low-dispersion settings, while increasing return by 12.8% in our main Craftax-Medium setting. Most of this gain comes not from selecting better actions, but from selecting among different stochastic realizations of the same action. Several competent verifiers achieve similar episode returns, suggesting that verifier quality is not the main constraint at our operating budget. Instead, computation is better allocated to states where useful dispersion is likely. On held-out seeds, gating branching using predicted dispersion retains 69% of the improvement from uniform branching while using 48% as many branching calls. These results characterize when branch-and-commit is useful and clarify what its gains represent.
  </details>

- **[One Table, Many Queries: Benchmarking Generalist Tabular Models Across Generation, Imputation, and Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13001)**  `arXiv:2611.13001`  
  _Yuanrui Wang, Kaifang Li, Xuanyue Li, Ziyun Li, Yan Lu, Xingxuan Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generation, imputation, and prediction query different aspects of the same tabular distribution, but are usually evaluated separately. This leaves unclear which capabilities they share, how their training objectives interact, and whether one model can perform all three well. We introduce the One Table Benchmark (\ours{}), spanning 56 datasets, 46 registered methods, and 20 capability axes under aligned data splits and explicit task contracts. We compare task ranks on aligned support, train five backbones under all seven non-empty objective combinations on five datasets with three seeds, and contrast fixed-model profiles with specialist portfolios. Rank comparisons connect imputation and prediction through numerical recovery and dependence preservation, and link generation to both through predictive utility; distributional fidelity follows different, sometimes opposing, rankings. Objective interventions show the largest pooled gains from including the recipient's own objective. Generation has the broadest coverage of positive pooled auxiliary effects, while prediction shows potential to improve imputation and synthetic-data utility, with responses that vary across backbones. Cross-addition effects reveal that objective benefits need not add and depend on the objectives already present. Fixed-model comparisons expose corresponding utility--fidelity trade-offs: some generalists surpass specialist portfolios on utility, while fidelity continues to favor specialization. Frozen foundation models show strong prediction and numerical point imputation, together with promising synthetic-data utility, highlighting their potential across all three tasks. These findings motivate unified tabular models that share useful conditional information while improving categorical reconstruction and faithful joint sampling.
  </details>

- **[Multimodal Graph Learning: A Survey of Models, Benchmarks, and Frontiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12990)**  `arXiv:2611.12990`  
  _Rui Xue, Tianfu Wu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal attributed graphs (MAGs), in which nodes carry text, images, and other modalities while edges encode relations among entities, underlie e-commerce, social media, content platforms, and creative applications. Learning on MAGs has developed along two complementary lines: graph-centric methods redesign how modalities are fused, propagated, and connected within message passing, while foundation-model-centric methods pretrain transferable multimodal graph encoders or bring graph context into multimodal large language models for prediction and generation. In parallel, several benchmarks have appeared with different datasets and evaluation protocols. This survey takes an algorithm-centric and evaluation-grounded view of the field and makes three contributions. Modeling: we introduce a unified message-passing formulation and organize methods into five integration levels (feature, propagation, topology, learning, and foundation model), covering discriminative and graph-conditioned generative models; within this formulation we derive representative methods as special cases, compare their assumptions, expressiveness, and cost, and identify cross-modal sensitivity as a property that separates otherwise similar architectures. Datasets and evaluation: we review recent benchmarks, summarize their datasets and metrics, define model-independent descriptors (label homophily, average degree, clustering, and edge provenance), and map each evaluation protocol to the baselines it uses. Frontiers: we connect multimodal graph learning to AI agents, covering the multimodal graphs that agents perceive and remember and graph engineering, the practice of organizing agent workflows as explicit graphs; we then discuss open problems, concrete efforts for the community, and research directions, including when graph structure helps in the presence of strong multimodal foundation models.
  </details>

- **[Third Eye: Beyond Immediate Gains in Recursive Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12988)**  `arXiv:2611.12988`  
  _Sujato Dutta, Padala Indira Sneha, Kypu Mahitha, Shashank Vanga, Neeraj Choudhary_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Does a useful self-update remain useful after the model updates again? Third Eye separates immediate performance from the outcome of a further native update. We evaluate five 3-8B backbones on math and code: 64 fresh runs, three candidates per run, and three continuations per candidate. Selection and final scoring use disjoint items. In the balanced Qwen3-4B/Llama-3.2-3B comparison, immediate-selected utility is near zero, but continuation reduces it by 1.78 percentage points (simultaneous 95% interval [0.68, 2.88]). Candidate-averaged immediate utility is already negative: selection partly accounts for the apparently neutral first step. Continuation also lowers utility for SmolLM3-3B and Llama-3.1-8B; Qwen3-8B remains inconclusive. Target and out-of-distribution losses dominate retention changes. Matched recipe and recipient-donor controls identify neither a reliable mitigation nor a single mechanism. The results support evaluating persistent self-updates as adaptation transitions, with independent scoring and a no-update baseline, rather than interpreting immediate performance as their full value.
  </details>

- **[MADSNet: A Multi-Scale Axial-Attentive Dense Spiking Network for Spike Camera Image Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12980)**  `arXiv:2611.12980`  
  _Sirui Li, Rui Zhao, Siqi Cai, Haizhou Li, Tiejun Huang, Yajing Zheng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spike cameras encode dynamic scenes into high-temporal-resolution binary spike streams for efficient high-speed vision. Although spiking neural networks (SNNs) can process such signals with sparse and accumulate-only computation, existing SNN-based reconstruction methods are typically shallow and still lag behind ANN-based models in reconstruction fidelity. This paper proposes MADSNet, a Multi-scale Axial-attentive Dense Spiking Network for spike camera image reconstruction. MADSNet adopts a hierarchical spiking encoder--decoder, where a multi-scale spike stem extracts early features, axial spike attention captures long-range dependencies, a dense spiking bottleneck enhances feature reuse, and a spike-to-continuous decoder reconstructs continuous intensity images from spike-domain representations. Experiments on gray-scale and color spike reconstruction show that MADSNet outperforms SSIR by 4.3 dB PSNR and Spk2ImgNet by 1.94 dB on SREDS. Compared with Spk2ImgNet, it achieves about 15$\times$ lower latency and uses only about 1/40 of the estimated energy, demonstrating that carefully designed SNN architectures can improve reconstruction quality while preserving computational efficiency.
  </details>

- **[StatPrompt-LLM: A Lightweight Statistical Refinement for Foundation-Style Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12974)**  `arXiv:2611.12974`  
  _Aniket Sinha, Veronica W. Tsou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-series forecasting has recently seen a paradigm shift with the introduction of foundation-style models that adapt large pretrained language architectures to sequential data. However, effectively exposing fundamental temporal structures, such as trend, scale, and temporal dependence, to these models remains an open challenge, particularly when the available observations are limited. In this paper, we conduct an exploratory empirical study across different generations of forecasting architectures and propose StatPrompt-LLM, a lightweight statistical refinement of Time-LLM. StatPrompt-LLM augments the existing Time-LLM prompting mechanism with dataset-level statistical information and introduces a zero-initialised autoregressive bypass as a complementary linear forecasting pathway. We evaluate our framework on electricity and meteorological time-series benchmarks, including ETTh1, ETTh2, and Weather, under full-prefix and reduced-prefix configurations. Under the 10% reduced-prefix configuration, StatPrompt-LLM achieved lower in-sample prediction errors than Time-LLM, with unweighted average relative reductions across datasets of 40.2% in MSE and 21.4% in MAE. Full-prefix results were more dataset-dependent. Ablation analyses revealed relatively modest and dataset-dependent changes when selected additional statistical descriptor groups were masked within the combined architecture. Computational profiling indicated additional model parameters and runtime overhead relative to Time-LLM.
  </details>

- **[Exploration via Exploitation: The Blessing of Reward Diversity in Personalized Federated RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12964)**  `arXiv:2611.12964`  
  _Jiin Woo, Gauri Joshi, Yuejie Chi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While federated reinforcement learning (RL) has traditionally guaranteed high efficiency in homogeneous environments, collaboration is often hindered when agents possess distinct goals. In this paper, we flip this paradigm, framing reward heterogeneity not as a hurdle to consensus, but as a structural asset that catalyzes exploration. We first propose Personalized Federated Upper Confidence Bound Value Iteration (PF-UCBVI), which achieves optimal linear speedup by decoupling shared dynamics from personalized objectives. To eliminate the risks of explicit exploration, we then introduce Personalized Federated Exploration-Free Value Iteration (PF-EFVI), a purely greedy algorithm that leverages reward diversity to ensure state-action coverage. We prove that PF-EFVI attains logarithmic regret without explicit exploration bonuses under sufficient reward diversity. Our results reveal that agent disagreement shifts the exploration burden from temporal complexity to spatial diversity, enabling efficient collective learning without explicit exploration.
  </details>

- **[ADIndex: Layer-Adaptive Sparse Attention Indexing via Group-Level Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12956)**  `arXiv:2611.12956`  
  _Jin-Fei Qi, Yiduo Li, Jiang-Xin Shi, Kan Liu, Tao Lan, Hanlin Tang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  DeepSeek Sparse Attention (DSA) reduces the computational cost of attention through token-level selection. However, its indexer still scores the entire causal prefix for each query, making indexing a dominant source of latency in long-context inference. Notably, the indexer alone can account for more than 50% of total prefill GPU time at long context lengths. To address this bottleneck, we propose ADIndex, an Adaptive and Distillation-driven Indexing method for token-level sparse attention. ADIndex integrates three key components: (i) a layer-wise calibration strategy that adaptively accommodates cross-query retrieval overlap, motivated by our observation of pronounced inter-layer heterogeneity; (ii) a group-level distillation scheme that leverages this heterogeneity to efficiently train adaptive layer-wise group indexers, capturing salient retrieval signals from individual queries; and (iii) a low-latency inference strategy that reduces repeated full-prefix scans through token bank sharing within query groups while retaining query-specific refinement. Experiments on DeepSeek-V3.2 and GLM-5.1 demonstrate that ADIndex preserves near-lossless task performance on LongBench, LongBench v2, and RULER while delivering substantial efficiency gains over standard DSA, including prefill and decoding kernel speedups of up to 6.22x and 3.40x, respectively, at a context length of 256K across the layer-wise reuse configurations, as well as an end-to-end serving speedup of 1.54x at a context length of 128K.
  </details>

- **[XposedMIA: Exploiting Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI) for One-Shot Membership Inference Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12067)**  `arXiv:2611.12067`  
  _Omar Hamdache, Serdar Kara, Kıvanç Kuzey Dikici, Abdullah Çağlar Öksüz, Anisa Halimi, Erman Ayday, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine learning (ML) services increasingly expose explainability outputs alongside model predictions. While such explanations are intended to improve transparency, they may also reveal information about the underlying training data. In this work, we show that the local surrogate geometry employed by Local Interpretable Model-agnostic Explanations (LIME) can be repurposed as a proxy for a sample's distance to the model's decision boundary. We extend this boundary-distance view to tabular counterfactual explanations and, for the first time, to visual counterfactual explanations for image classifiers. We present XposedMIA, a one-shot, explanation-based black-box membership inference attack (MIA) framework that converts the returned explanation into a proxy for the queried sample's distance to the model decision boundary. After a one-time calibration step on a small labeled calibration set of as few as 30 samples, our attack requires only a single query for each target sample. Unlike prior label-only attacks requiring iterative queries or shadow training, our method relies solely on the predicted label and its accompanying explanation. Our evaluation indicates that XposedMIA achieves high effectiveness, with success rates of up to 0.95 using only a single query per target, revealing that releasing explanations introduces an additional channel for membership leakage.
  </details>

- **[Can Hindsight Critics Guide Delayed Actors?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11863)**  `arXiv:2611.11863`  
  _Francisco M. López, Francisco Cruz_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sensory delays create temporal misalignments between the state of the environment and the observations available to an agent, turning Markovian control problems into partially observable ones. In this work, we inquire whether such misalignments can be mitigated during training by using hindsight to recover aligned state-action transitions. We introduce Hindsight Critics, an asymmetric extension of the Soft Actor-Critic for reinforcement learning under partial observability. The critics are trained on hindsight-aligned transitions whereas the actor learns from the resulting privileged value estimates despite only having access to delayed observations. Comparisons against delay-aware and unaware baselines on the DelayBench suite show that Hindsight Critics can partially compensate for observation delays without requiring modifications to the agent's observations during deployment.
  </details>

- **[STABLE: A Continual Learning Optimizer with Adaptive Drift Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10655)**  `arXiv:2611.10655`  
  _Xiaoyan Li, Lanpei Li, Massimo Coppola, Vincenzo Lomonaco, Antonio Carta_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual learning suffers from catastrophic forgetting, leading to the loss of past knowledge and unstable adaptation across task streams. Existing approaches typically address this issue through replay buffers, architectural changes, or additional regularization terms. We argue that forgetting is a consequence of unstable learning dynamics, which may benefit from an optimizer-level solution. The main challenge is that learning dynamics are time-dependent, as demonstrated by empirical phenomena such as the stability gap. First, we formally prove that, for distance-based classifiers, bounded parameter drift guarantees bounded margin erosion under a local score-stability condition. Based on this insight, we propose STABLE, a primal-dual optimizer with adaptive drift control. STABLE enforces a drift budget by increasing the dual variable when the budget is violated and decreasing it otherwise. This results in high stability without penalizing plasticity. Experiments on long-horizon unimodal streams and CLIP-based multimodal streams show that vanilla PEFT baselines trained with STABLE match or outperform specialized continual learning methods. In the multimodal setting, STABLE further preserves zero-shot transfer, matching or exceeding dedicated multimodal continual learning methods. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[TORNADO: Adaptive Latent Stochastic Transport for Calibrated Probabilistic PDE Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10251)**  `arXiv:2611.10251`  
  _Jorge Mifsut-Benet, Armand Kassaï Koupaï, Ramon Daniel Regueiro-Espino, Nicolas Baskiotis, Patrick Gallinari_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Probabilistic neural surrogates for PDEs enable ensemble forecasting, but often exhibit miscalibration when used for uncertainty quantification, especially in compute-constrained, few-step inference regimes. Existing generative approaches typically rely on fixed global stochasticity, which cannot adapt predictive uncertainty to the local dynamical state. We propose TORNADO, a latent probabilistic forecasting framework that formulates next-step prediction as stochastic transport between VAE encoder posteriors. Unlike existing approaches with globally prescribed stochasticity, TORNADO learns a SDE with state-dependent diffusion, enabling the predictive uncertainty to adapt to the local latent dynamics. The model is trained using analytic conditional endpoint targets derived from a Gaussian interpolant, enabling simulation-free learning. An additional energy distance regularization term improves distributional alignment. Empirically, TORNADO improves uncertainty calibration over representative generative baselines, including stochastic interpolants, diffusion models, and flow matching, while maintaining comparable predictive accuracy. Our results show that adapting stochasticity to the latent state, rather than prescribing it globally, is an effective mechanism for improving calibration in practical neural PDE forecasting settings. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Volatility Heredity in Multivariate Time Series: A Statistically Guided Temporal Convolutional Approach to Variable Ordering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07746)**  `arXiv:2611.07746`  
  _Kaoutar El Azaar, Mohamed Hamlich, Moncef Garouani, Franck Ravat, Asmae Chakir, Mohamed Tabaa_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Volatility heredity concerns the information that past fluctuations in one series may carry about another after accounting for the target's own persistence. QS-TCN compares this dependence in both directions to estimate an order of temporal precedence. For each series, quasi-maximum likelihood estimation yields a persistence coefficient, and the matrix pencil method extracts temporal modes from the autocovariances of squared observations. These estimates define filters in a temporal convolutional network. The network models the target's squared observations from their own past and scores the association between the residuals and the filtered history of a candidate source. Differences between the two directional scores are aggregated into a global order.In an exploratory simulation with 100 systems in each of four volatility regimes, an ensemble of ten models trained with fixed seeds achieved higher mean ordering quality than CCF-AUC, a lead--lag method based on distance correlation, in every regime. Against CCF-AUC applied to squared standardized observations, the mean gains were 8.69 percentage points under positive linear coupling and 7.81 points when nonlinear coupling depended on the target's state. In the other two regimes, paired 95\% bootstrap intervals for the gains included zero. Mean agreement with reference constraints was 71.55\% across nine Finance systems and 54.02\% across 27 simulated functional magnetic resonance imaging systems, compared with 50\% expected under random ordering. In a panel of daily currency and gold returns, the estimated order placed the dollar--franc and dollar--yen exchange rates before gold.
  </details>

- **[EEGDash Corpus: A Training-Ready Public Corpus for EEG Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06356)**  `arXiv:2611.06356`  
  _Kuber Shahi, Bruno Aristimunha, Arnaud Delorme_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electroencephalography (EEG) foundation models are pretrained on tens of thousands of hours. The largest clinical archives restrict redistribution, while public datasets use different formats and licences. Here, we rebuild the 77 redistributable datasets of the REVE pretraining corpus that reach its 200 Hz input (14,075 hours across 7,272 subjects) and release them as eegdash-corpus-v0, with raw-file access by name through EEGDash and a checksummed Zarr store on Hugging Face, converted with REVE's preprocessing recipe. Every dataset keeps its licence, and any subset of datasets can be selected by name. To test whether this public store pretrains REVE-Base, we train the model twice from scratch with REVE's trainer and score it on seven public OpenEEGBench tasks. First, under linear probing, the frozen encoder reaches 0.331 in chance-adjusted balanced accuracy, against 0.333 for the released model (untrained: 0.200), a difference smaller than the spread between our two pretraining seeds. Second, after fine-tuning, it reaches 0.273 against 0.325 published (0.301 in our rerun), and part of this gap comes from the scoring pipeline. A public, training-ready EEG corpus can therefore pretrain the frozen representations of a foundation model and provide a shared base that other groups can audit.
  </details>

- **[Recasting Visual Language Models as EEG Foundation Models for Clinical Neurodiagnostics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05538)**  `arXiv:2611.05538`  
  _Xin Zhou, Elena Sergeeva, Nan Bi, Wenyu Song, Lijun Yin, Hao Deng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Historically automated electroencephalography (EEG) systems was done using task-specific supervised models trained on purposely annotated data. A more recent modeling paradigm relies on creating a unified representation, or a 'foundation model', for multiple modality-specific tasks through large-scale domain-specific pretraining on the unannotated EEG signal. The quality of this unified representation directly depends on the availability of the modality-specific datasets. At the same time, generalist Vision Language Models (VLMs) exhibit broad few-shot learning capacities as well as good fine-tuning adaptability to different visual and language tasks. Can VLMs learn how to read and interpret EEG visually, emulating to a degree the way human clinicians inspect the EEG patterns? In this work we make a case that they can: we show that generalist VLMs without large-scale EEG pretraining perform competitively across a collection of clinical neuro-diagnostic tasks, can adapt to different tasks in a few-shot learning setting better than specialist foundation models, and exhibit better performance with model scaling when modality-specific models fail to exhibit.
  </details>

- **[Nominate-then-Adjudicate: LLM-Assisted One-Pass and Per-Instance MIP Solver Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29391)**  `arXiv:2610.29391`  
  _Yifan Zhao, Qianyang Zhao, Xinyu Guan, Kai Wei, Yuming Deng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parameter configurations control presolving, cut generation and branching strategies in mixed-integer programming (MIP) solvers, directly affecting solving efficiency. Effective configurations vary across instances, while searching for them online requires additional solver runs. We propose Nominate-then-Adjudicate: an empirical performance model (EPM) shortlists candidates from a fixed portfolio, and one large language model (LLM) call selects using static features, compressed formulation text and memory distilled from offline solver measurements. Text exposes distinctions omitted by the chosen aggregate features; selection requires no online candidate solves or foundation-model weight updates. Across sixteen synthetic families, adjudication improves on the EPM's top-ranked candidate (top-1), raising mean normalized performance score from 0.377 to 0.426 and the win rate against SCIP's default from 84.9% to 90.2%; all ten sequential batches show positive mean-score gains. On 400 Ecole instances, it outperforms the per-instance baseline ISAC+ in mean runtime-based score, with gains concentrated in facility location. Total solver time falls by 69.6% versus default, excluding LLM latency. Ablations support text and memory; a second LLM improves over top-1.
  </details>

- **[Data Density Scaling Laws for Image Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19013)**  `arXiv:2610.19013`  
  _Alvard Barseghyan, Ani Vanyan, Hakob Tamazyan, Hrant Khachatrian_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current scaling law formulations suggest that increasing the number of unique samples in a dataset consistently improves model performance, while repeated exposure to the same samples provides diminishing value. In this work, we challenge this assumption for image self-distillation models. We train iBOT models on random subsets of seven datasets spanning satellite imagery, driving scenes, egocentric video, and natural images, and evaluate their frozen representations on downstream tasks. We propose a density-interaction scaling law that combines unique-image count $U$ and repetition $R$, fitting it on runs with at most 50 million image presentations and testing it at larger budgets $C$. On Maxar dataset, a random subset containing approximately 1.5% of the training pool improves average nearest-neighbor classification accuracy by 3.11 percentage points over training on the full pool at the same budget of 200 million image presentations. Smaller subsets also outperform the full pool on SATLAS and Ego4D, whereas ImageNet favors larger pools at higher budgets. At a fixed subset size, further training can improve performance, lead to a plateau, or reduce performance. Our law captures smaller-subset advantages that the compared baselines cannot represent and improves prediction at higher budgets on Maxar, SATLAS, and Ego4D under nearest-neighbor evaluation. These findings show that both the number of unique images and how often they are repeated matter when allocating a training budget.
  </details>

- **[TransferBreaker: Breaking Adversarial Transferability in Fine-Tuned Speech Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09109)**  `arXiv:2610.09109`  
  _Mojtaba Nafez, Aref Mousavi, Mohammad Ebrahim Mahdavi, Mobina Poulaei, Kiarash Kiani Feriz, Mohammad Hossein Rohban_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many organizations fine-tune publicly available pretrained Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) models and deploy them in black-box settings, assuming limited access provides protection. We show this assumption is fragile: adversarial perturbations crafted on the public base model transfer effectively to fine-tuned target models, severely degrading performance and posing concerns for safety-critical applications. We propose TransferBreaker, a unified fine-tuning framework that suppresses adversarial transfer by integrating Base Adversarial Fine-Tuning, which restricts adversarial training to base-effective perturbations; Latent Jacobian Regularization, which enforces latent-space invariance by suppressing adversarially sensitive directions; and HybridGrad-AFT, which improves robustness against adaptive attacks by interpolating transferable perturbations from base and target gradients. We theoretically justify all components and evaluate TransferBreaker across three languages and four large ASR models, reducing adversarial WER from 92.6 to 27.8. Our code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LeapQuant: Efficient Linear Attention with Accurate Recurrent State Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38166)**  `arXiv:2609.38166`  
  _Yi Pan, Haocheng Xi, Kan Zhu, Xingyang Li, Yibo Wu, Mayank Mishra, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent LLMs increasingly adopt hybrid designs that replace standard attention with linear attention, such as Gated DeltaNet (GDN) and Kimi Delta Attention (KDA). Although they compress the context into a fixed-size recurrent state and substantially reduce the cost of long-context processing, repeatedly reading and updating that state remains a major inference bottleneck. Quantization offers a natural way to reduce this cost, but can significantly degrade model quality, due to the accumulation of rounding errors and the presence of outlier rows and columns in the state. To address these challenges, we propose LeapQuant, a training-free method that achieves near-lossless performance under 8-bit recurrent-state quantization. First, to mitigate error accumulation, we propose per-window quantization, which leaps over a window of tokens and quantizes the state only once at its end. Within a window, outputs are computed from the fixed low-bit state together with high-precision buffered updates. Second, to reduce the error introduced by each quantization, LeapQuant retains the state's largest outliers as a few high-precision Compensator Tokens, which share the update path of real tokens. We then smooth the remaining residual before quantization to further reduce the error. Comprehensive experiments across the Qwen, Kimi, and GLM model families show that LeapQuant substantially reduces memory and compute costs during inference. With accuracy comparable to the FP32 baseline, it achieves average speedups of 2.05--3.70$\times$ at the kernel level and 1.47$\times$ for end-to-end inference on NVIDIA B200, RTX PRO 6000, and RTX 5090 GPUs.
  </details>

- **[Learning Perturbation Robust Policies for LLM Agents with Stable Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34064)**  `arXiv:2609.34064`  
  _Pengxin Wang, Yuanzhe LI, Yuxin Ren, Huanrui Yang, Jingdi Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has become an effective post-training paradigm for long-horizon large language model (LLM) agents. However, we find that the resulting policies can be sensitive to various policy perturbations, such as hidden-state noise, pruning, and quantization. In this work, we study how to improve perturbation robustness during policy optimization. We first introduce the notion of a perturbation robust policy and analyze conditions under which perturbed policy updates preserve stable monotonic improvement. Based on this analysis, we introduce Stable Perturbation-Robust Policy Optimization (SPrPO), which applies adaptive and sensitivity-aware perturbations during RL training. We evaluate SPrPO on ALFWorld and WebShop and conduct systematic experiments across multiple perturbation types and scales, showing improved perturbation robustness while maintaining stable policy optimization.
  </details>

- **[Derivative-Informed Training of Neural Operators On-the-Fly via Sketched Tangent Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32797)**  `arXiv:2609.32797`  
  _Xinhan Yang, Lu Lu, Shancong Mou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Derivative-informed training improves neural operators by directly supervising their input-output sensitivities, which is crucial when neural operators are used as differentiable surrogates for inverse problems, PDE-constrained optimization, design, and control. However, existing methods rely on offline-generated derivative labels, making data generation slow, storage-intensive, and difficult to adapt across datasets, resolutions, or perturbation bases. We propose sketched tangent consistency loss (sTCL), an on-the-fly derivative-informed training objective that, for PDEs with a known and differentiable residual, enforces sensitivity consistency directly from the governing equation without offline tangent labels or neural-operator architecture changes. sTCL uses randomly sketched input perturbations to provide a lightweight derivative-level physics constraint during training. However, raw forward-sensitivity residual penalties can fail for stiff, ill-conditioned, indefinite, or coupled saddle-point tangent operators. To address this, we introduce lightweight operator-aware loss-conditioning mechanisms selected by a simple tangent-operator decision rule. Across Helmholtz, nonlinear diffusion-reaction, Burgers, Allen-Cahn, and Navier-Stokes, with the same neural-operator backbone for all methods, the PDE-specific sTCL losses achieve solution and Jacobian accuracy comparable to offline derivative-informed training (DIFNO) while eliminating the offline derivative-data generation and storage pipeline. These results show that on-the-fly derivative-informed training need not merely amortize offline tangent-solve cost into training; with appropriate sketching and loss design, sTCL provides an effective drop-in path to derivative-informed neural operators. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Binary Quantized Neural Network Training Is W[1]-Hard Parameterized by Input and Output Dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27932)**  `arXiv:2609.27932`  
  _Tao Jiang, Minbo Gao, Shaowei Cai_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When the weights and biases of a neural network range over a fixed finite alphabet, training the network to fit a data set exactly becomes a finite combinatorial problem, and parameterized complexity asks which structural quantities of an instance make this problem tractable. Ganian, Sommer, and Sorge (ICLR 2026) showed that training quantized networks is fixed-parameter tractable when parameterized jointly by the input dimension $\alpha$, the output dimension $\omega$, and the treewidth of the architecture, and they asked whether $\alpha+\omega$ alone suffices.
